4.2.6.3 Category 5 — Custom Application: Developed to meet individual user requirements where no commercially available solution is suitable. May use linear (waterfall) or Agile methods. Testing covers correct installation, functionality and design, fitness for intended use, and any further tests from risk assessments. Rigorous testing should be performed at both functional and design levels.

4.2.6 Practical Application of GAMP Categories

Categorization Is a Starting Point, Not an End Point

GAMP 5 warns very clearly: do not rely purely on a categorization table to decide validation activities (table-driven approaches should be avoided).

The category (Category 3/4/5) is only a starting point; the actual depth of validation must also consider:

  • How much does this system function affect GxP?
  • How reliable is the supplier's quality system?
  • What is the level of the system's complexity and novelty?

Even among Category 4 systems, the depth of validation can differ greatly between a LIMS that manages batch records and a system that manages employee training records.

4.2.6.4 Terminology — IQ/OQ/PQ vs. GAMP 5

The specific terminology used to describe life cycle activities and deliverables varies from company to company. This Guide does not use traditional pharmaceutical process validation terminology such as IQ/OQ/PQ to describe system life cycle activities. This aligns with the US FDA, who also chose not to use the terminology in General Principles of Software Validation because the terminology may not be well understood among many software professionals. Such terminology implies an inherently linear approach.

Whatever terminology is used for verification activity, the overriding requirement is that the regulated company can demonstrate that the system is compliant and fit for intended use.

4.2.6.4 Terminology Mapping Explained

Why Does GAMP 5 Move Away from IQ/OQ/PQ Terminology?

The traditional IQ/OQ/PQ terminology has several fundamental problems:

  • Implied linear assumption: the IQ → OQ → PQ sequence suggests that validation can only proceed in this fixed order, which is incompatible with Agile methods
  • Cross-discipline communication barrier: IT engineers and software suppliers do not understand these terms, which creates communication friction
  • Misleading focus: many pharmaceutical companies concentrate on "completing the IQ/OQ/PQ documents" rather than "confirming the system is fit for its intended use"

Practical Translation: How to Use This Idea Within an Existing GMP System

Many regulators and company SOPs still use the IQ/OQ/PQ terminology. GAMP 5 does not ask you to abolish these terms immediately; it asks you to understand that:

  • The essence of IQ = confirming that installation and configuration are correct (this does not necessarily require a document called an "IQ Protocol")
  • The essence of OQ = confirming that the system operates in accordance with specifications within the defined operating range
  • The essence of PQ = confirming that the system is fit for its intended use in the actual business environment

If your company's SOP requires IQ/OQ/PQ, you can keep using this framework, but when planning, use GAMP 5 critical thinking to decide how deep each part needs to go.

The Role of FAT / SAT

GAMP 5 notes that Factory Acceptance Testing (FAT) and Site Acceptance Testing (SAT) are acceptance activities carried out by the regulated company before formal release. The key principle: verification activities should not be unnecessarily repeated.

If the supplier has already performed adequate FAT at the factory, the regulated company's SAT on site can focus on installation verification and testing of site-specific conditions, rather than re-running every FAT test case on site.

Phase 3: OPERATION

4.3 Operation

This section provides guidance on system operation. Not all the activities will be directly relevant to all systems. The approach and required activities should be selected and scaled according to the nature, risk, and complexity of the system in question through the application of critical thinking.

Once the system has been accepted and released for use, there is a need to maintain compliance and fitness for intended use throughout its operational life. This is achieved by the use of established and up-to-date procedures and training that cover use, maintenance, and management.

The operational phase of a system may last many years, and may include changes to services, software, hardware, the business process, and regulatory requirements. The integrity of the system and its data should be maintained at all times and verified as part of periodic review.

Change management should provide a dependable mechanism for prompt implementation of technically sound improvements following the approach to specification, design, and verification described in this Guide. The rigor of the approach, including the extent of documentation and verification, should be based on the risk and complexity of the change through the application of critical thinking.

Key operational process groups (Table 4.2):

  • Handover
  • Service Management and System Monitoring
  • Incident and Problem Management, Deviation, and CAPA
  • Configuration Management
  • Audits and Review (Periodic Review)
  • Continuity Management (Backup, BCP, Disaster Recovery)
  • Security and System Administration
  • Records Management
Phase 3: Operation

4.3 The Operation Phase Explained

The Operation Phase: The Longest and Most Easily Overlooked Phase

The operation phase is usually the longest part of the system life cycle: a SCADA or LIMS system may remain in operation for 10-15 years. Yet at many pharmaceutical companies, attention to compliance drops sharply once the validation work is finished.

The GAMP 5 position is that maintaining compliance in the operation phase is as important as the initial validation. The core mechanisms for maintaining compliance are effective operating procedures, training, change management, and periodic review.

CDMO Scenario: Managing the Operation Phase of a SCADA System

Your CDMO has had a SCADA system controlling an aseptic filling line for three years. Typical events in the operation phase include:

  • The supplier pushes a software update (must go through change control, with impact assessment)
  • A filling head is added (the related functions must be reassessed and validated)
  • A computer crashes and must be reinstalled (have the backup and restore procedures been validated?)
  • Annual periodic review (confirm that the system still meets current regulatory requirements)

The Eight Process Groups of the Operation Phase

GAMP 5 Table 4.2 lists the main process groups of the operation phase, each with a corresponding appendix (O1-O13). This does not mean every system must establish all of these processes; rather, you select the applicable ones according to the system's risk and complexity.

4.3.1 Handover

Handover is the process for transfer of responsibility of a computerized system from a project team or a service group to the operations team or a new service group. The support group should be involved at the earliest opportunity to ensure effective knowledge transfer and establishment of operational procedures. A period of elevated support and maintenance, often referred to as Hypercare Services, may be arranged to facilitate the transfer.

4.3.3 Incident and Problem Management and CAPA

The incident management process aims to categorize incidents to direct them to the most appropriate resource or complementary process to achieve a timely resolution; whereas problem management involves analyzing root causes and preventing incidents from happening in the future.

There should be a procedure defining how problems related to software, hardware, and procedures should be captured, reviewed, prioritized, progressed, escalated, and closed.

CAPA is a process for investigating, understanding, and correcting discrepancies based on root-cause analysis, while attempting to prevent their recurrence. In the operational environment the CAPA process should feed into the overall CAPA system used for GxP operations.

4.3.4 Change Management

Change management is a critical activity that is fundamental to maintaining the proper functioning and controlled status of systems and processes. All changes that are proposed during the operational phase of a computerized system, whether related to software (including middleware), hardware, infrastructure, or use of the system, should be subject to a formal change-control process.

This process should ensure that proposed changes are appropriately reviewed to assess impact and risk. Regression analysis and regression testing may be required. The process should ensure that changes are suitably evaluated, authorized, documented, tested, and approved before implementation, and subsequently closed.

Configuration management includes those activities necessary to precisely define a computerized system at any point during its life cycle. Configuration items should only be modified by application of a change management process.

4.3 Key Operational Processes Explained

Incident vs. Problem: Two Different Management Processes

These concepts come from ITIL (the IT service management framework):

  • Incident Management: the goal is to "restore service as quickly as possible". For example, if the LIMS suddenly goes down and IT restarts the service and the system recovers, that is incident management, and speed is the priority.
  • Problem Management: the goal is to "find the root cause and prevent recurrence". If the same LIMS crashes once every month, that is a Problem, and it requires an in-depth root cause investigation (database index efficiency? memory leak?) and a fix.

In a GMP environment, an Incident needs to be recorded (deviation record) and a Problem needs a CAPA. The two must be linked.

Change Control: Link to GMP Facility Change Control

GAMP 5 requires that all changes to the hardware, software, infrastructure, and manner of use of a computerized system go through a formal change-control process.

This is the same logical framework as the change control SOP for GMP facilities. In a CDMO, change control for computerized systems should be integrated into the site-wide change control system rather than operating in isolation.

Key requirement: before a change is implemented, it must be assessed, authorized, documented, tested, and reviewed. This means that emergency changes also need a mechanism for retrospective documentation, and "urgency" cannot be used as a reason to skip documentation requirements.

The Concept of the Configuration Item

A Configuration Item (CI) is a component of the system that should not change under normal operation. Examples include:

  • The LIMS version number and installation package
  • The database schema design
  • System parameter files
  • API definitions of integration interfaces

Any change to a CI must go through the change management process: you cannot modify settings manually in the production environment and then add the records afterwards.

4.3.5 Periodic Review

Periodic reviews are used throughout the operational life of systems to verify that they remain compliant with regulatory requirements, fit for intended use, and meet company policies and procedures, including those related to data integrity. Periodic reviews should be:

  • Scheduled at an interval appropriate to the impact and operational history of the system. Risk and other assessments should be used to determine which systems are in scope and the frequency of periodic review.
  • Performed in accordance with a predefined process
  • Documented with corrective actions tracked to satisfactory completion

4.3.6 Continuity Management

4.3.6.1 Backup and Restore: Processes and procedures should be established to ensure that backup copies of software, records, and data are made, maintained, and retained for a defined period within safe and secure areas. Restore procedures should be established, tested, and the results of that testing documented.

4.3.6.2 Business Continuity Planning (BCP): A series of related activities ensuring that an organization is fully prepared to respond effectively in the event of failures and disruptions. Critical business processes and systems should be identified, and the risks to each assessed. Plans should be established and exercised to ensure timely and effective resumption. Plans should be prioritized based on patient and business risk, in case of disruption to multiple systems.

4.3.6.3 Disaster Recovery Planning: A subset of BCP, plans should be specified, approved, and rehearsed for the recovery of specific systems in the event of a disaster. Disaster Recovery (DR) plans often require a shared responsibility model between internal organizations and external service providers (e.g., cloud service providers).

4.3.7 Security Management

Computerized systems and data should be adequately protected against willful or accidental loss, damage, or unauthorized change. Role-based security should be implemented. Security provisions should ensure that data is protected against unauthorized intrusions including cyber security attacks. Intrusion prevention and detection processes should be in place.

4.3.5-4.3.8 Periodic Review, Continuity of Operation, and Security Management

Frequency of Periodic Review: There Is No Fixed Answer

GAMP 5 does not specify "once a year" or "once every two years"; instead it requires the frequency to be determined based on the following factors:

  • The degree of impact of the system on patient safety and product quality
  • The operating history of the system (are there frequent problems?)
  • Whether the regulatory environment has changed

A high-risk system (such as a SCADA directly controlling an aseptic process) may need annual review, while a low-risk HR system may be adequately reviewed once every three years.

Backup and Restore: The Most Easily Overlooked GMP Requirement

Almost every IT system has a backup schedule, but in a GMP environment, the backup restore procedure must be tested, and the test results must be recorded.

A common problem: "We back up every day, but we have never tried restoring from a backup." This is non-compliant in a GMP environment, because a procedure that has never been tested cannot be relied on to work in an emergency.

The Prioritization Logic of the BCP

When multiple systems fail at the same time, the BCP needs a clear order of priority. GAMP 5 requires prioritization based on patient risk and business risk.

A typical CDMO order of priority:

  1. Batch record system (directly affects product release decisions)
  2. SCADA/process control system (affects production continuity)
  3. LIMS (affects release testing)
  4. ERP/procurement system (affects the business, but can run on manual workarounds for a short time)
Phase 4: RETIREMENT

4.4 Retirement

This activity covers system withdrawal, system decommissioning, system disposal, and migration of required data.

4.4.1 Withdrawal

Withdrawal is the removal of the system from active operation, i.e., users are deactivated, interfaces disabled. No data should be added to the system from this point forward. Special access should be retained for data reporting, results analysis, and support.

4.4.2 Decommissioning

Decommissioning is a controlled process by which an application or system is removed from use in an organization once it has been retired and/or has become obsolete.

4.4.3 Disposal

Data, documentation, software, or hardware may be permanently destroyed. Each may reach this stage at a different time. Data and documentation may not be disposed of until they have reached the end of the record-retention period, as specified in the company's record-retention policy, following an authorized and documented process.

Consideration should be given to:

  • Establishing procedures covering system retirement, including withdrawal, decommissioning, and disposal as appropriate
  • Documentary evidence to be retained of actions taken during retirement of the system
  • GxP records to be maintained, their required retention periods, and which records can be destroyed
  • The need to migrate records to a new system or archive, and the method of verifying and documenting this process
  • Ability to retrieve these migrated records on the new system

4.4.4 Data Migration

Data migration may be required when an existing system is replaced by a new system, when an operational system experiences a significant change, or when the scope of use of a system is changed. The migration process should be accurate, complete, and verified.

Phase 4: Retirement

4.4 The Retirement Phase Explained

The Three Steps of Retirement

GAMP 5 breaks retirement down into three conceptually distinct steps:

  • Withdrawal: ceasing use. User accounts are disabled, interfaces are cut off, and no new data is written. However, the system may remain online so that historical data can be consulted.
  • Decommissioning: controlled removal of the system from the infrastructure, including removal at both the hardware and software levels.
  • Disposal: permanent deletion of data or destruction of hardware. Note: GxP records may only be destroyed after the record retention period has expired.

CDMO Scenario: Retiring an Old MES

Suppose your CDMO is replacing a 10-year-old MES with a new MES:

  • Withdrawal: after switching to the new MES, the old MES is blocked from creating new batches but keeps the ability to look up old batch records
  • Data migration: decide which historical batch records are migrated to the new system and which are kept in an archive system; the accuracy and completeness of the migration must be verified
  • Decommissioning: confirm the record retention requirements (batch records are typically retained for the product shelf life + 1 year, or a minimum of 5 years), and only then take the servers offline
  • Disposal: in line with data security requirements, ensure that sensitive data is securely destroyed and keep a record of it

Three Verification Requirements for Data Migration

GAMP 5 requires data migration to be:

  • Accurate: the migrated data is consistent with the original data
  • Complete: all records that should be migrated have been migrated, with nothing missing
  • Verified: the two points above require documented confirmation activities

Data migration is a major risk point for data integrity. If data is corrupted or lost during migration and this goes unnoticed, it may affect future batch reviews, trend analyses, or regulatory inquiries.

Chapter Summary — Key Takeaways

  • Life cycle = 4 phases: Concept → Project → Operation → Retirement. Each phase has defined activities; the operation phase is typically the longest.
  • Specification & verification is not linear: GAMP 5 explicitly supports Agile, iterative, and incremental approaches alongside the traditional V-model.
  • CSV framework is built on system-specific validation plans and reports, with scalable rigor based on risk, complexity, and novelty — not on document count.
  • Critical thinking is a continuous mindset across all phases: consider multiple layers of assurance, avoid unnecessary paperwork, and document the rationale for your decisions.
  • IQ/OQ/PQ → GAMP 5 activities: DQ ≈ Design Review; IQ ≈ Installation/configuration verification; OQ ≈ Functional/operational testing; PQ ≈ Fitness-for-use acceptance testing.
  • Operation phase requires formal change control for all changes, periodic reviews, incident/problem management with CAPA integration, backup/restore testing, and security management.
  • Retirement is a controlled process: withdrawal → decommissioning → disposal, with data migration verified for accuracy and completeness.

Chapter Summary: Key Points

  • Life cycle = 4 phases: Concept → Project → Operation → Retirement. The operation phase usually lasts the longest, but its compliance requirements are the most easily overlooked.
  • Diverse verification approaches: the V-model is only one of many acceptable approaches; iterative Agile methods are fully compliant under the GAMP 5 framework.
  • Critical thinking runs through the entire life cycle: the level of compliance has nothing to do with the volume of documentation and everything to do with how targeted and effective the controls are. Layers-of-assurance thinking is the core tool for assessing risk.
  • Categorization (Cat. 3/4/5) is a starting point, not an end point: the actual depth of validation must also consider the degree of GxP impact, supplier capability, and system novelty.
  • Four pillars of the operation phase: change management (all changes go through change control), periodic review (frequency determined by risk), CAPA integration, and business continuity planning (BCP + DR + backup restore testing).
  • Retirement is not simply switching off the machine: it requires planning for record retention periods, verification of data migration, and secure destruction, with documented evidence of the retirement activities.
2

Section 2: Ch5-8: QRM, Regulated Company, Supplier & Efficiency

p49-p84

ISPE GAMP 5 2nd Edition | Section 02a | Doc p49–p68

Learning Objectives

  1. Explain the five-step QRM process defined in GAMP 5 and how it links to ICH Q9 principles — and how this changes from checkbox compliance to science-based validation strategy.
  2. Distinguish between process-level risk assessment and system-level functional risk assessment, and know when each applies.
  3. Describe the governance elements required for a robust computerized system compliance program, including policy, roles, inventory, and planning.
  4. Identify the key roles (Process Owner, System Owner, Quality Unit, SME, Supplier, End User) and their specific responsibilities in the CSV life cycle.
  5. Map the 12 system-specific activities in Table 6.1 to a practical CDMO validation program, including how supplier data reduces in-house testing burden.
  6. Apply the concept of system inventory and categorization to determine appropriate validation depth for any given GxP system.

Chapter 5: Quality Risk Management (Doc p49–p55)

5.1 Overview

QRM is a systematic process for the assessment, control, communication, and review of risks. It is an iterative process used throughout the computerized system life cycle from concept to retirement.

For a given organization, a framework for making risk-management decisions should be defined to ensure consistency of application across systems and business functions. Terminology should be agreed upon, particularly regarding definitions and metrics for key risk factors.

Such a framework is most effectively implemented when it is incorporated into the overall QMS and is fully integrated with the system life cycle.

Scope Note

This chapter is primarily aimed at new computerized systems. It does not imply that formal risk assessments are required for all existing systems. The extent of risk management required for existing systems should be considered as part of periodic review.

This chapter focuses on software products and custom applications rather than on infrastructure.

5.1 Overview: Interpretation and Application

What is QRM, and why does it matter?

Quality risk management (QRM) is not a form to be filled in. It is a decision-making framework that runs through the entire life cycle of a computerized system. The core question it answers is: "How much validation does this system need?"

For a CDMO, this question comes up every day: a new LIMS module, a new environmental monitoring system, an updated ERP configuration. The QRM framework makes every decision traceable, rather than based on gut feeling or "that's how we've always done it."

Analogy: QRM is the "site survey before construction"

Think of the geological survey done before building a plant. It is not meant to create problems; it tells you where the foundation needs reinforcing and where you can save material. QRM does exactly the same for computerized systems: it concentrates validation resources where the real risk is.

Important scope limitation

This chapter applies only to new systems; the extent of risk management for existing systems should be decided during periodic review. This means you do not need to go back and redo a formal RA for every legacy system, but at each periodic review you must assess whether one needs to be added.

5.2 Science-Based Quality Risk Management

Determining the risks posed by a computerized system requires a common and shared understanding of:

  • Impact of the computerized system on patient safety, product quality, and data integrity
  • Supported business processes
  • CQAs for systems that monitor or control CPPs
  • User requirements
  • Regulatory requirements
  • Project approach (contracts, methods, timelines)
  • System components and architecture
  • System functions
  • Supplier capability

The organization also should consider other applicable risks, such as Health, Safety, and Environment (HSE).

Managing the risks may be achieved by:

  • Elimination by design — modifying processes or system design
  • Reduction to an acceptable level — applying controls to lower severity, decrease probability, or increase detectability
  • Verification — demonstrating that risks are managed to an acceptable level

It is desirable to eliminate risk, if possible, by modifying processes or system design. Design reviews can play a key role in eliminating risk by design.

A systematic approach should be defined to verify that the risk associated with a system has been managed to an acceptable level. The overall extent of verification and the level of detail of documentation should be based on the risk to patient safety, product quality, and data integrity, and take into account the complexity and novelty of the system.

The criticality of a business process is independent of whether it is manually processed, semi-automated, or fully automated. Systems that support critical processes include those that:

  • Generate, manipulate, or control data supporting regulatory safety and efficacy submissions
  • Control critical parameters and data in pre-clinical, clinical, development, and manufacturing
  • Control or provide data or information for product release
  • Control data or information required in case of product recall
  • Control adverse event or complaint recording or reporting
  • Support pharmacovigilance

5.2 Science-Based QRM: Interpretation and Application

What "science-based" really means

GAMP 5 stresses science-based to counter a common bad habit: treating QRM as a "paperwork game" in which the form is completed and stamped, but nobody understands the system's actual risk.

Science-based QRM requires you to truly understand: Which CPP does this system control? Which CQA does it affect? If the data were corrupted, what would the consequence be for the patient?

Priority order of the three risk control strategies

GAMP 5 sets out a clear order of priority: eliminate by design > reduce by control > confirm by verification. This is very practical for a CDMO:

  • Eliminate by design: for example, setting field limits in the system so users simply cannot enter unreasonable values. This is more reliable than review after the fact.
  • Reduce by control: SOPs, electronic signatures, alarms; these reduce the probability of occurrence or improve detectability.
  • Confirm by verification: IQ/OQ/PQ come last, to demonstrate that the first two layers of control actually work.

"Business process criticality ≠ degree of automation"

GAMP 5 stresses that whether a process is manual, semi-automated or fully automated, it is a critical process if it affects patient safety. This means that even an old system that "just displays a number" is a GxP system that must be brought under control if that number influences a release decision.

CDMO practice: which systems are most often overlooked?

  • Environmental monitoring (EM) systems, whose data go directly into batch records
  • Weighing systems / balance interfaces, which directly affect formulation accuracy
  • Cold chain temperature recording systems, which support release decisions
  • Excel macros, often regarded as "not a system" but functionally exactly that

5.3 Quality Risk Management Process

The ICH Q9 describes a systematic approach to QRM intended for general application within the pharmaceutical industry. It defines two primary principles:

"The evaluation of the risk to quality should be based on scientific knowledge and ultimately link to the protection of the patient; and the level of effort, formality and documentation of the quality risk-management process should be commensurate with the level of risk."

In the context of computerized systems, scientific knowledge is based upon the system specifications and the business process being supported.

Key ICH Q9 Terms Used in This Guide

  • Harm: Damage to health, including the damage that can occur from loss of product quality or availability.
  • Hazard: The potential source of harm.
  • Risk: The combination of the probability of occurrence of harm and the severity of that harm.
  • Severity: A measure of the possible consequences of a hazard.

Process Risk Assessment

QRM activities should start at the business process level, at a level higher than individual systems. A process risk assessment (also known as business process risk assessment) is a non-system-specific high-level assessment of the business process or data flow, which may occur before system-specific QRM activities.

The process risk assessment is aimed at identifying key high-level risks to patient safety, product quality, and data integrity, and identifying the required controls to manage those risks. Typical inputs include:

  • Defined business process scope
  • Process descriptions and/or diagrams
  • Identified regulatory requirements for the proposed process scope
  • Identified company quality requirements

Five-Step QRM Process

This Guide applies the general principles of ICH Q9 to describe a five-step process for risk management as an integral part of achieving and maintaining system compliance.

This process applies during the project phase. Examples of application include: determining the need for supplier audit, determining the rigor and extent of testing, determining corrective actions from test failures, determining impact of proposed changes, and determining the frequency of periodic reviews.

Step 1 – Perform Initial Risk Assessment and Determine System Impact

An initial risk assessment should be performed based on business processes and business risk assessments, user requirements, regulatory requirements, and known functional areas. The results should include a decision on whether the system is GxP regulated (i.e., GxP assessment) and an overall assessment of system impact.

A system cannot have a higher impact than the business process it supports.

Step 2 – Identify Functions with Impact on Patient Safety, Product Quality, and Data Integrity

Functions that have an impact on patient safety, product quality, and data integrity should be identified by building on information gathered during Step 1, referring to relevant specifications, and considering project approach, system architecture, and categorization of system components.

Individual functions cannot have a higher impact than the system as a whole.

Step 3 – Perform Functional Risk Assessments and Identify Controls

Functions identified during Step 2 should be assessed by considering possible hazards, and how the potential harm arising from these hazards may be controlled. A more detailed assessment may analyze severity of harm, likelihood of occurrence, and probability of detection.

Criteria to consider include: criticality of the supported process, specific impact of the function within the process, and nature of the system (e.g., complexity and novelty).

Appropriate controls include, but are not limited to:

  • Modification of process design
  • Modification of system design
  • Application of external procedures
  • Increasing the detail or formality of specifications
  • Increasing the number and level of detail of design reviews
  • Increasing the extent or rigor of verification activities

Step 4 – Implement and Verify Appropriate Controls

The control measures identified in Step 3 should be implemented and verified to ensure that they have been successfully implemented. Controls should be traceable to the relevant identified risks. The effort, formality, and documentation of the verification activity should be commensurate with the level of risk.

Step 5 – Review Risks and Monitor Controls

During periodic review of systems, or at other defined points, an organization should review the risks and verify that controls are still effective. The organization should consider whether:

  • Previously unrecognized hazards are present
  • Previously identified hazards are no longer applicable
  • The estimated risk associated with a hazard is no longer acceptable
  • The original assessment is otherwise invalidated (e.g., following changes to applicable regulations or change of system use)

The frequency and extent of any periodic review should be based on the level of risk and should consider previous findings and operational history.

5.3 The Five-Step QRM Process: Interpretation and Application

Interpreting the two core principles of ICH Q9

First: risk assessment should be based on scientific knowledge and ultimately linked to the protection of the patient. Note the word "ultimately": the goal is not to protect the company or to pass an inspection, but to protect the patient.

Second: the level of effort, formality and documentation should be commensurate with the level of risk. This is the rationale behind GAMP 5's rejection of validating everything the same way: do less for low-risk systems and more for high-risk systems.

Process risk assessment vs. system functional risk assessment

Process RA should be performed before a system is selected. It assesses "the risk of this business process" without assuming any particular system. For example, the environmental monitoring process for aseptic filling is high risk in itself, whatever system is used.

System functional RA then assesses specifically "which functions of this system have an impact." Only with the process RA in place can the scope of the system RA be set correctly.

The key constraint in Step 1: the system cannot exceed the process

「A system cannot have a higher impact than the business process it supports.」

This rule is significant: if a business process is not GxP-critical in itself, the system supporting it does not need GxP validation. Conversely, if the process is critical, the system must be validated. This is the first line of defense in deciding whether validation is necessary.

The importance of Step 5 is often underestimated

Periodic review of risk is not just "confirming that the old RA is still valid." GAMP 5 requires you to actively assess:

  • Have new hazards appeared that were not identified before? (common after system upgrades)
  • Have regulatory changes made the earlier assessment invalid? (for example, the revision of Annex 11)
  • Are some risks no longer applicable, so that controls can be relaxed?

The last point is the one most often missed. GAMP 5 explicitly allows the stringency of existing controls to be reduced after review, which reflects the principle of proportionality to risk.

Chapter 6: Regulated Company Activities (Doc p55–p68)

6.1 Governance for Achieving Compliance

Responsibility for the compliance of computerized systems lies with the regulated company. This involves activities at both the organizational level and at the level of individual systems.

Achieving robust, cost-effective compliance requires strong governance. Key elements of successful governance include:

  • Establishing computerized systems compliance policies and procedures
  • Identifying clear roles and responsibilities
  • Training
  • Managing supplier relationships
  • Maintaining a system inventory
  • Planning for validation
  • Continual improvement activities
  • Data governance

Effective governance is achieved by integrating these activities into the management of the organization.

6.1.1 Computerized Systems Policies and Procedures

Regulated companies should have a defined policy for ensuring that computerized systems are compliant and fit for intended use. The policy should typically include a commitment to:

  • Identify and comply with all applicable GxP requirements
  • Integrate life cycle activities into the regulated company's QMS
  • Identify and assess each system
  • Ensure GxP regulated systems are compliant and fit for intended use according to established SOPs
  • Follow a validation framework, including the use of validation plans and validation reports as necessary
  • Maintain compliance throughout the life of a system

Further details should be documented in SOPs, which may be supplemented by guidance and templates, typically addressing: system inventory, impact assessment, roles/responsibilities, life cycle approach, planning/risk management/specification/verification activities, system operation and management, record and data management, and security management.

6.1.2 Identifying Clear Roles and Responsibilities

Roles and responsibilities for activities should be documented, allocated, and communicated. Key responsibilities include:

  • Defining, approving, and maintaining policies and SOPs
  • Compiling and prioritizing the system inventory
  • Producing plans and reports
  • Managing compliance and validation activities
  • Maintaining compliance during operation

6.1.3 Training

Training ensures that persons who develop, validate, maintain, support, or use computerized systems have the education, training, and experience to perform their assigned tasks. A risk-based approach should be used to determine the rigor of training required.

For computerized systems, the regulated company should:

  • Establish the necessary training needs, including users, suppliers, data centers, IT departments, engineering, maintenance
  • Provide training to satisfy these needs
  • Evaluate the effectiveness of the training
  • Ensure that staff are aware of the relevance and importance of their activities (e.g., GxP)
  • Ensure that supplier staff are adequately trained, e.g., as part of supplier assessment
  • Maintain appropriate training records
  • Ensure training is maintained up-to-date, e.g., following system changes

6.1.4 Managing Supplier Relationships

Responsibility for activities may be with the suppliers, but in all cases regulatory accountability lies with the regulated company. The regulated company must have defined roles and responsibilities for acceptance and release of GxP computerized systems.

Even though regulated companies cannot delegate their regulatory accountabilities to a supplier, they may leverage the knowledge, experience, activities, and artifacts of an IT/IS service provider through risk-based assessment, management, and governance processes.

The regulated company should verify, prior to contract placement, that the supplier has adequate expertise and resources to support user requirements and expectations. The most common mechanism is the supplier assessment, which may include an audit depending on risk, complexity, and novelty.

6.1.5 Maintaining the System Inventory

Regulated companies should maintain an inventory of computerized systems, showing those that are GxP regulated. The inventory should provide summary information such as the validation status, ownership, impact, current system version, and supplier. Automated equipment may be listed separately and duplication should be avoided.

The inventory should be at the level of systems that support business processes, rather than individual items of hardware. The system inventory may be used for planning periodic reviews.

6.1.6 Planning for Validation

Computerized system validation within a business unit is typically performed using a hierarchical framework of plans covering GxP regulated computerized systems. Computerized system validation plans describe how to ensure compliance and fitness for intended use of specific systems, and specify scope, approach, resources, roles and responsibilities, and the types and extent of activities, tasks, and deliverables.

6.1.7 Continual Improvement Activities

Understanding the effectiveness of current processes is best gained by considering current levels of conformance (e.g., established by audit and trending performance), by reviewing current processes against recognized good practices, and applying critical thinking.

Metrics may be gathered throughout the system life cycle, including:

  • Design and development metrics (e.g., from design and code reviews)
  • Testing metrics (e.g., from analysis of test failures and resulting actions)
  • Operation and maintenance metrics (e.g., from incident management, change management, backup and restore)

6.1.8 Data Governance

"The arrangements to ensure that data, irrespective of the format in which they are generated, are recorded, processed, retained and used to ensure the record throughout the data lifecycle."

Data governance encompasses the people, processes, and technology required to achieve consistent, accurate, and effective data handling. It provides the structure within which appropriate decisions regarding data-related matters may be made according to agreed models, principles, processes, and defined authority.

6.1 Governance Framework: Interpretation and Application

Governance is the "backbone" of the CSV program

GAMP 5 Chapter 6 has two layers: organization-level governance (6.1) and system-level activities (6.2). Many companies skip 6.1 and go straight to 6.2. The result is that every project reinvents the wheel, consistency is lacking, and inspectors observe a "lack of a systematic approach."

Good governance means that even if people change, the next person can pick up the SOP and perform to the same standard.

A key CDMO question: who owns the CSV program?

In many CDMOs, ownership of the CSV program is a gray area:

  • Quality Assurance (QA): knows the regulatory requirements but not the technical details
  • IT: understands the systems but not GxP thinking
  • Engineering: understands the equipment but not data integrity

GAMP 5's answer: regulatory responsibility always rests with the regulated company (led by QA), but execution must be cross-functional. In practice, the most effective model is for QA to set the policy and minimum standards, for IT/Engineering to execute according to the policy, and for them to report to QA regularly.

6.1.3 Risk-proportionate training

Training itself should also be risk managed: for high-risk systems, user training should be more rigorous, with more complete assessment and more detailed records. For low-risk systems (such as office productivity tools), it can be simplified.

A common mistake: requiring the same training format and records for every system, which wastes resources and is hard to enforce.

6.1.4 Supplier relationships: "responsibility cannot be outsourced"

This is an iron rule of GxP: no matter how much you outsource, whether system implementation, maintenance or even the data center, regulatory responsibility always stays with you. The wording GAMP 5 uses is "regulatory accountability cannot be delegated."

But you can make use of supplier capabilities to reduce your validation effort. This is permitted and recommended (once the supplier's QMS has been confirmed through supplier assessment).

6.1.5 System inventory: the "asset map" of CSV

A system inventory sounds simple, but it is where a CSV program most easily gets out of control. Common problems include:

  • Incomplete inventory: systems are running but not listed
  • Inventory not kept up to date: system version or owner has changed but the inventory has not
  • Inventory too granular: every PC is listed, which makes it hard to manage

GAMP 5 states clearly that the inventory should be at the level of "systems supporting business processes," not hardware components. A LIMS is one record, not one record per workstation.

6.1.8 Data governance: a concept that goes beyond computerized systems

Data governance covers people, processes and technology. This means data governance exists even where there is no computerized system: how a paper batch record is generated, reviewed and retained all fall within its scope.

GAMP 5 lists data governance as part of organizational governance and stresses that it is not the responsibility of the IT department but of the whole organization.

6.2 System-Specific Activities

Table 6.1 shows the typical regulated company activities required for a configurable computerized system. Note: This table is indicative only. Activities required for a specific system should be determined based on risk, complexity, and novelty, by applying critical thinking.

Table 6.1: Typical Activities for a Configurable Computerized System

6.2 System-Specific Activities: Interpretation and Application

Table 6.1: the GAMP 5 "validation road map"

This 12-step table is one of the most practical tools in GAMP 5 Second Edition. It condenses a complete CSV program into one checklist, useful for planning a validation project, spotting missing activities, or explaining the CSV workload to management.

Important reminder: GAMP 5 says this is "indicative." Not every system needs all 12 steps. The depth and formality of each step should be determined by risk, complexity and novelty.

6.2.3 Key Roles and Responsibilities

6.2.3.1 Process Owner

The process owner is ultimately responsible for ensuring that the computerized system and its operation are in compliance and fit for intended use in accordance with applicable SOPs. Process owners are typically the head of the functional unit using the system. Specific activities include:

  • Approving key documentation as defined by plans and SOPs
  • Providing adequate resources (personnel and financial) to support development and operation
  • Ensuring adequate training for end users
  • Ensuring that SOPs required for the operation of the system exist, are followed, and are reviewed periodically
  • Ensuring changes are approved and managed
  • Reviewing assessment/audit reports and taking appropriate actions to ensure GxP compliance
  • Coordinating input from other groups (e.g., finance, information security, HSE, legal)

6.2.3.2 System Owner

The system owner is responsible for the availability, support, maintenance, and security of a system. For systems supporting regulated processes, the ownership of the data resides with the GxP process owner, not the system owner. The system owner acts on behalf of the users. Specific activities include:

  • Approving key documentation as defined by plans and SOPs
  • Ensuring SOPs for maintenance exist, are followed, and reviewed periodically
  • Ensuring adequate training for maintenance and support staff
  • Ensuring changes are managed
  • Ensuring availability of information for the system inventory and configuration management
  • Managing the system life cycle, including upgrade and replacement planning
  • Ensuring that data integrity risks are identified and controlled

6.2.3.3–6.2.3.5 Quality Unit (Corporate & Operational)

The quality unit provides an independent role in:

  • Approving or auditing key documentation (policies, procedures, acceptance criteria, plans, reports)
  • Focusing on quality critical aspects
  • Approving changes that potentially affect patient safety, product quality, or data integrity
  • Auditing processes to verify that compliance activities are effective

Corporate Quality operates at the corporate level: setting policy, maintaining oversight of company standards, auditing for compliance, reviewing effectiveness of quality systems. Regulatory authorities require independence from business activities.

Operational Quality (division/business unit level): implementing quality standards, reviewing risk assessment and control activities, supporting project phase activities, supporting life cycle processes (change control, document management), managing quality of external service providers. Specific operational quality activities can be delegated to IT departments or engineering departments with their own quality management functions.

6.2.3.6 Subject Matter Expert (SME)

SMEs have a key role in performing reviews and assessments, and taking technical decisions, based on science-based process and product understanding, and sound engineering principles. SMEs should take the lead role in the verification of the computerized system. Responsibilities include planning and defining verification strategies, defining acceptance criteria, selection of appropriate test methods, execution of verification tests, and reviewing results.

6.2.3.7 Supplier

Suppliers (including internal suppliers such as IT or engineering) play an important support role. Specific activities include: provisioning existing documentation, preparing and reviewing documentation, acting as SME for technical aspects, performing and supporting testing, managing changes and configuration, and supporting processes for maintaining system compliance.

6.2.3.8 End User

In addition to using the system in accordance with approved procedures, end users may be involved in: providing input to user requirements, evaluating prototypes, testing, acceptance and handover, developing SOPs for system use, reporting defects, and identifying opportunities for improvement.

6.2.3 Key roles: Interpretation and Application

Process Owner vs. System Owner: the most commonly confused distinction

The Process Owner is responsible for the business process and also for the data. This is usually a functional department head (for example, the head of Production or the QC manager). The Process Owner has ultimate responsibility for "the compliance of the system."

The System Owner is responsible for system availability, and is usually in IT or Engineering. The System Owner is responsible for "whether the system runs," but does not own GxP data.

Key point: data ownership belongs to the Process Owner, not the System Owner. This confusion is often found during CDMO client audits.

Analogy: the landlord-tenant relationship

The System Owner is like the landlord, responsible for the maintenance and safety of the building. The Process Owner is like the tenant, responsible for ensuring that activities in the building comply with regulations (GxP). The data (the property inside the rented home) belongs to the tenant, not the landlord.

Corporate Quality vs. Operational Quality

Large pharmaceutical companies usually have both roles, while a smaller CDMO often combines them. But the principles GAMP 5 stresses still apply:

  • Corporate QA: sets policy, performs audits and stays independent; it must not take part directly in validation execution
  • Operational QA: supports execution, reviews documents and agrees how deviations are handled

Even if your QA team has only three people, you should clearly define who plays which role. Otherwise you will not be able to explain it during an audit.

The role of the SME is seriously underestimated

GAMP 5 states clearly that SMEs should "lead the validation work," not just sign passively. This means:

  • The test plan should be drafted by the SME, not fully outsourced to the supplier
  • Acceptance criteria must be defined by the SME
  • Technical judgments during test execution are the SME's responsibility

In a CDMO, SMEs are usually production engineers or QC scientists, and how deeply they are involved directly affects validation quality.

The role of the End User: more than just a user

GAMP 5 specifically names the ways End Users can contribute: writing requirements, evaluating prototypes, executing tests, developing SOPs, reporting defects and suggesting improvements. This is the GxP version of user-centered design, intended to ensure the system is "truly fit for use," not merely "technically passed the tests."

6.2.4–6.2.9: Core System Activities

6.2.4 Requirements Specification (RS)

The RS describes what the system should do. The RS is the responsibility of the regulated company but may be written by a third party or supplier. It should be adequately reviewed by SMEs and approved by the process owner.

6.2.5 Determine Strategy for Achieving Compliance

6.2.5.1 Risk Assessment

An initial risk assessment should be performed during planning to determine whether the system is GxP regulated, the impact of the system, and the need for further risk assessments (described in Chapter 5).

6.2.5.2 Assessment of System Components

The process of assessing system components applies the GAMP software categories and hardware categories as input to establishing the required activities, based on how the system is constructed or configured. This should take into account architecture, complexity, and novelty, including maturity and level of configuration or customization. Categorization should, however, be regarded as only part of the process of defining the required life cycle strategy based on critical thinking.

6.2.5.3 Supplier Assessment and Education

The regulated company should formally assess each supplier to establish their quality capability. The assessment may find that a supplier has a well-established QMS or has attained recognized third-party certification such as ISO 9001. The strategy should take account of assessment conclusions.

If another regulated company has already assessed the supplier for the same reason, then subject to that company agreeing to share that information, an additional assessment may not be necessary. The justification for not assessing a specific supplier should be formally documented.

Regulated companies should be prepared to assist in the education and training of suppliers.

6.2.6 Planning

Planning is an essential activity for any system development and should address all aspects, including activities that demonstrate compliance and fitness for intended use. Since the supplier may provide deliverables or directly support activities, planning provides the opportunity to decide how best to leverage supplier activities and documentation to avoid unnecessary duplication.

6.2.7 System Specifications

Types of specifications may include functional specifications, configuration specifications, and design specifications. In Agile development, types and naming of deliverables may differ. The applicability depends on the specific system and should be defined during planning.

Design Reviews evaluate deliverables against standards and requirements, identify issues, and propose corrective actions. For GAMP Category 3, design reviews by the regulated company are not typically required. For Category 4, focus on configuration and customization. For Category 5, may be conducted at various levels of specification.

6.2.8 Development and Review of Software for Custom Applications

Code reviews by the regulated company are not required for standard and configurable software products. Custom applications and custom software should be developed in accordance with defined standards. The need for and extent of reviews should be based on risk, complexity, and novelty.

6.2.9 Test Strategy and Testing

The regulated company is responsible for ensuring that the test strategy will demonstrate compliance and fitness for intended use. The number and types of tests should be based on risk, complexity, and novelty. The role of the supplier, including use of existing supplier documentation, should be considered when developing the strategy.

The results of testing should be documented against defined acceptance criteria based on specifications. Test failures should be captured, reviewed, documented, and managed.

6.2.4–6.2.9 Core activities: Interpretation and Application

6.2.4 URS: the most underrated document

The requirements specification (RS/URS) is the foundation of the whole validation pyramid. A vague URS leaves every later test without an anchor: what to test, how to set acceptance criteria, and how to judge whether a deviation is significant.

GAMP 5 stresses that the RS is the responsibility of the regulated company. A third party or the supplier may draft it, but an SME must review it and the Process Owner must approve it. It cannot be a matter of simply "stamping the document the supplier gave you."

6.2.5.2 System assessment: categorization is a means, not an end

GAMP software categories (Cat 1, 3, 4, 5) are a tool, not the destination. GAMP 5 states clearly that "categorization should be seen as part of defining the life cycle strategy, not all of it."

Practical implication: a Cat 4 COTS system that is highly customized and supports a critical process may need a validation depth close to Cat 5. Conversely, a system that is technically Cat 5 but low risk can be validated with a lighter approach.

6.2.5.3 Supplier assessment: the highest-leverage activity

This is the cost-effectiveness tool that GAMP 5 Second Edition stresses most: by assessing the quality of the supplier's QMS, you directly reduce your in-house validation effort.

  • Supplier holds ISO 9001 certification → the development process has basic assurance
  • Supplier has a SOC 2 report → information security and availability have been assessed by a third party
  • Other regulated companies have already assessed the same supplier → their conclusions can be referenced (with written agreement)

All of these can be used to reduce the scope of your IQ/OQ testing, provided you state clearly in the validation plan how you are using the supplier's data.

6.2.7 Adapting specification documents to Agile

GAMP 5 Second Edition specifically addresses Agile development. Traditional FS/DS documents may not exist, but the core requirement is unchanged: there must be enough specification information to support validation, although the form may differ (for example, User Stories + Acceptance Criteria).

6.2.9 Test strategy: look at what the supplier has first, then decide what to do yourself

The problem at many CDMOs is that as soon as they receive a system they start running IQ/OQ/PQ without looking at the supplier's test documentation at all. The GAMP 5 approach is to first assess which test documents the supplier has, and then decide which tests need to be repeated and which can be referenced. This avoids a great deal of duplicate testing.

"The number and type of tests should be based on risk, complexity and novelty." This is GAMP 5's core argument against "reflexive test everything."

6.2.10–6.2.12: Reporting, Operation, and Retirement

6.2.10 Reporting and Release

At the conclusion of the project, a computerized system validation report should be produced summarizing:

  • The activities performed
  • Any deviations from the plan
  • Any outstanding and corrective actions
  • A statement of fitness for intended use of the system

In some cases, specific computerized system validation reports may not be required. Release of the system into the operating environment should be performed in accordance with a controlled and documented process.

6.2.11 Maintaining System Compliance During Operation

The regulated company is responsible for maintaining system compliance during operation. This includes the operational processes described in Section 4.3 of GAMP 5, covering:

  • Change and configuration management
  • Periodic review
  • Incident management
  • Business continuity and disaster recovery
  • Security management
  • Data backup and archiving

6.2.12 System Retirement

System retirement is the managed withdrawal of the computerized system from use. Key considerations include:

  • Migration of data to a new system, if applicable
  • Archiving of data and documentation for the required retention period
  • Ensuring continued accessibility to archived data
  • Decommissioning activities documented and controlled

6.2.10–6.2.12: Interpretation and Application

6.2.10 Validation report: the importance of the "Fit for Intended Use" statement

The core of the validation summary report (VSR) is one statement: this system is fit for its intended use. This is not just a formatting requirement; it means someone has made a judgment on the whole validation process and accepted responsibility for it.

Unresolved deviations and outstanding CAPAs in the report must be recorded, together with the rationale for why they do not affect system release. You cannot pretend the problems do not exist.

6.2.11 Maintaining compliance: "the real challenge begins after release"

Many companies put all their resources into the validation project and neglect maintenance after release. The operational activities listed in GAMP 5 (change management, periodic review, incident management and so on) make up the longest phase of the system life cycle and the place where control is most easily lost.

For a CDMO, what client audits check most often is: is your change management SOP being followed? When was the last periodic review?

6.2.12 System retirement: the most easily overlooked life cycle phase

When a new system goes live, nobody tends to think about a retirement plan for the old one. But GxP requires that data remain accessible for the legally required retention period. The retirement plan must answer:

  • Where will the data go? Will the format remain readable?
  • How long will it be retained? Who is responsible for custody?
  • If an audit needs data from five years ago, can it be found within a reasonable time?

Section Summary

Key English Takeaways

  • QRM is iterative and proportional — not a one-time exercise, and effort must match risk level throughout the life cycle.
  • Process RA precedes system RA — a system cannot have higher impact than the business process it supports.
  • Five QRM steps: Initial RA → Identify critical functions → Functional RA & controls → Implement & verify → Review & monitor.
  • Governance is the foundation — policy, roles, inventory, and validation planning must be in place before system-specific activities.
  • Regulatory accountability cannot be delegated — supplier activities support compliance, but the regulated company remains responsible.
  • Table 6.1 is not prescriptive — apply the 12 steps with critical thinking based on risk, complexity, and novelty of each system.
  • Supplier assessment reduces testing — leveraging supplier QMS data and documentation is encouraged to avoid unnecessary duplication.
  • Data ownership ≠ system ownership — data belongs to the Process Owner, not the System Owner (typically IT).

Commentary: Key Insights

  • QRM is a decision framework, not a document format: the aim is to concentrate validation resources where the real risk is, rather than spreading them evenly.
  • The essence of the five-step process: look at process risk first → then system functions → identify controls → verify they work → review periodically.
  • Governance is the backbone of the CSV program: without policy, inventory and clear division of responsibilities, even a very well executed single validation project cannot be sustained.
  • The most common CDMO gaps: incomplete system inventory, confusion between Process Owner and System Owner, missing retirement plans, and periodic reviews done as a formality.
  • Supplier assessment is a leverage tool: ISO 9001 and SOC 2 certification and other companies' assessment conclusions can all be used to reduce the scope of in-house testing.
  • What is the opposite of science-based QRM? Not asking about risk, not looking at the process, and applying the same depth of validation to everything (both over-validation and under-validation are problems).
  • SMEs lead validation: not QA-led, not supplier-led, but led by SMEs who understand the business process and the system, with QA providing independent oversight.
ISPE GAMP 5 Second Edition | doc p69–p84

Learning Objectives

  1. Distinguish the three supplier product categories (Cat 3/4/5) and how each changes the scope of supplier involvement in CSV.
  2. Describe the 12-step Supplier Good Practices framework (Table 7.1) and map it to your own vendor qualification workflow.
  3. Explain when sub-supplier assessments are required and why cloud hosting introduces direct compliance risk.
  4. Apply the criteria for leveraging supplier documentation to reduce regulated company verification burden, with documented rationale.
  5. Explain all 9 efficiency improvement strategies and identify which ones offer the greatest time savings for a CDMO with limited CSV resources.
  6. Evaluate whether screenshot/witness evidence is required for a given test, using the GAMP 5 secondary evidence framework.

Chapter 7 — Supplier Activities

7.1 Supplier Products, Applications, and Services

Although the responsibility for compliance with GxP regulations lies with the regulated company, the supplier may have considerable involvement in the process. Regulated companies may be able to leverage supplier knowledge and documentation, subject to suitability following formal assessment. This may involve an audit, depending on risk, complexity, and novelty.

Suppliers provide a range of products, applications, and services for hardware, software, and related technologies including cloud-computing services. The relationship between supplier and regulated company will vary significantly depending upon the product, application, or scope of service being provided. Consultants, for instance those acting as implementation partners, should have sufficient education, training, and experience to advise on the subject for which they are retained.

7.1.1 Standard Product (GAMP Category 3)

If the product is purchased off-the-shelf and does not require configuration to support business processes, or only offers defined ranges of factory-provided values or ranges (parameterization), supplier involvement with the regulated company is typically limited to the provision of documentation, training, support, and maintenance. The product should be developed and maintained by the supplier in accordance with good practices.

7.1.2 Configured Product (GAMP Category 4)

If the product requires configuration to support specific business processes, supplier involvement will typically include support with specification, configuration, verification, and operation of the system. Procedures to follow should be agreed between the regulated company and the supplier and be documented in the appropriate plan. Procedures adopted may be those of the regulated company or from the supplier QMS.

7.1.3 Custom Application (GAMP Category 5)

For a custom application, the regulated company typically contracts a supplier to develop the application based on defined requirements. Therefore, the supplier will be involved during the full project life cycle of the system, and also to provide support during system operation. Procedures to follow should be agreed between the regulated company and the supplier and be documented in the appropriate plan.

7.1.4 Service Provision

Suppliers that provide IT/IS services (including cloud-computing services) should operate within a QMS. Quality planning should define the activities, procedures, deliverables, and responsibilities for establishing delivery and monitoring of the service. Such a plan is a contractual document, and as such, should be approved for use by both the supplier and the regulated company.

The required information may be satisfactorily covered by other contractual documents such as a service level agreement (SLA), in which case a separate plan would not be required.

Commentary: Supplier Categories and Division of Responsibilities

Practical Analogy: How Common CDMO Systems Are Categorized

Cat 3 examples: balances (Sartorius / Mettler-Toledo), pH meters. No configuration needed, only calibration records.

Cat 4 examples: SCADA systems (Wonderware, iFix), LIMS (LabWare, SampleManager). Extensive configuration is required, but the core software is maintained by the vendor.

Cat 5 examples: in-house BPR systems, custom reporting systems. The vendor develops to your requirements and you must validate fully, including FS/DS review.

Who Is Responsible for Compliance?

GxP regulatory responsibility always rests with the regulated company (i.e., the CDMO); the supplier is only a helper. Even if the supplier has performed all the testing, the CDMO must still evaluate those test results and formally accept them. This does not change with Cat 3/4/5.

Special Considerations for Cloud Services (SaaS/IaaS)

A cloud service provider must operate within its own QMS, and the quality plan must be jointly approved by both parties. If the SLA already covers the necessary information, a separate quality plan can be waived. Refer to ISPE GAMP GPG: Enabling Innovation, Chapter 4 (IT Service Management).

7.2 Supplier Good Practices

Table 7.1 lists good practice activities that apply to product and application development, support, and service provision.

Commentary: The 12-Step Supplier Good Practices

Why Does a CDMO Need to Understand These 12 Steps?

During supplier assessment, you need to verify that the supplier carries out these 12 steps. If the supplier's QMS fully covers these activities, you can leverage their testing and documentation, thereby reducing your own validation workload.

In Practice: Supplier Audit Checklist

When you conduct a paper audit or on-site audit of a LIMS or MES vendor, the following are high-value items to check:

  • Step 1 (QMS): Does the supplier hold ISO 9001 certification, or an equivalent internal QMS?
  • Step 6 (Design review): Are design review records available to you? Can they support your DQ?
  • Step 8 (Testing): Are the FAT/SAT test scripts and result records complete? Is the format suitable for inclusion in your validation package?
  • Step 9 (Commercial release): Does the supplier have clear release criteria? Do the release notes describe known defects?
  • Step 11 (Maintenance): Is the patch management process documented? Is the security update schedule transparent?

Important Distinction: Commercial Release vs GxP Release

Step 9, "commercial release", is the supplier's formal release of a product to customers; it is not the act of putting the system into a GxP environment. "Release to Production" into the GxP environment is always the responsibility of the regulated company and requires IQ/OQ/PQ and Quality Unit approval.

7.3 Quality Management System

It is recommended that suppliers follow a QMS, preferably based on recognized standards. The QMS should define:

  • The process being followed to deliver and support the product, application, or service
  • The process to understand customer business process needs and capture requirements (including business, quality, and regulatory requirements)
  • Responsibilities, including clear separation of authority between QA and other groups such as product development, product support, finance, or marketing
  • Deliverables
  • Management of documentation, records, and information
  • Planned reviews of the QMS and internal audits
  • Management of incidents/problems/non-conformities
  • Approach to continual improvement, including the adoption of current software development models, methods and good practices, and associated selection of supporting tools and automation

The QMS should be based on a life cycle concept. There are many equally valid life cycle approaches that may be used by suppliers, including Agile. This Guide does not recommend any particular approach, but rather highlights those activities expected of suppliers to support the regulated company in achieving and maintaining compliance.

The QMS should include procedures covering:

  • Software management, control, and release
  • Development change control
  • Configuration management
  • Traceability
  • Training of supplier staff
  • Document, records, and information management
  • Backup and restore

The QMS should cover the approach to continual improvement. CMMI provides an approach based on a framework for assessing and improving organizational capability and maturity. The use of ITIL for IT service delivery is encouraged. Industry guidance such as ISPE GAMP should be treated as supporting information and should not override the supplier's established QMS.

Commentary: Supplier QMS Requirements

Both Agile and Traditional Waterfall Development Are Acceptable

GAMP 5 Second Edition explicitly states no preference for a particular lifecycle model. Whether Waterfall, Agile (Scrum/Kanban) or a hybrid approach, all are acceptable as long as the supplier can provide traceable documentation that meets regulatory requirements.

Practical implication for a CDMO: when a supplier says "We use Agile, so we have no traditional FS/DS", you should ask for equivalent Agile documentation (User Stories, Sprint Review Records, Acceptance Criteria) rather than rejecting it outright.

QA Independence in the QMS

A supplier QMS requires a clear separation of responsibilities between QA and departments such as development, marketing and finance. This is the same concept as QA independence in pharmaceutical GMP. When assessing a supplier, you can ask: "Who has the authority to approve a product release? Can QA independently block a non-compliant release?"

CMMI and ITIL Are a Plus

A supplier holding CMMI Level 3+ certification demonstrates higher software development maturity, which can serve as a basis for reducing on-site audits. IT service providers with ITIL certification have standardized processes for incident, change and problem management, which is especially important for SaaS platforms.

7.4 Requirements

Requirements are captured and defined internally by the supplier (in the case of product development), and/or may be provided by the customer (for a configured product, custom application, or a service). The requirements should define clearly and precisely what the system should do and state any constraints.

Requirements should be developed, reviewed, and approved in accordance with the development method implemented by the supplier whether it be linear or iterative. Changes to requirements should be controlled.

Regulated companies wish to maximize the use of supplier testing to support their compliance activities. Therefore, requirements should be written such that they can be tested. Individual requirements should be traceable through the life cycle.

For configured products and custom applications, the regulated company should describe the business processes to be automated. In the case of configured products, these processes should be aligned with the functionality of the product to be used. This may require significant process reengineering.

The use of appropriate and effective software tools should be considered by suppliers to manage information/records that may otherwise have been managed via paper documentation.

Commentary: Testability of Requirements Specifications

Core Principle: Requirements Must Be Testable

GAMP 5 stresses that supplier requirements must be "testable". This is not only a requirement for suppliers; it is also a core criterion for the regulated company when reviewing the URS/RS. An untestable requirement (such as "the system shall be easy to use") is meaningless in validation.

Configured Products May Require Process Reengineering

When procuring a Cat 4 system (such as SAP or Oracle), business processes need to "fit the standard functionality of the system", rather than forcing the system to fit all existing processes. GAMP 5 explicitly states that this "may require significant process reengineering". A CDMO introducing a large LIMS or ERP should assess this cost in advance.

7.5 Supplier Quality Planning

The supplier should define how the QMS will be implemented for a particular product, application, or service. This should include defining the life cycle model, project organization, activities, procedures, deliverables, and responsibilities for establishing fitness for intended use. The approach may include prototyping or other software development techniques. The role of supplier QA should be clearly defined.

These supplier quality requirements may be captured in a separate Quality Plan or other supplier documentation. Quality requirements should be clearly documented, reviewed, approved, accessible, and followed.

7.5.1 Prototyping

Prototyping methods may be used to clarify user requirements or to evaluate areas of risk. Typically, a prototype is used to evaluate the acceptability of a user interface, the performance of critical algorithms, suitability of the overall solution, or aspects of system performance such as capacity and speed.

The aims and objectives of the prototype should be clearly defined, and the prototype evaluated against these to ensure the objectives are met. Prototyping is often used as part of Agile software development where control is achieved through Agile ceremonies (e.g., daily Scrum), control of the sprint backlog, use of tools, and sprint reviews and retrospectives.

Commentary: Supplier Quality Plan

Core Content of a Quality Plan

For the regulated company, the supplier quality plan is a very important document for assessment. It should cover:

  • The lifecycle model adopted (Waterfall, Agile, hybrid)
  • QA roles and division of responsibilities
  • List of key deliverables
  • Overview of the test strategy
  • Change control procedures

If a supplier cannot provide a quality plan, this is a red flag and should be recorded in the supplier assessment report.

What Prototyping Means for a CDMO

When you commission a custom system (such as BPR automation or an equipment interface system), it is advisable to ask the supplier to provide a prototype first. This lets you verify, before heavy development investment, that key functions meet the requirements, and identify potential risks, lowering the cost of late changes. An Agile Sprint Demo is one form of prototyping.

7.6 Sub-Supplier Assessments

Suppliers should formally assess their sub-suppliers as part of quality planning. They also should be periodically reassessed in accordance with the QMS.

The decision whether to perform an audit of their sub-suppliers should be documented and based on a risk assessment.

In some cases, such as those sub-suppliers related to the location and hosting of GxP regulated data, sub-supplier management may have a direct impact on regulated company compliance, and should be made transparent.

See Appendix M2 for further details on supplier assessments.

Commentary: Sub-supplier Assessment

Sub-supplier Risk in the Cloud Era

This is one of the most important topics in modern CSV. When you use a SaaS LIMS (such as Veeva Vault or Benchling) and the LIMS vendor's servers run on AWS or Azure, AWS/Azure is your sub-supplier.

GAMP 5 explicitly states that where a sub-supplier is involved in the storage and hosting of GxP data, sub-supplier management has a direct impact on the compliance of the regulated company and must be transparent.

In Practice: Handling Cloud Sub-suppliers

  • Ask the main supplier to provide: a summary of its sub-supplier assessment report (or ISO 27001 or SOC 2 Type II certification)
  • State in the Technical Agreement: data storage location, backup strategy, DR plan
  • Periodic reassessment: if a sub-supplier (such as a cloud platform) suffers a major security incident, the main supplier should notify you
