  • Supplier switching clause: if the main supplier changes cloud platform, it should notify you in advance and allow you to perform an impact assessment

Third-party Libraries Are Also Sub-suppliers

When a software supplier uses open-source or commercial third-party libraries in its product (such as log4j or OpenSSL), the suppliers of those libraries also fall within the scope of sub-suppliers. The log4shell vulnerability in recent years is a typical example. A good supplier should maintain a Software Bill of Materials (SBOM) and perform dependency security scanning.

7.7 Specifications

For product development, the supplier should document the functionality and design of the system to meet the defined requirements. This should cover software, hardware, and configuration.

References to documentation and specifications should not be interpreted as requiring traditional documents, and the maintenance of records in appropriate and effective software tools is encouraged.

Functional specifications should clearly and completely describe what the product will do, produced such that objective testing can be subsequently performed. Design specifications should be based on the functional specifications and should be sufficiently detailed so that the product can be developed.

Specifications should be reviewed and approved with traceability established between related documents. They should be managed under change control.

If the supplier is involved in configuring a product, service, or developing a custom application, the number and level of specifications can vary considerably and should be agreed with the regulated company.

7.8 Design Reviews

Design reviews evaluate deliverables against standards and requirements, identify issues, and propose required corrective actions. They should be planned and systematic reviews of specifications, design, and development, and should be planned to occur at suitable stages during the life cycle, based on risk, complexity, and novelty. Design reviews aim to identify and eliminate issues that would otherwise lead to changes at a later stage.

7.9 Software Production / Configuration

The supplier should establish and maintain a formal system for controlling software production. Appropriate methods and tools should be used and defined. Rules and conventions, such as acceptable languages, coding standards, version control, and naming conventions should be established. The use of code reviews should be considered.

Existing software should be used in accordance with documented build processes and taking into account any changes in the system hardware, interfaces, and peripherals.

If the supplier is involved in configuring a product, it should be performed in accordance with the controlling configuration specification and follow appropriate guidelines and recommendations.

Commentary: Specifications, Design Review and Software Development

Flexibility of Specification Documents

GAMP 5 explicitly states that specification documents do not have to be in the traditional Word document format. Suppliers may use:

  • Epics/Stories in Jira (Agile approach)
  • Requirements management tools (such as DOORS, Polarion)
  • Design drawings plus technical datasheets

The key point is that these records must be traceable, under change control, and able to support subsequent testing.

Design Review: CDMO Practice

For Cat 5 custom systems, you as the regulated company are advised to participate in the supplier's design reviews rather than merely receive the final report. This is like a quality check during construction, which is far cheaper than finding problems after handover.

Records of your participation in design reviews can serve as DQ evidence, reducing the formal DQ work needed later.

The Importance of Code Review

GAMP 5 recommends (but does not mandate) that suppliers implement code review. For Cat 5 systems, you should ask about the code review process during supplier assessment: is it manual review, an automated static analysis tool (SonarQube), or both? This directly affects your confidence in system reliability, and also how deeply you need to test later.

7.10 Testing

For product development, the supplier should test the product in accordance with approved test plans and test specifications. The test specifications, when executed, should demonstrate that all requirements, functionality, and design have been met.

This may involve one or many stages of testing depending on the nature of the product:

  • Module (unit) testing
  • Integration testing
  • System testing

Test records for each stage should be reviewed and approved, and retained for a period defined in the QMS (not to be shorter than the supported lifetime of the current software version). Test failures should be managed in accordance with a formal documented process.

If the supplier is involved in configuring a product or developing a custom application, the number and level of test specifications can vary considerably and should be agreed with the regulated company.

7.11 Commercial Release

System release to customers should be performed in accordance with a formal process that describes the criteria for release, responsibilities, records to be retained, and items to be released, including software, hardware, and documentation. Clear policies and criteria should be established for the acceptability or otherwise of product release, based on the number and severity of known defects. Release notes defining fixes, changes, known problems, and new features should accompany each release, including minor releases and patches.

Note: Commercial release by a supplier is not a release into the GxP environment, which is a regulated company activity.

7.12 User Documentation and Training

The supplier should provide adequate system management documentation, operational documentation, and training for both maintenance and operation in accordance with agreed contracts.

7.13 System Support and Maintenance During Operation

The supplier should support and maintain the system in accordance with agreed contracts. Formal procedures should be followed, typically covering:

  • Operational change control
  • Configuration management
  • Patch management
  • Incident management
  • Documentation management
  • Backup and restore
  • Business continuity
  • Disaster Recovery (DR)
  • Managing software product releases
  • Training of supplier staff
  • System maintenance
  • Security management

7.14 System Replacement and Retirement

The supplier should manage the replacement or withdrawal of products or service in accordance with a documented process and plans. Sufficient notice of the retirement of a system or version should be given to regulated companies to allow them to plan for their required activities. The supplier also may support the regulated company with the retirement of computerized systems.

Commentary: Testing, Release and Lifecycle

Release Notes Are an Important Supplier Document

GAMP 5 requires that every release (including minor version updates and patches) be accompanied by release notes listing fixes, changes, known issues and new features.

Practical implication for a CDMO: when handling supplier version updates, release notes are your first-hand source for assessing the scope of impact and deciding whether revalidation is needed, and they are also supporting documents for the change control record.

Retirement Notice: A Clause That Cannot Be Forgotten

GAMP 5 requires the supplier to give "sufficient notice" before a system is retired. In the Technical Agreement, it is advisable to agree explicitly on the minimum notice period (at least 12 months is recommended) and whether the supplier will assist with data migration. The consequence of omitting this clause: the supplier suddenly announces the end of support for a version, your validated system instantly becomes unsupported software, and you face significant inspection risk.

Coverage of the 13 Maintenance Areas

The 12 maintenance areas listed in Section 7.13 (change control, configuration management, patching, incident management, etc.) form a complete framework for assessing a supplier's service capability. When drafting the Technical Agreement, responsibility for each of these 12 should be confirmed one by one and made concrete in the SLA (e.g., incident response time, backup frequency, DR exercise cycle).

Layered Architecture of Supplier Testing

Understanding the layers of supplier testing helps you decide which tests can be leveraged and which you must perform yourself:

Chapter 8 — Efficiency Improvements

Chapter 8 Introduction

This Guide provides a flexible framework for achieving compliant computerized systems that are fit for their intended use. The benefits will be obtained only if the framework is applied effectively in the context of a particular organization.

In this Second Edition of the Guide many examples of efficiency improvements are also discussed in the context of critical thinking.

Aspects that can assist efficiency include:

  • Establishing verifiable and appropriate user requirements
  • Making risk-based decisions
  • Leveraging supplier input
  • Leveraging existing records and information
  • Using efficient testing practices
  • Employing a well-managed handover process
  • Managing changes efficiently
  • Anticipating data archiving and migration needs
  • Using tools and automation

Commentary: Overview of the 9 Efficiency Improvements

Efficiency Improvement Checklist for CSV Project Managers

  • Can every requirement in the URS be objectively tested? (8.1)
  • Are systems classified by risk, with more validation effort invested in high-risk ones? (8.2)
  • Have you assessed whether the supplier's FAT/unit test reports can be leveraged directly? (8.3)
  • Are there existing test scripts for similar systems within the company that can be reused? (8.4)
  • Is the depth of testing based on risk, rather than "test everything"? (8.5)
  • Are screenshots and witnessing actually needed, or are electronic records sufficient? (8.5)
  • Does the handover plan include a Hypercare period and Rollback procedure? (8.6)
  • Can changes pass quickly, avoiding unnecessary approval layers? (8.7)
  • Are data retention periods and format standardization planned before implementation? (8.8)
  • Are automated testing tools, ALM and electronic protocol management used effectively? (8.9)

The Reality of Limited CDMO Resources

Many small and mid-sized CDMOs have CSV teams with limited headcount and cannot validate every system to the same depth. The 9 strategies in GAMP 5 Chapter 8 are tools to help limited resources achieve the most efficient compliance investment. The core logic: focus effort where it truly affects patient safety, product quality and data integrity.

8.1 Establishing Verifiable and Objective User Requirements

Requirements should be analyzed to ensure that they are fully defined and are verifiable and objective. For example:

Incomplete Requirement: Room shall be controlled at 20°C.

Complete Requirement: Room shall be controlled at 20°C ± 2°C. Excursions of no greater than 7°C are permitted for <10 minutes.

The level of detail is dependent on the novelty and complexity of the processes and system being implemented. Table 8.1 lists aspects to consider when developing verifiable and objective requirements.

Commentary: A Verifiable URS

Why Do Vague URS Statements Waste Time?

Vague requirements cause losses late in a CSV project that far exceed the time spent clarifying them up front:

  • Testers cannot tell whether a test has passed (the PASS/FAIL criteria are unclear)
  • Extra approval cycles are needed to explain what a requirement means
  • During an inspection, regulators may question the validity of the validation
  • Under change control, it is hard to judge whether a change is "like-for-like"

The Seven Aspects of Table 8.1: Roles in the URS Review Meeting

In URS review meetings, make sure each of the following areas of knowledge is represented:

  • Process knowledge: process engineer or batch production personnel
  • Business knowledge: business process owner (Process Owner)
  • Analytical capability: quality engineer or system validation personnel
  • Technology/product: IT or equipment engineer
  • Process/product impact: quality assurance (QA)
  • Technical writing: document control personnel or a validation engineer with writing experience

Flag CQA / CPP Requirements Specifically

Requirements directly related to critical quality attributes (CQAs) or critical process parameters (CPPs) should be clearly flagged in the URS (for example with a "GxP Critical" label). These requirements receive higher priority in later risk assessment and test depth decisions, and are also a focus of inspections.

8.2 Making Risk-Based Decisions

Risk management provides an opportunity to scale life cycle activities. Benefit may also be achieved if organizations use risk assessments as input to decisions to omit or include an activity. Examples of areas where risk assessments may help with scaling include:

  • Number and depth of design reviews required
  • Need for, and extent of, source-code review
  • Rigor of supplier assessment
  • Depth and rigor of testing

Similar opportunities exist during system operation:

  • Extent and level of specification and verification of changes
  • Rigor of the backup and restore process
  • Level of business continuity required
  • Frequency and level of DR
  • Degree to which identity checks are completed prior to providing access rights
  • Scope and frequency of periodic reviews

The benefits of risk-based decision-making can be maximized only if the conclusions and decisions can be leveraged. Therefore, there should be a practical, searchable means of access to conclusions and decisions available to those involved in decision-making — e.g., a risk register.

The risk-based approach should be focused and resourced for maximum effectiveness and efficiency. Organizations should not invest more effort and time into the risk-management process than is commensurate with the potential impact on patient safety, product quality, and data integrity.

Commentary: Pragmatic Application of Risk-based Decisions

Risk Tiering Determines Validation Depth

The core logic of GAMP 5: not all systems and functions need the same depth of validation. The risk assessment should answer:

  • If this system fails, what is the impact on patient safety, product quality and data integrity?
  • Is the likelihood of failure reduced by the supplier's good practices?
  • Do other existing controls (SOPs, environmental monitoring, manual review) reduce the residual risk?

Risk Decisions Must Be Retrievable and Reusable

GAMP 5 specifically notes that risk assessment conclusions must be accessible in a searchable way. Using a "Risk Register" is best practice. Each time you validate a similar system or assess a change, you can cite earlier risk conclusions without starting from scratch. This is a key tool for improving efficiency.

Warning: Excessive Risk Assessment Is Also Waste

GAMP 5 has a key caution: "organizations should not spend more time and effort in the risk management process than its potential impact is worth." In other words, writing a 20-page risk assessment report for a simple benchtop pH meter is itself a waste of resources. Following the principle of proportionality, spend time on high-risk systems.

8.3 Leveraging Supplier Input

Supplier activities, including testing, may be used by the regulated company as part of verification, provided the regulated company has assessed the supplier processes as suitable. This assessment may include a supplier audit, depending on the risk, complexity, and novelty of the system.

The regulated company should assess the supplier for evidence of:

  • An acceptable supplier quality system
  • Supplier technical capability
  • Supplier application of good practice such that activities performed by and information obtained from the supplier will be complete, accurate, and suitable to meet the purpose of verification

Supplier documentation, information, and records should be assessed for content and quality. Regulated company procedures and processes should be flexible regarding acceptable format and structures so that supplier documentation may be leveraged.

If inadequacies are found in the supplier quality system, technical capability, or documentation, then the regulated company may choose to manage potential risks by applying specific, targeted additional verification checks or other controls, rather than repeating supplier activities and replicating supplier documentation.

The decision and justification to use supplier activities to support verification should be based on the intended use of the system and should be documented and approved by SMEs, which may include the quality unit.

Suppliers also may have tools or techniques unique to the specification and testing of their product which may be leveraged by the regulated company during specification and testing.

Commentary: Strategies for Leveraging Supplier Documentation

The Strategy That Saves a CDMO the Most Time

For a CDMO with limited resources, Section 8.3 is the most practically valuable part of Chapter 8. Applied correctly, it can save 30-60% of validation testing effort.

Three Prerequisites for Leveraging Supplier Documents

Leveraging must have a documented basis, and all three prerequisites must be met:

  1. Supplier QMS assessment passed: you must assess the supplier's quality system (paper audit or on-site audit) and have a documented conclusion
  2. Technical capability confirmed: the supplier has sufficient technical capability to perform the tests, and the test methods are reliable
  3. Document format acceptable: your SOPs allow the use of supplier documents in non-standard formats (review your company's validation SOP in advance)

Practical Advice: Making Supplier Documents Easier to Leverage

During negotiation of the purchase contract or Technical Agreement, proactively request:

  • Test reports from the supplier in a GAMP 5-compliant format (not its internal format)
  • FAT test scripts that can be customized to your URS
  • Test record retention periods no shorter than your regulatory requirements
  • Permission for you to review (without having to hold all of) the supplier's detailed test records

8.4 Leveraging Existing Information

In addition to the use of supplier activities and information, regulated companies should also leverage their own activities and information related to existing systems when introducing new, similar systems. Examples include:

  • Laboratory equipment
  • Secondary manufacturing equipment
  • Packaging equipment

Relevant information to reuse may include risk assessments, RS, various plans, test specifications, test results, and design reviews.

The new system should be assessed and any differences with the existing system identified and managed by the introduction of appropriate specification and verification as required. A review of existing information should determine which may be used as is or updated as required. These conclusions should be documented.

The new system should be subject to installation and verification based on user requirements. The validation report should explain the rationale for reuse of documentation.

Commentary: Reusing Existing Documentation

Document Reuse Strategy for "Similar Systems"

When the company already has a validation package for a given brand of system and purchases new equipment of the same brand and model (or a similar model), existing documents can be reused extensively. The scope of reuse:

  • Risk assessment (RA): update the difference analysis and add risks specific to the new equipment
  • Requirements specification (RS/URS): copy and revise into a version suited to the new equipment
  • Test scripts: copy directly and confirm version differences before execution
  • Validation plan/report templates: copy and update the system description and information

Reuse Must Be Supported by a Documented Difference Analysis

GAMP 5 requires that if existing documents are reused, the differences between the old and new systems must be formally assessed and the rationale for reuse explained in the validation report. "It's the same, so we just used it" is not enough; you need a written record such as "after assessment, the differences were confirmed as X, the impact on the original test scripts is Y, and therefore it was decided to reuse/update item Z".

A Typical CDMO Scenario

A filling line is expanded from four heads to six, adding two filling pumps of the same model. The IQ/OQ/PQ documents of the existing four pumps can serve as a starting point:

  • IQ: copy the installation qualification scripts directly and perform on-site installation qualification of the new machines
  • OQ: leverage the functional test scripts and confirm that the parameter ranges of the new machines are consistent
  • PQ: must be re-executed under the new configuration, because the flow distribution across the whole line has changed

8.5 Using Efficient Testing Practices

Testing is a major, time-consuming exercise. It perhaps offers the greatest opportunity for efficiency savings.

8.5.1 Reuse of Test Results

Many systems have large amounts of test results available due to suppliers following a QMS independently of a regulated company. On a project, there may be pre-delivery testing, which may include factory acceptance testing (FAT). Wherever possible, regulated companies should clearly communicate to suppliers the testing and document requirements in advance such that supplier test documentation is of the required standard to support compliance activities.

Testing also may take place to meet other business or legal requirements, such as HSE, and finance such as SOX. Unnecessary duplication of testing should be avoided.

8.5.2 Extent of Required Testing

The level and type of testing should be risk-based and based on the nature of the component involved (standard, configurable, or custom). Types of testing include:

  • Normal case (positive)
  • Invalid case (negative)
  • Repeatability
  • Performance
  • Volume/load
  • Regression
  • Structural testing

While user requirements should be verified by the regulated company through installation and acceptance tests demonstrating fitness for intended use, other required tests should be identified based on risk, complexity, and novelty. The regulated company's procedures should allow for the use of such existing test evidence subject to a documented and justified review and approval by an SME.

8.5.3 Secondary Test Evidence

The generation and retention of secondary supporting hard copy or image evidence such as screenshots, in addition to the primary test result or output, is unnecessary in most cases and does not add value. It often adds significant cost without associated benefit. Such additional evidence should be generated and maintained only where value-added and necessary for effective testing.

Examples where additional test evidence may be useful:

  • Complex results, which may be difficult or time consuming to record manually
  • When the result is essentially visual or pictorial
  • Where there is a need for before and after comparisons

Test evidence may be retained electronically provided adequate security and retention mechanisms are established. Systems may also have data audit trails or other system logs that capture much of the information traditionally captured by screen prints.

8.5.4 Use of Test Witnesses

The use of witnesses during testing is not a requirement and involves a significant overhead without added benefit. A regulated company may choose to witness some tests (e.g., site or factory acceptance testing) for commercial, contractual, communication, education, or training reasons. It is disproportionate and unnecessary to require test witnessing or requiring the tester to initial every test step to affirm they followed the instructions.

Decisions to use witnesses should consider knowledge and experience of testers, practical issues (e.g., two-person processes), level and degree of review by an SME, and degree of automation of tests and resulting test evidence.

Commentary: Efficient Testing Practices

Testing Is the Biggest Opportunity for Efficiency Savings

GAMP 5 states that "testing may offer the greatest opportunity for efficiency savings". The following are strategies a CDMO can apply immediately:

8.5.1: Tell the supplier your requirements before FAT

Common mistake: waiting until the supplier has finished FAT before reviewing the documents, then finding the format unacceptable, wasting both parties' time.

Correct approach: before procurement or at the kick-off meeting, give the supplier your list of "acceptable test documentation standards", including: test script format, how Pass/Fail criteria are recorded, signature requirements for testers and reviewers, and electronic record retention format.

8.5.2: Test Sampling Strategy

There is no need to test every possible combination; decide test coverage based on risk:

  • High-risk functions: normal case + boundary values + abnormal cases + repeatability = full testing
  • Medium-risk functions: normal case + one abnormal case is sufficient
  • Low-risk functions: normal case + reference to supplier test results

For example: "calculate analytical results" in a LIMS is a high-risk function (it affects release decisions) and must be tested fully. "Change personal password" is a low-risk function and one test is enough.

8.5.3: Screenshots Are Not Mandatory!

This is where many companies have wasted large amounts of time for years. GAMP 5 explicitly states that screenshots are, in most cases, unnecessary and add no value.

Cases where screenshots are useful (only these three):

  1. The result is complex and difficult to record manually
  2. The result is inherently visual (such as charts or interface appearance)
  3. A before-and-after comparison is needed

For most numerical test results (whether a number is within range, whether a calculation is correct), recording the actual value is enough. The system's audit trail is itself a record, so there is no need to take a screenshot of it again.

8.5.4: Witnessed Testing Is Not Mandatory!

Requiring the tester to initial next to every step is "unnecessary and disproportionate"; the wording in GAMP 5 is very direct. The only reasonable grounds for witnessed testing are:

  • Commercial or contractual reasons (customer requirement)
  • Communication, education or training purposes
  • The nature of the system requires two people to operate it (for example an operator on site and an engineer in the control room)

Practical impact for a CDMO: the practice of "quality personnel must witness every test step" can be abolished and replaced by SME review of test records. This frees up a great deal of QA time.

8.6 Employing a Well-Managed Handover Process

System handover (from the project team to the process owner, system owner, and operational users) should be well-managed. It is a prerequisite for the effective maintenance of system compliance during operation. Handover should be planned in accordance with agreed criteria and should consider:

  • Support requirements for maintenance, as defined by IT or engineering
  • Outstanding problems or deficiencies
  • How long business processes can be stopped to enable handover
  • Ability and steps required to roll back to a previous operational state
  • Situations where updates are not optional (e.g., updates pushed to SaaS applications)
  • Information required at handover (specification and verification documentation, user and maintenance manuals or guides), or new or updated user procedures
  • Training needs (user, support, and maintenance)
  • Clear communication between groups, e.g., with application support and client service groups
  • The impact on the change-control process to apply during the handover period, when handover is to be phased
  • Responsibilities during handover, e.g., for accepting the system and for assessing the severity of outstanding problems or deficiencies
  • Potential need of a period of elevated support and maintenance, often referred to as hypercare
  • Preservation of implied as well as explicit knowledge, e.g., through SME lists and recording of lessons learned

Commentary: Good System Handover

Handover Is a Prerequisite for Maintaining Compliance

Many CDMOs rush to go live once validation is complete and lack a formal handover plan, resulting in:

  • Operators not receiving sufficient training
  • IT not knowing how to support the system (backup procedures, access management)
  • Unresolved issues surfacing in production
  • Tacit knowledge held only by the project team being lost after handover

Hypercare Period: Enhanced Support After Go-live

GAMP 5 specifically mentions "Hypercare", a period after the system formally goes live (typically 2-4 weeks) during which the supplier and/or project team provide enhanced support. Recommendation for a CDMO: clearly define the duration, support channels and response times of Hypercare in the Technical Agreement and project plan.

The Problem of Forced Updates for SaaS Systems

GAMP 5 explicitly lists "forced pushed updates for SaaS applications" as a factor for the handover plan to consider. For a CDMO: if you use a cloud LIMS or ELN, the supplier may push functional updates without consulting you. Your handover plan and ongoing change control procedure must include a mechanism for "how to handle involuntary updates"; otherwise the system may continue to operate in an unvalidated state.

Handover Checklist (CDMO Edition)

  • ☐ All Outstanding Issues have been classified (Critical/Major/Minor) before handover
  • ☐ The Rollback procedure has been tested and documented
  • ☐ All user training has been completed and recorded
  • ☐ IT has received the system architecture documents, backup procedures and access management SOPs
  • ☐ QA has approved go-live (including the decision to accept open OOS/deviations)
  • ☐ Support channels and contacts for the Hypercare period have been confirmed
  • ☐ Project lessons learned have been recorded (Lessons Learned)

8.7 Managing Changes Efficiently

Efficient change management should be executed in conjunction with configuration management. Key elements include:

  • Documented description and business benefit of the change
  • Confirmation of availability of resource
  • Assessment of the impact of the change on the application, the underlying infrastructure, the people (users and engineering support staff), and the documentation
  • Leveraging the risk assessment information from the original project and assessing any new risks introduced by the change to define the strategy for maintaining compliance — including the need for any regression testing
  • Evaluation of the change from the financial, technical (IT or engineering), and compliance perspectives at the lowest technically competent level prior to management approval
  • Establishing and maintaining the distinction between changes at the pharmaceutical quality system level (impacting the medicinal product life cycle) versus changes at the IT delivery and service management level
  • Minimizing the number of approval points in the process
  • Documentation and communication of the decision
  • Execution and verification of the change, using traceability to identify existing applicable tests
  • Closing the change record in a timely manner

Weaknesses in change management systems that may lead to inefficiencies include:

  • Lack of scalability for minor changes or standard infrastructure components
  • Failure to execute change management steps in the appropriate sequence
  • Failure in scheduling or identifying dependencies
  • Abuse or misapplication of emergency change processes
  • An inability to prevent unnecessary changes
  • Failure to keep specifications current
  • Failure to leverage existing documentation for risk assessment, traceability matrices, or protocols
  • Lack of follow-up processes to close a change record
  • Independent change processes leading to duplication of effort
  • Inappropriate application of like-for-like principles in change management
  • Inadequate management of changes conducted by a supplier, leading to life cycle documents and configuration management records that are out-of-date
  • Lack of adequate follow-up after emergency changes

Commentary: Efficient Change Management

Pharmaceutical Quality System Level vs IT Service Management Level

GAMP 5 specifically stresses distinguishing two levels of change:

  • Pharmaceutical quality system level: changes affecting the product lifecycle (such as BPR format changes or release criteria modifications). These must go through GMP change control with QA approval and may require revalidation.
  • IT service management level: technical changes to infrastructure (such as server updates or antivirus upgrades). These can follow the IT change management process, and when risk is low a full GMP validation process is not needed.

Confusing these two levels is the main cause of inefficiency.

Misuse of "Emergency Change" Is a Common Problem

GAMP 5 lists "abuse or misuse of the emergency change process" as a warning sign of inefficiency. Emergency changes should be clearly defined (for example, a system outage affecting production), and every emergency change must be followed by supplementary assessment and completion of documentation. If emergency changes make up too high a proportion of all changes (for example over 20%), the change management process itself should be reassessed.

Practical Improvement: Reduce Unnecessary Approval Layers

GAMP 5 explicitly recommends "minimizing approval points". Improvement measures to consider:

  • Set up a fast approval path for low-risk infrastructure changes (such as patches and antivirus updates)
  • Use a "like-for-like" fast track, but define the criteria carefully and ensure it is not abused
  • Establish a pre-approved Standard Change Catalogue
  • Complete the change assessment at the "lowest technically competent level", escalating to management only when compliance decisions are involved

Supplier Update Management: The Challenge of Keeping Documents in Sync

When the supplier updates the system, your validation documents (FS, test scripts, validation report) must be updated in step. GAMP 5 lists "lifecycle documents becoming outdated because of supplier updates" as a warning sign of inefficiency. This is especially important for SaaS systems: if the supplier pushes functional updates every quarter, your document management and revalidation assessment process must be able to sustain that frequency.

8.8 Anticipating Data Archiving and Migration Needs

Data archiving and migration requirements should be considered to ensure that data structures and formats are efficient.

8.8.1 Different Retention Periods

It may be difficult to archive data with different retention periods that share the same data structures. It may also be difficult to destroy retained data that is no longer required (to reduce risk exposure and retention costs) where data with different retention periods share the same data structures. A data structure that separates data by retention period can address the requirements of archiving and data destruction, but may involve a complex database design.

8.8.2 Data Formats

The migration of custom data formats to a replacement system requires special attention and may cause difficulties. The use of standard data formats should assist subsequent data extraction and migration.

8.8.3 Static and Dynamic Data

Data migration may be complicated where static and dynamic data is combined in a form that is difficult to separate.

8.8.4 Data Ownership

Lack of clear data ownership can lead to failure to appropriately dispose of data at the end of its retention period or make such processes and decisions unnecessarily difficult.

Commentary: Data Archiving and Migration Planning

Plan the Data Exit Strategy Before Implementation

Many companies discover that data migration is a nightmare only when they replace a system 5-10 years after go-live. GAMP 5 recommends considering the following in advance, during the implementation stage:

  • Are retention periods consistent across different types of data? (batch records vs system logs vs user activity records)
  • Is data stored in a standard format (CSV, XML) or a proprietary format?
  • Can static data (configuration, master data) be separated from dynamic data (batch results, analytical data)?
  • Who owns the data: the supplier or you?

Real Risk Scenarios for a CDMO

Scenario 1: Batch records have a 30-year retention period and system logs a 2-year retention period, but both are stored mixed together in the same database tables. After 2 years you must delete the system logs, but technically it is impossible to delete only part of the records, so you either keep everything (increasing cost) or risk deleting batch records by mistake.

Scenario 2: The LIMS vendor goes out of business or is acquired, and your analytical data is stored in the system in a proprietary format that cannot be exported to a new system, forcing you to keep paying expensive maintenance fees for the old system.

Data Ownership Clauses Must Be Explicit in the Contract

The Technical Agreement should clearly specify: your ownership of GxP data stored in the supplier's system (especially a SaaS supplier's); the data export format and timeframe after service termination; and the obligation to confirm destruction after the data retention period ends. This is a basic data integrity compliance requirement.

8.9 Using Tools and Automation

The use of appropriate tools and automation to support IT process and infrastructure management, system life cycle management, and software specification, development, and testing, can bring significant efficiency improvements, while also increasing quality and lowering risk. The use of such tools is discussed in Appendix D9.

Commentary: Tools and Automation

The Tools Themselves Also Need to Be Validated

When automated test tools are used to test GxP systems, the test tools themselves also need to be validated (Computer Software Assurance). Many CDMOs overlook this compliance requirement when adopting automated testing. For related guidance, refer to ISPE GAMP RDI GPG: Data Integrity by Design, Appendix S2.

Overview of CSV Efficiency Tools

GAMP 5 encourages the use of tools and automation but does not specify particular tools. The following is a classification of efficiency tools commonly used by CDMOs:

Section Summary

Chapter 7 establishes that while GxP compliance responsibility remains with the regulated company, supplier activities can be formally leveraged to reduce the regulated company's validation burden — provided the supplier is properly assessed. The 12-step good practices framework (Table 7.1) defines what a qualified supplier should deliver, from QMS establishment through retirement management.

Chapter 7 establishes that although GxP compliance responsibility always rests with the regulated company, supplier activities can be formally leveraged to reduce validation burden once a formal assessment has been completed. The 12-step good practices framework defines the full scope of services a qualified supplier should provide.

Chapter 8 provides 9 actionable efficiency strategies. The most impactful for resource-constrained CDMOs are: (1) leveraging supplier test evidence with documented justification; (2) eliminating unnecessary screenshots and test witnesses; (3) reusing existing validation documentation for similar systems; and (4) applying risk-based test depth rather than uniform comprehensive testing.

Chapter 8 provides 9 actionable efficiency strategies. The four most valuable for a resource-limited CDMO are: leveraging supplier test evidence (with a documented basis), eliminating unnecessary screenshots and witnessing, reusing validation documents from existing similar systems, and deciding test depth based on risk (rather than testing every item uniformly).

  • GAMP Category 3/4/5 determines the scope and depth of supplier involvement and regulated company verification obligations.
  • Sub-supplier management, especially for cloud-hosted GxP data, directly impacts regulated company compliance and must be transparent.
  • Risk registers should be searchable and reused across projects and change events — not created anew each time.
  • Commercial release (supplier activity) is entirely separate from GxP release to production (regulated company activity).
  • Data archiving and migration strategy should be designed at system implementation, not at retirement.

Section 3: App M1-M4: Validation Planning, Supplier Assessment, QRM & Software Categories

p85-p134

ISPE GAMP 5 Second Edition | p85 – p106

Learning Objectives

  1. Distinguish between a Validation Policy, Validation Master Plan (VMP), and a computerized system Validation Plan — and know when each is required.
  2. Define the mandatory content elements of a computerized system validation plan, including scope, risk management, validation strategy, deliverables, and acceptance criteria.
  3. Explain how to correctly scope a CSV validation project — which systems to include and what depth of testing is proportionate to risk.
  4. Describe the three types of supplier assessment (basic, postal/email audit, on-site/virtual audit) and select the appropriate method based on system impact and supplier risk.
  5. Apply the M2 supplier assessment process — from initial risk decision through audit, corrective actions, acceptance, and periodic re-evaluation.
  6. Identify red flags in supplier assessment results that indicate more in-house regulated company testing is required.

Appendix M1 — Validation Planning: Introduction & Scope

9.1 Introduction

Appendix M1 covers the production of individual validation plans for systems or projects (computerized systems validation plans), and also gives information on validation policies and Validation Master Plans (VMPs) for background and context.

Computerized system validation plans describe how the validation is to be performed for specific systems. Validation policies define management intent and commitment. VMPs describe the areas of the company where validation is required and provide an overview of validation planning for those areas.

It is a regulatory expectation that validation activities are planned — see for example EU Annex 11, Section 4.

Changes from GAMP 5 First Edition

  • Take into account the validation of SaaS solutions
  • Take into account the validation of systems developed in an incremental or iterative manner (Agile)
  • De-emphasize VMP, with the main focus remaining on computerized system validation planning

9.2 Scope

This guidance may be applied to all GxP regulated computerized systems — both new and existing systems, and sites and organizations in which these systems are used.

Where a computer system is regarded as one component of a wider manufacturing process or system — particularly in an integrated QbD environment — specific and separate computerized system validation may not be necessary, and separate computerized system validation plans would not be required. This environment requires both complete product and process understanding and that the CPPs can be accurately and reliably predicted and controlled over the design space.

For automated manufacturing equipment, separate computer system validation should be avoided. Computer system specification and verification should be part of an integrated engineering approach to ensure compliance and fitness for intended use of the complete automated equipment.

Commentary: Document Hierarchy

Three-Tier Document Structure

GAMP 5 divides CSV planning documents into three tiers. Many people mix them up, but the logic is actually quite clear:

  • Validation Policy: company level; defines the overall approach and management commitment. It is usually an SOP or corporate procedure explaining "how our company handles CSV".
  • Validation Master Plan (VMP): site or large-project level; lists all facilities, systems and processes in scope together with their validation status, schedule and change control approach. Inspectors often look at this document.
  • System Validation Plan (CSV Plan / SVP): single-system level; describes in detail how this specific system will be validated. This is the core topic of M1.

Analogy: Building a Factory

Think of the three documents as building a factory:

  • Validation Policy = the company's "construction standards manual"
  • VMP = the master plan for the whole site
  • System Validation Plan = the construction drawings for one particular building

Without drawings for each building, the plant cannot be built. Without a master plan, there is no coordination between the drawings.

An Important Shift in GAMP 5 Second Edition

The second edition explicitly de-emphasizes the VMP, which is a signal worth noting. Regulators increasingly care whether each system has been done properly, not whether you have a polished VMP document.

The inclusion of SaaS and Agile is also important: LIMS increasingly run as SaaS and MES vendors are adopting Agile development, so the traditional V-model validation plan needs to be adapted.

Scoping: Which Systems Need Their Own CSV?

GAMP states clearly that if a computerized system is "part of process equipment" (for example, the PLC of a filling machine), it should be integrated into equipment qualification, and should not be given a separate CSV.

A common CDMO mistake: writing a separate CSV plan for the controller of every autoclave, which creates an explosion of documents with no real benefit. The correct approach is to cover verification of the PLC software within the equipment IQ/OQ.

9.3 Computerized System Validation Plans — Content Requirements

9.3.1 General Guidelines

A computerized system validation plan should be produced for each GxP regulated computerized system focusing on aspects related to patient safety, product quality, and data integrity. It should summarize the system and/or project, identify measures for success, and clearly define criteria for final acceptance and release of the system.

The plan should define:

  • What activities are required
  • How they will be performed and who is responsible
  • What the output will be
  • What the requirements are for acceptance
  • How compliance will be maintained for the lifetime of the system

The level of detail in the plan should reflect the risk, complexity, and novelty of the system. For simple or low-risk systems a separate plan may not be needed — applicable aspects of planning may be covered within another document or process.

Planning should commence as early as possible, ideally no later than during the development of the initial user requirements.

The plan, along with the associated report, may be one of the first documents offered during an inspection or audit to demonstrate regulatory compliance. It should therefore be written at a level suitable to be understood by a wide readership. Jargon and technical detail should be avoided.

9.3.2 Roles and Responsibilities

Responsibility for computerized system validation planning ultimately rests with the process owner. This may be delegated to a project manager and also may involve the system owner.

Typically, the computerized system validation plan is approved by the process owner and quality unit.

Even though regulated companies cannot delegate their regulatory accountabilities to a supplier or service provider, they may leverage the knowledge, experience, and activities of the supplier or service provider through risk-based assessment, management, and governance processes.

In the case of SaaS, the plan should describe how the regulated company will leverage product specification and verification activities performed by the supplier as part of product development and performed under the supplier's QMS. The plan should describe the configuration specification and verification activities and user acceptance activities required to ensure fitness for intended use.

Commentary: The Nature of a Validation Plan

A Validation Plan Is a Strategy Document, Not a Test Script

Many people write the validation plan as an index of test scripts, which is a misunderstanding. The core function of a validation plan is to answer:

  • Where does this system's GxP risk lie?
  • What strategy will we use to confirm it is compliant and fit for intended use?
  • Who is responsible for what across the whole lifecycle?

Test scripts are execution-level documents and belong later in the OQ/PQ protocols.

The Inspector's Perspective

GAMP states clearly that the validation plan is the first document pulled out during an inspection. Inspectors use it to understand your overall strategy and then decide where to dig deeper.

So when writing a validation plan, ask yourself: "Could this document let an FDA inspector who is unfamiliar with this system understand our compliance strategy within 15 minutes?"

Special Handling for SaaS Systems

When using a SaaS LIMS (such as LabVantage Cloud or Veeva Vault), the supplier is responsible for validating most of the infrastructure and application. Your validation plan needs to explain:

  • How you will "leverage" the supplier's validation documents (SOC 2 report, IQ/OQ documents)
  • Which Configuration Qualification and UAT activities you will perform yourself
  • Your maintenance strategy when the supplier releases new versions

Regulatory responsibility always remains with you and cannot be passed to the supplier.

CDMO Practice: Who Approves the Validation Plan?

GAMP says: the Process Owner plus the Quality Unit (QU).

In actual CDMO operations, this is usually:

  • LIMS → QC manager (Process Owner) + QA
  • MES → Manufacturing manager + QA
  • ERP → IT/Finance manager + QA (for GxP-relevant modules)

The IT department is usually not the Process Owner; it acts as technical support. Clarifying this role is very important, otherwise the validation plan will never find anyone to take ownership.

9.3.3 Contents of the Computerized System Validation Plan

Required Content Sections

9.3.3.1 Introduction and Scope

  • The scope of the system
  • The objectives of the validation process
  • Applicable main regulations
  • Review, maintenance, or update process for the plan itself

9.3.3.2 System Overview

  • Business purpose and intended use for the system
  • A description of the system and its data at a high level
  • System scope and boundaries, and overview of the system architecture

Diagrams are encouraged. If the system is being developed using an incremental and iterative approach (Agile), information relevant to the initial release should be provided.

9.3.3.3 Organizational Structure

Roles and responsibilities should be described. Regulated company roles typically include:

  • Project manager — project management, planning, control of resources and costs, monitoring progress, reporting to senior management, liaising with the quality unit
  • Quality unit — ensuring compliance, reviewing and approving deliverables, approving the release of the system
  • Process owner / system owner — implementing and managing the system
  • SMEs — planning and defining verification strategies, performing reviews, defining acceptance criteria, selecting test methods, executing tests, reviewing results

The number of personnel required to approve specific documents should be kept to a minimum.

9.3.3.4 Quality Risk Management

An initial risk assessment should be performed based on an understanding of business processes, initial user requirements, regulatory requirements, and known functional areas. The results should include a GxP assessment decision and an overall assessment of system impact.

Large enterprise systems (e.g., ERP) may have some functionality declared GxP relevant while other functionality is outside the GxP scope. The method by which this decision is made should be described.

9.3.3.5 Validation Strategy

The strategy should be based on:

  • Risk assessment
  • Assessment of system components and architecture
  • Supplier assessment

The validation strategy should describe the life cycle model; specification and verification approach (linear, iterative, incremental, or Agile); inputs and outputs required for each stage; acceptance criteria; approach to traceability; approach to design review; and approach to leveraging supplier activities.

9.3.3.6 Deliverables

The deliverable items to be produced should be listed, including responsibility for production, review, and approval. Note that deliverables may include records within tools and supporting systems and are not constrained to traditional documentation.

9.3.3.7 Acceptance Criteria

The overall acceptance criteria for the system should be described. The approach to handling significant deviations should be defined. For Agile development, acceptance criteria are typically defined in the Minimum Viable Product (MVP) and Definition of Done (DoD).

9.3.3.8 Change Control

Requirements for project change control should be defined, including reference to relevant procedures and/or tools. The stage at which operational change control will be applied should be defined.

9.3.3.9 Standard Operating Procedures

The SOPs to be created or updated as a result of the implementation of the system should be defined, with responsibility for their production, review, and approval.

9.3.3.10 Supporting and Operational Processes

  • Training (including project team and user training)
  • Documentation, record, and knowledge management
  • Configuration management
  • Maintaining compliance and fitness for intended use
  • Operational processes

9.3.3.11 Glossary

Definitions of any terms and abbreviations that may be unfamiliar to the readership should be included.

Commentary: Practical Meaning of the Validation Plan Elements

The Challenge of Managing Multiple Systems in a CDMO

The GxP computerized systems a typical sterile manufacturing CDMO must manage include:

  • LIMS (Laboratory Information Management System)
  • MES (Manufacturing Execution System)
  • BMS (Building Management System — cleanroom differential pressure, temperature and humidity)
  • Autoclave controllers
  • Filling machine PLC
  • ERP (modules related to inventory and batch records)
  • Lyophilizer control system

Every system needs a validation plan, but the depth differs: LIMS and MES have the greatest impact and need full CSV documentation; the filling machine PLC can be integrated into equipment qualification.

Determining GxP Scope in ERP

ERP (such as SAP or Oracle) is a good example: the system is large, but not every module is GxP.

Modules that usually need CSV:

  • Batch record management / electronic batch records
  • Raw material inventory and release status management
  • Expiry date control

Modules that usually do not need CSV: procurement, finance, HR.

The validation plan must clearly state the rationale for this decision, rather than simply saying "the GxP functions of the ERP".

Recommended Contents of a CDMO Validation Plan Template

  1. System GxP impact assessment (high/medium/low) and the rationale
  2. System boundary diagram (which systems does it interface with? what are the data flows?)
  3. RASCI matrix (Responsible/Accountable/Supporting/Consulted/Informed)
  4. Summary of supplier assessment results (referencing the SA Report)
  5. Which supplier documents will be leveraged (IQ/OQ documents, development testing records)
  6. List of in-house validation activities (configuration specification, UAT, performance qualification)
  7. Ongoing compliance plan after go-live (annual review, change control)

9.4–9.5 Validation Policies & Validation Master Plans

9.4 Validation Policies

Regulated companies should have corporate or site-level policy documents that define their overall approach to computerized system quality and compliance. Such documents should define, or make reference to, the following:

  • Roles and responsibilities for activities and support
  • High-level expectations for deliverables
  • Standards, templates, and procedures expected to be followed throughout the organization
  • Definition of high-level processes, including the process to determine whether a system is GxP regulated
  • Requirements for record and knowledge management

These policies should be readily available to all those with responsibilities for verification and validation activities, and should be referred to by relevant planning documents.

9.5 Validation Master Plans

9.5.1 General Guidelines

A VMP may be used to define the overview plan for a given period of time, large project, or program of work under which there may be several individual validation plans. Computerized system validation is often a subset or part of a VMP covering all of an organization's validation activities.

The VMP should be a clear and concise summary document, typically covering:

  • Summary of facilities, systems, equipment, or processes in scope, and their respective validation status
  • Current status of these facilities, systems, equipment, or processes
  • Change-control process to be followed
  • Planning and scheduling (including activities for new systems, activities driven by change, and periodic review)

The VMP requires approval by management, and is often subject to regulatory inspection.

VMP Planning Hierarchy (Figure 9.1🔍)

Companies may structure VMPs hierarchically. Within a site, there may be:

  • VMP Level 1: Single VMP for the entire site
  • VMP Level 2: Separate plans for individual areas on the site
  • System-level plans: Detailed plans for specific systems within a given area

Companies may merge VMP Level 1 and Level 2 into a single plan, or operate with a collection of Level 2 VMPs and not collate them into higher-level plans.

9.5.2 Roles and Responsibilities for VMPs

Responsibility for creating VMPs rests with senior management. Regardless of who prepares a VMP, senior-management support is essential to ensure adequate resources for the required activities. Facility or area management should approve VMPs. The quality unit should approve the policies and procedures for validation.

9.5.3 Contents of the VMP

The VMP should be a summary document that is brief, concise, and clear. It should cover:

  • Scope
  • Reference to relevant policies
  • Organizational structure
  • Summary of facilities, systems, equipment, and processes
  • Record types, content, availability, and retention
  • Planning and scheduling
  • Change control
  • Reference to existing documents

Commentary: VMP vs System Validation Plan

Positioning and Practical Value of the VMP

The greatest use of a VMP is actually to present the full picture of compliance externally: an inspector can understand the validation scope and status of the entire site from a single document.

Internally, the practical functions of the VMP are to:

  • Let management see the validation resource needs (money, people, time)
  • Provide a "dashboard" of the validation status of all systems
  • Ensure consistent standards across validation activities in different departments

Two Common VMP Mistakes

Mistake 1: The VMP is too detailed
The VMP is a summary document, not a validation guideline. If your VMP exceeds 50 pages, you have probably packed the content of the system validation plans into it.

Mistake 2: The VMP is out of step with reality
The VMP lists 20 systems requiring CSV, but 10 have not been done. In an inspection this is a red flag. The VMP should reflect the current situation honestly and explain the plan.

Signal from GAMP 5 Second Edition: The VMP Is Downgraded

The second edition explicitly "de-emphasizes the VMP", which reflects a shift in regulatory practice:

FDA and EMA inspectors increasingly prefer to go directly to system-level validation documents rather than start from the VMP. What they want to see is: "For this specific system, is your URS → IQ/OQ/PQ → annual review chain complete?"

If the VMP is not kept up to date (many companies have not touched theirs for two or three years), it actually creates a negative impression during an inspection.

CDMO Recommendation: A Streamlined VMP Strategy

For small and mid-sized CDMOs, we recommend a "one-tier VMP plus system index" strategy:

  1. One site VMP (about 10-15 pages): describes the validation policy, scope, organization and change control principles
  2. CSV system inventory (as an appendix): lists all GxP systems, their validation status and next review date
  3. A validation plan for each system (maintained separately)

Update the CSV system inventory every year; revise the VMP itself only when there is a major policy change. This is more realistic and more effective than maintaining a huge VMP.

Appendix M2 — Supplier Assessment: Introduction & Rationale

10.1 Introduction

Appendix M2 provides a risk-based approach to performing supplier assessments. Regulated companies should consider formally assessing each supplier of GxP regulated computerized systems and services. The assessment approach should be based upon the application of critical thinking to understand the risk to patient safety, product quality, and data integrity.

Topics covered in M2:

  • The reasons for carrying out supplier assessments
  • The different types of assessment
  • The assessment process
  • Postal/email audits and on-site/virtual online audits
  • Joint and shared audits
  • Corporate audits
  • Supplier preparation for an audit
  • Supplier certification
  • International standards and certification

The term audit in this appendix covers both a physical or virtual online visit to the supplier and a formal assessment using a questionnaire (known as a postal or email audit).

M2 covers assessments of both prospective and existing suppliers of computerized services and systems, including cloud (XaaS) service providers. It may also be used to assess external service providers (validation, project management, engineering support, maintenance) and internal functions such as IT and engineering.

10.2 Reasons for Supplier Assessment

Regulated companies require a high level of confidence that computerized systems will meet their technical, commercial, and regulatory requirements. They also wish to leverage the knowledge, experience, and documentation of the supplier.

The computerized system supplier should build quality and integrity into a software product during development, as it cannot be added effectively (e.g., through testing and rework) later by the regulated company. The supplier is also better positioned to produce most of the required information during the development process.

Supplier assessments are also an opportunity to develop relationships with suppliers, to clarify expectations and intentions, and to identify misunderstandings and risks. The assessment report should provide a balanced view of what was found, including positive observations, along with a list of concerns.

Consideration should be given to adopting a broader approach that covers multiple products/services from a supplier to avoid multiple assessments — particularly important for larger, globally regulated companies.

Commentary: Why Assess Suppliers?

Core Logic: You Cannot Fix Quality After Delivery

The most critical sentence in GAMP: "The supplier should build quality and integrity into a software product during development, as it cannot be added effectively later by the regulated company."

This is the fundamental reason supplier assessment exists: before engaging a supplier you must confirm that their development process is reliable, because you cannot "add quality back" once you have received the software.

Analogy: Purchasing a Pharmaceutical Raw Material

It is like buying an API raw material: you can run some quality control tests, but if the supplier's manufacturing process itself has problems, your sampling tests alone cannot catch every issue.

That is why you perform vendor qualification, to ensure their facility and processes comply with GMP. The logic of CSV supplier assessment is exactly the same.

Three New Considerations in GAMP 5 Second Edition

  1. XaaS/Cloud suppliers: SaaS suppliers may not accept a traditional postal audit, so you need to rely on certifications (SOC 2+, ISO 27001).
  2. Agile development tools: suppliers use Jira or Azure DevOps to manage requirements and test records, and the artifacts in these tools can serve as assessment evidence, not just traditional paper documents.
  3. Data integrity: the assessment must cover how the supplier ensures data integrity, including audit trails and access control.

CDMO Practice: A Compliance Strategy for "Leveraging Supplier Documents"

The greatest practical benefit of supplier assessment is that it lets you reduce your own validation testing.

If the supplier's SDQ (Software Development Qualification) documents are of good quality and their IQ/OQ testing is very thorough, you can complete validation using these documents plus site-specific UAT, rather than performing all functional testing from scratch.

But this reduction must be based on the assessment results, and the rationale must be explained in your validation plan.

10.3–10.4 Types of Assessment & The Assessment Process

10.3 Types of Assessment

There are three main options for performing a supplier assessment:

  1. Basic Assessment — based on available information (for components considered as commodities, e.g., common desktop applications, a documented decision not to perform any assessment may be appropriate)
  2. Postal/Email Audit — using a questionnaire; can also be performed via email
  3. On-Site Audit (or Virtual Online Audit) — by relevant specialist, auditor, or audit team

Typically, a basic assessment is sufficient for lower-impact systems, while higher-impact systems may require formal audits. Postal audits may be appropriate for suppliers of standard and configurable products and services.

Note: Virtual online audits should be considered as a subset of on-site audits and performed to the same level and detail as on-site audits.

10.4 Assessment Process

The assessment process should be completed before any contract is finalized and the service commenced.

The main steps of the overall assessment process (Figure 10.1🔍):

  1. A risk-based decision on the most appropriate assessment route
  2. Perform a basic assessment if sufficient; otherwise, perform a postal/email audit or an on-site/virtual online audit
  3. Document assessment or produce audit report
  4. Determine and monitor corrective actions and document completion (could involve follow-up audit)
  5. Accept or reject the supplier

Once suppliers have been accepted, they may be subject to periodic re-evaluation at a frequency specified in SOPs. Regulated companies normally maintain a supplier audit schedule indicating which suppliers have been audited, when, and the reason.

10.4.1 Need for Audit

A decision on the need for an audit should be taken based on the results of the initial risk assessment and system impact, considering novelty and complexity, and the categorization of components (see Appendix M4).

10.4.2 Basic Assessment

A basic assessment may be based on:

  • A review of public domain information, including information regarding certification
  • Market reputation
  • Knowledge and experience of prior performance
  • Discussions with other regulated companies

The results of the assessment should be documented. For components that are considered as commodities, a documented decision not to perform any assessment may be appropriate.

Commentary: Logic for Choosing Among the Three Assessment Methods

Core Principle: Complete the Assessment Before the Contract

GAMP states clearly: "assessment should be completed before any contract is finalized".

In practice, many companies sign the contract first and only perform the supplier assessment after the system has gone live, which is wrong from a regulatory standpoint. The purpose of assessment is to help you make the purchasing decision, not to backfill documents afterwards.

If you have already adopted a supplier that was not assessed, the remedy is to perform a "retrospective assessment" and explain in the validation plan why no assessment was done beforehand.

Common Misuse of the Basic Assessment

The Basic Assessment is suitable only for commodity software, such as Microsoft Excel or the Windows OS.

A common error is to downgrade a Postal Audit to a Basic Assessment on the grounds that "this software is well known" or "everyone in the industry uses it". Being well known does not equal GxP quality; Oracle EBS or SAP also need a formal assessment.

Recommendations for Managing the Supplier Audit Schedule

We recommend maintaining a "Supplier Audit Schedule" that includes:

  • Supplier name and product/service
  • Date and method of the last assessment
  • Assessment result (accepted / conditionally accepted / rejected)
  • Open corrective actions
  • Planned date and method of the next assessment
  • Responsible auditor

This list is very useful in an inspection, because it lets you quickly demonstrate that you have control over all your suppliers.

10.4.3 Postal/Email Audits

Purpose and Applicability

There is a significant cost to both regulated companies and suppliers associated with conducting audits at the supplier's premises. A postal/email audit provides a mechanism for reducing these costs.

A postal/email audit may be used as:

  • A part of the tendering process to determine if a supplier merits further consideration (shortlisting)
  • A preliminary audit to focus efforts in critical areas during a subsequent on-site audit
  • A broad audit to determine whether the system/service is a mature, trustworthy product that does not require an on-site audit
  • A follow-up audit for suppliers that have passed an on-site audit with outstanding corrective actions
  • A means of periodically reassessing a supplier providing ongoing service
  • A means of auditing other premises of the supplier where the same QMS is implemented

Postal/Email Audit Questionnaire Content

Questionnaires typically include:

  • Company overview including product-specific locations
  • Organization, roles and responsibilities, staff training and experience
  • Key products and/or service history and development plans
  • QMS implementation at company level and for product-related processes
  • Product/project management
  • Software development life cycle processes and deliverables
  • Software development life cycle support processes
  • Service delivery processes
  • User training
  • Product support/maintenance
  • IT infrastructure management and control
  • Security
  • Use of subcontractors

The assessment of software products will normally be version specific — the questionnaire should be drawn up accordingly.

Postal/Email Audit Evidence

The value of any postal/email audit will be limited or even irrelevant if there is no evidence supplied to support the completed questionnaire. The postal/email audit questionnaire should therefore request supporting evidence wherever possible, including real examples of the work performed.

If during review of the supplier's response problems or concerns are found, the postal/email audit process may be terminated and an on-site/virtual online audit scheduled and conducted.

Commentary: Practical Points on Postal Audits

The Many Uses of a Postal Audit

A postal audit is flexible and can play different roles:

  • Pre-purchase screening: when evaluating 3 LIMS vendors, send the postal audit questionnaire first and narrow the field to 1-2 vendors based on the responses before conducting an on-site audit
  • Periodic re-evaluation of existing suppliers: for vendors you have worked with for years, a postal audit can replace an expensive on-site audit for the re-evaluation every 2-3 years
  • Corrective action follow-up: if an on-site audit resulted in CAPAs, a postal audit can be used to confirm completion

The Biggest Pitfall of Postal Audits: No Supporting Evidence

Many suppliers write polished answers, but if no actual documentary evidence (actual artifacts) is attached, the value of the questionnaire is close to zero.

Ask the supplier to provide:

  • Examples of actual test plans (not templates, but ones that were really executed)
  • Examples of change control records
  • The current version of the software quality plan
  • Statistics from customer issue/defect records (may be anonymized)

The Importance of Version Specificity

GAMP specifically reminds us that software assessment is "version-specific".

An assessment you performed on LIMS v5.2 cannot automatically be applied to v6.0. A major version upgrade requires re-assessment (at minimum, an updated assessment report explaining the scope of impact of the upgrade).

This is especially important for SaaS suppliers, who may release updates every quarter; you should require in the contract that the supplier notifies you of major version changes and provides updated validation support documents.

Triggers for Escalating a Postal Audit to On-Site

Under what circumstances should a postal audit be escalated to an on-site audit?

  • The supplier's answers to critical questions are vague
  • The documents provided are clearly incomplete or contradictory
  • The supplier refuses to provide supporting documents
  • There is negative feedback in the market (complaints, FDA warning letter records)
  • The system impact assessment is raised (high-risk system)

GAMP says such an escalation is entirely reasonable, and the final on-site report should explain why the approach changed from postal to on-site.

10.4.4–10.4.8 On-Site/Virtual Online Audits

10.4.4 Preparation and Organization

10.4.4.2 Scope of the Audit

The audit scope is determined by the purpose of the audit and the supplier's main activities:

  • Detailed audits: Cover all aspects relating to the product or service under consideration. Also used to assess the supplier's capability to produce a quality product when custom services are being sought.
  • Follow-up audits: Concentrate on specific areas of concern identified during previous audits, or the progress made on agreed corrective actions.
  • Surveillance audits: Focus on areas of weakness found during previous audits, on new products and services; provide a vehicle for monitoring ongoing compliance.

10.4.4.3 Select Audit Team

On-site audits of complex systems may require a team of at least two: a lead auditor plus a system user and/or a technical specialist. A lead auditor should have formal auditing qualifications and experience in computerized system development and their use in a regulated environment.

10.4.4.4 Supplier Notification

Audit details should be confirmed in writing: reason, objective, scope, location (on-site or virtual online), timing, and audit team details. A provisional agenda should also be provided.

10.4.5 Performing the Audit

An on-site/virtual online audit has three parts:

  1. Opening meeting: Formal introductions; lead auditor summarizes purpose and scope; agenda confirmed.
  2. Review and inspection: The audit team examines the supplier's practices and records using a "show me" (evidence-based) approach. Auditors interview designers and operatives as well as management. Issues of potential concern should be raised with the supplier when found.
  3. Closing meeting: Lead auditor lists observations (both positive and areas of concern); supplier representatives respond; next steps explained.

10.4.6 Audit Report

The audit report is a quality record that:

  • Provides a formal record of the audit and its findings
  • Acts as a major input when determining corrective action
  • Provides objective evidence to support the selection or continued use of a supplier

The audit report normally contains: Introduction; Scope; Organization and agenda; Detailed findings; Record of closing meeting; Conclusions.

10.4.7 Supplier Acceptance and Rejection

Based on the audit outcome, the regulated company may decide:

  • To use the supplier unconditionally
  • To use the supplier for certain products or versions only
  • To use the supplier subject to specific corrective actions being addressed
  • To provide additional regulated company supervision and/or conduct additional testing to overcome shortfalls
  • To agree with the supplier on the application of a documented QMS for the purposes of the contract
  • To prohibit the use of the supplier

10.4.8 Corrective Actions and Follow-up Audits

If corrective actions are required, the regulated company should follow up and obtain documentary evidence of successful completion. Evidence could include copies of new procedures, testing records, design review, and code review documents. A letter of confirmation from the supplier is not normally sufficient.

10.4.9 Re-Evaluation

Once accepted, suppliers should be subject to periodic re-evaluation. The decision on whether to reaudit can be influenced by:

  • The criticality of the product/service provided
  • Change of supplier ownership (acquisitions/mergers)
  • Changes in the supplier management structure
  • Changes to the QMS
  • Change of license model (e.g., closed source to open-source or freeware)
  • Change of delivery model (e.g., on-premise to cloud)

Commentary: Strategy and Techniques for On-Site Audits

The "Show Me" Principle

GAMP states clearly that auditors should take a "show me" approach: verbal explanations are not accepted; you need to see the actual documents and records.

Typical exchanges during an audit:

  • "Do you perform code reviews?" → "Yes" → "Please show me the code review records for the last three sprints"
  • "Do you track defects?" → "Yes, in Jira" → "Please show me the complete tracking record of one resolved critical bug"

A verbal claim without supporting documents has zero value in the audit record.

Five Levels of Supplier Acceptance Decision

GAMP gives five possible acceptance outcomes, forming a graduated decision scale:

  1. Unconditional acceptance: the ideal outcome; the supplier meets expectations in all respects
  2. Partial / version-specific acceptance: certain functions or versions are acceptable, others are not
  3. Conditional acceptance + CAPA: there are problems but they can be fixed, with a deadline set
  4. Acceptance + increased in-house testing: the supplier has gaps, but you compensate with your own testing (highest cost)
  5. Rejection: the problems are too serious for the system to be used

The fourth option (increased in-house testing) is the most commonly underestimated: when you choose it, you are using your own validation resources to make up for the supplier's shortcomings. It must be stated explicitly in the validation plan, with the additional testing effort estimated.

Red Flags: When Is Increased In-House Testing Needed?

Any of the following is a red flag indicating that the amount of in-house testing should be increased:

  • The supplier has no formal SDLC documentation
  • Test records are incomplete or cannot be found
  • There is no traceability from requirements to tests
  • QA involvement in the development/testing process is very low
  • Version history shows a high rate of "emergency patches" (hotfixes)
  • The supplier refuses to show actual development process records
