Compliance expectations must be broken down into verifiable functional requirements so that each can be tested in OQ/PQ.

D1.3.3 Contents of the Requirements Specification

RS Structure: Key Sections

Introduction

The introduction should provide information on: who produced the document, under what authority, and for what purpose; contractual status; relationship to other documents; high-level description including breakdown of primary components; and assumptions/restrictions. A data flow diagram should be referenced or created.

Overview

An overview should explain why the system is required, the essential system functions, interfaces, and what is required. It should address: background, impact on patient safety, product quality, and data integrity; scope including system limits and boundaries, key objectives, applicable GxP requirements, and other applicable regulations.

Operational Requirements — Functions

Functional requirements should be broken down to the level of individual functions using a recursive hierarchy. The following should be specified as appropriate:

  • Objective of the function, details of use, inputs, outputs, algorithms
  • Calculations, including all critical algorithms and data entry/edit checks that support CQAs
  • Functions that inherently need to be configurable and any limits to configuration
  • Performance: response, sizing, throughput (quantitative and unambiguous)
  • System safety including action in case of software or hardware failures
  • Security including access control
  • Audit trails
  • Use of electronic signatures
  • Output (e.g., reports, files)
  • Error conditions, unambiguous error messages, failure actions

Data Requirements

Data-handling requirements should be documented based on the impact to patient safety, product quality, and data integrity. Topics include: definition of records and data, primary record specification, critical data definition, data-validation checks, data migration, backup and recovery, data retention, and data security/integrity.

Technical Requirements

System technical requirements should address: changes in system operation (start-up, shutdown, failover), disaster recovery, performance and timing requirements, minimum capacity requirements, hardware requirements, portability, availability, and configurability.

Interface Requirements

System interfaces should describe how systems or sub-systems interact. Key interface topics include: data transmitted and received, data type/format/ranges, timing, rates of data transfer, communications protocol, data sharing/storage/destruction, mechanisms for initiation and interruption, error handling, and access/security.

Nonfunctional Attributes & Environment

Nonfunctional attributes include availability (disaster recovery, backup/restore, RTO/RPO) and maintainability (expansion possibilities, cloud storage elasticity, lifetime). Environment requirements address physical layout, physical conditions (temperature, humidity, EMI, dust), physical security, and power requirements.

Constraints

System constraints include: compatibility with existing systems, regulated company strategy, availability, reliability requirements, maximum allowable downtime, statutory obligations, working methods, user skill levels, expansion capability, expected lifetime, and long-term support.

Life Cycle Requirements

Life cycle requirements include: development standards required of the supplier, quality assurance procedures, mandatory design methods, special testing requirements, test data, load testing, acceptance testing, information supplier must make available on regulatory inspection, training courses, archiving facilities, and support after acceptance.

What Does NOT Belong in the RS

The following should not be included in the RS:

  • System configuration/design details
  • Implementation details
  • Project deadlines
  • Cost
  • Project organizational details

Commentary: Structure of the RS

The core question of the RS: "What does the system need to do?"

The RS (Requirements Specification) answers only one question: What does the system need to do? It does not answer "how." This boundary is critical:

  • The RS says: "The system shall automatically send a QC review notification to the designated reviewer within 24 hours of batch production completion."
  • The CS/DS says: "Use the SMTP service via Exchange Server to send an HTML-format notification email, triggered when the batch status field changes to 'Released'."

Common CDMO pitfall: design details in the RS

Many CDMO URSs say: "The system shall use Oracle Database 18c as the back-end database." This is a design detail, not a requirement. The correct requirement would be: "The system database shall support concurrent multi-user access with a response time of no more than 2 seconds, and shall provide a complete backup and restore mechanism."

The problem with specifying a particular technology: what if the supplier does not use Oracle? Your requirement immediately excludes every other qualified supplier, and the restriction has no business justification.

Audit trail requirements: a concrete example

"Audit trail" is often requested only vaguely in an RS. A well-formed data requirement should be written like this:

  • Which fields need an audit trail (all of them? only GMP-critical ones?)
  • The audit trail record shall include: original value, new value, ID of the person making the change, timestamp (UTC + time zone), and reason for change (mandatory entry)
  • The audit trail record shall not be modifiable or deletable by any user (including system administrators)
  • The audit trail shall be reviewable independently by QA, without technical support

All four of these sub-items can be verified one by one in OQ testing.

CDMO LIMS requirements classification exercise

Which requirement category should each of the following items fall into?

D1.3.2 & D1.4 — Ownership and Requirements Capture

Ownership

Ownership of business requirements lies with the business process owner of the regulated company. Without user ownership, the business operational needs and any associated issues can never be fully understood and captured. Defined requirements form the basis for acceptance of the system by users.

Software or service providers may maintain a separate set of requirements that are more technical in nature. In Agile approaches, these requirements are often owned by the product owner. SMEs, including those from third parties, may help both the user and technical communities analyze and understand the operational needs.

Requirements Capture

For Category 4 and 5 systems, requirements capture is often the most difficult and time-consuming aspect of producing an RS. A suitably experienced individual should be identified and made responsible for managing the requirements-capture process.

Discussions and Interviews

Participants should include process and system owners, business process participants and users, and SMEs. Key principles:

  • Ask open-ended questions so requirements can be investigated
  • Determine the participant's actual involvement in the process to be automated
  • Ask participants to identify weaknesses of the existing process
  • Use terms familiar to participants; avoid technical jargon
  • Avoid proposing solutions — proposing supplier-specific solutions stifles consideration of actual needs
  • Examine issue logs from existing systems being replaced

Observation

The business function should be observed, noting that "what is done today may not be the best solution for tomorrow." Caution should be exercised regarding designing a new system to duplicate the current business process — the current process may not be the best way to meet all requirements.

Workflow Analysis

Use cases describe the interactions between a system and hardware/software/equipment and people outside the system. Use cases aid in: gathering requirements, developing SOPs, developing training materials, writing test scripts, and designing a system.

Workshops

Workshops usually involve multifunctional meetings. All affected user groups should be represented while keeping the size manageable. Participants should focus on their area of expertise and be empowered to make decisions for that area.

Requirements Planning Pitfalls

  • A common understanding among team members should be established
  • All required levels of the business should be involved
  • Ambiguous requirements should be avoided
  • Requirements should be classified to ensure appropriate focus on critical requirements
  • Functionality that will not be used should be avoided
  • The original scope should be maintained; extending scope only through formal change control
  • Multiple requirements within a single-requirement statement should be avoided

Commentary: Requirements Capture in Practice

The business process owner: the most important person

GAMP 5 stresses that ownership of requirements belongs to the business process owner, not to the IT department or the supplier. In a CDMO setting:

  • LIMS requirements → QC manager or QA manager
  • EBR/MES requirements → production manager or process development
  • Environmental monitoring system → facilities engineering + QA

The role of IT is that of technical adviser, ensuring that requirements are technically feasible, but IT should not drive the content of the business requirements.

"Today's process is not the best solution"

This is the point most easily overlooked in requirements capture. Many projects ask in interviews "How do you do it now?" and then convert the existing paper-based process directly into system requirements.

But the current process may be full of manual steps that exist only because "there was no system before." Implementing a LIMS is an opportunity to redesign the process, not to digitize bad habits.

A good question is: "If you could design this process from scratch, how would you do it?" rather than "How do you do it now?"

Common CDMO pitfall: scope creep

The most common problem in the requirements capture phase is scope creep. Every stakeholder wants to add their own "nice to have" to the RS. Ways to prevent it:

  • Mark each requirement with a priority: Mandatory / Beneficial / Nice to have
  • After the RS is approved, any new requirement must go through formal change control
  • Record which stakeholder raised which requirement (traceability)
  • Conduct periodic RS reviews to confirm that no unapproved requirements have been quietly added

Appendix D2 — Functional Specifications (Retired)

Appendix D2 — Retired

Appendix D2: Functional Specifications has been retired in GAMP 5 Second Edition.

The content has been incorporated into Appendix D1 — Specifying Requirements. Functional design specifications for custom systems can be prepared if needed and are covered in Appendix D3 — Configuration and Design.

Commentary: What the Retirement of D2 Means

Why was the separate FRS appendix abolished?

The first edition of GAMP 5 treated the URS and FRS as two separate appendices. In practice this caused a problem: many companies could not tell whether a requirement belonged in the URS or the FRS, leading to duplicated documents, requirements scattered across two documents, and difficult maintenance.

The second edition takes a more practical approach: requirements are placed together in the RS, and as long as the structure is clear (separate sections for business requirements, functional requirements, data requirements, technical requirements, and so on), it can serve all purposes.

Practical impact for CDMOs: if your current SOP requires producing both a URS and an FRS, consider merging them into a single well-structured RS. However, if validated systems already use the old format, there is no need to force an upgrade - regulators care more about whether the requirements are complete and traceable than about what the document is called.

Appendix D3 — Configuration and Design: Introduction & Overview

D3.1 Introduction

This appendix provides guidance for defining the required configuration of system components and for system design. Based upon the type of system (configurable or custom, on-premise or SaaS), Configuration and Design Specifications (CS and DS) provide a detailed, technical expansion of the RS. They explain how the system will do what is defined in the RS.

D3.2 Scope

This appendix applies to the production of all CS/DS. Separate documents may not always be needed to adequately define configuration and design aspects. A hierarchy of specifications may be required for larger more complex systems, while specifications may be combined for smaller, simpler systems or systems classified as low risk. A system may be used to record and track configuration and design information including required approvals.

D3.3.1 Overview of Configuration and Design

Configuration

Configuration specifications should be provided for configured products and cover the appropriate configuration of the software products that comprise the system to meet specified requirements. This includes the definition of all settings and parameters supporting the intended business process.

It may be possible to maintain configuration information electronically in systems with robust configuration management (e.g., audit trails). Such an approach should be clearly documented.

Design

Custom applications require design of hardware and software, and also may require CS.

  • Hardware design defines the hardware components of a system, e.g., system or component architecture, or interfaces.
  • Software design occurs at two levels: at the higher level it defines the software modules (sub-systems), interfaces between modules, and interfaces to external systems including data flow. At the lower level it describes the operation of the individual software modules. These specifications should be unambiguous, clear, and precise.

D3.3.2 General Guidelines

The use of tables and diagrams to illustrate CS and DS is highly recommended. Standardized tables help ensure all relevant parameters and settings have been defined. Diagrams can clarify data flow, control logic, data structures, interfaces, and mapping to corresponding business processes.

Configuration and design should cover both hardware and software aspects. Each specification in a tier should be uniquely referenced and traceable back to its appropriate higher-level specification.

Where a system is hosted by a service provider, the CS/DS are typically produced and maintained by the service provider. The regulated company should leverage the service provider effort and where necessary produce a supplementary specification based upon the system configurations required to support the intended business process. Oversight should be maintained through a quality agreement.

All specifications should be structured in a way that supports traceability through the life cycle from individual requirements to associated testing.

Commentary: CS vs. DS - COTS and Custom Software

Configuration Specification (CS) vs. Design Specification (DS)

This is the core distinction in Appendix D3:

  • Configuration Specification (CS): used for existing commercial off-the-shelf software (COTS) or configurable products. You do not modify the source code; you only set parameters. The CS records "what value I set this setting to, and why."
  • Design Specification (DS): used for custom software development. You design the modules, define the data structures and write the logic. The DS records "how the software was built."

Analogy: LIMS configuration vs. custom system design

COTS LIMS (for example LabWare): no DS is needed. What you need is a CS recording: which fields are enabled for which sample types? Which workflows have electronic signatures enabled? How many days is the audit trail retention set to?

Custom-developed MES module: a DS is needed, detailing the database schema of the batch record, the logic flow diagram of each functional module, and the API interface definitions between modules.

Key point: if you cannot modify the software's source code (COTS), you do not need a DS - the design of the software is the supplier's responsibility, and your responsibility is to configure it correctly and verify that the configuration meets your requirements.

CDMO pitfall: over-specifying requirements for COTS systems

This is a common problem when CDMOs procure a standard LIMS: the RS contains very detailed configuration details, for example "the audit trail screen shall have a specific field order."

The problem is that the UI of COTS software is fixed and you cannot change it. If the RS demands a configuration that is impossible to achieve, either your RS can never be met, or you will have to keep justifying deviations.

The correct approach: the RS describes the business requirement (the audit trail can be queried), the CS records the actual configuration the system provides (the audit trail is in the Admin > Reports > Audit Trail module), and confirms that it meets the business requirement.

Special considerations for SaaS / cloud systems

When using a cloud SaaS system (for example Veeva Vault QMS):

  • The CS/DS is usually maintained by the service provider
  • The CDMO should ask the supplier for system configuration documentation and make this an explicit requirement in the quality agreement
  • The CDMO needs to produce a "supplementary configuration specification" recording the customer-side configuration you make in the SaaS system (such as workflows, role permissions and custom fields)
  • The supplier is responsible for qualification (IQ) of its infrastructure, but you need to confirm that it holds appropriate SOC 2 or equivalent certification

D3.3.3 Configuration Specification Content

Configuration Specification

The required configuration of components to be provided as all or part of the solution should be defined. This includes but is not limited to:

  • Required configuration settings or parameters
  • Reason for setting, with reference to controlling specification (i.e., the RS requirement it fulfills)
  • Tools or methods that will be used to set the required options
  • Dependencies and impacts on other modules or systems
  • Infrastructure items such as operating systems and layered software
  • Security of settings

For simple systems it may be possible to incorporate this information into the RS.

Configuration Introduction Section

The introduction to a CS/DS should contain:

  • Ownership of the document
  • Who produced the document, under what authority, and for what purpose
  • The contractual status of the document (if applicable)
  • Relationship to other documents (RS, other configuration or design specifications, etc.)

Configuration Overview Section

The overview should briefly describe the configuration and/or design as defined in the document, including the storage and in some cases, location of the records and the involved systems. The overview may be illustrated using diagrams. The overview should not contain detailed design information.

Commentary: Key Elements of the CS

Configuration management: settings must not be changed casually

Once the system has been validated (an approved Configuration Baseline), any configuration change must follow the change control procedure, with the impact reassessed and revalidation performed.

A common problem: an IT administrator changes a system parameter "while at it" during maintenance (for example changing the session timeout from 30 minutes to 120 minutes) without going through change control. This is a major finding in an audit, because the Validated State has been compromised.

The most important field in a CS: "why it is set this way"

A good Configuration Specification records not only "what the setting value is" but also "why it is set this way," linked to the corresponding requirement ID in the RS.

For example:

This traceability structure lets validation testers, when writing OQ/PQ test cases, map directly to the configuration in the CS and then to the requirement in the RS.

D3.3.4 Hardware Design Specification

Hardware Design

Hardware Design Specifications (DS) are required for custom applications. The overall architecture of the hardware required should be defined. At a high level this may be illustrated by an annotated block diagram.

Computer System Architecture

The following should be covered as appropriate:

  • Main computer system: primary hardware components (CPU, memory, bus type, clock accuracy) or Virtual Machine (VM) components (VM software, processing unit, memory, elasticity of setup)
  • Storage including location of data: all proposed storage devices with maximum storage capacities (hard disk, cloud storage)
  • Peripherals: including sensors, wearables, mobile devices, and BYOD setups
  • Interconnections/networks: all interconnections of hardware components and connections to external equipment, including cable specifications, connector specifications, network connections, wireless connections, and bandwidth capabilities
  • Configuration: DIP switch settings, device addresses, pin assignments, encryption, mobile device lockdown
  • Embedded systems (within process equipment): layout diagrams, location diagrams for sensors, electrical wiring diagrams, P&ID drawings

Inputs and Outputs

Input and output formats should be specified where necessary. For external equipment: accuracy, isolation, range of current and voltage, type and numbers of interface cards, timing, and encryption and security.

Environment & Electrical Supplies

The operating environment for hardware should define: temperature, humidity, external interference, physical security, shielding against RF/EMI/UV, hardening against dust or vibration, and location. Electrical supply requirements should address: filtering, loading, grounding protection, UPS, and power consumption/heat emission for HVAC capacity calculations.

Commentary: Hardware Design Specification

Which systems need a hardware DS?

Not every computerized system needs a hardware DS. The following is a guide for deciding:

  • HDS needed: custom embedded control systems (such as a filling machine PLC control panel), custom hardware integration (such as a special sensor interface), complex network architecture design
  • HDS usually not needed: standard COTS software running on supplier-provided servers, SaaS systems (hardware managed by the cloud provider), standard PC workstations running a LIMS client

CDMO practice: environmental condition requirements

In a sterile pharmaceutical CDMO environment, hardware design needs particular attention to:

  • Touchscreens in cleanrooms: IP protection rating (usually IP65+ is required) and easy-to-clean surface design
  • Electromagnetic interference (EMI): the motors of filling equipment may affect nearby PLC signals, so appropriate shielding is required
  • UPS requirements: a batch record system must have enough time to finish saving data during a power outage
  • Embedded systems (filling machines): hardware design documents (P&ID, electrical drawings) must exist before IQ can be performed

D3.3.5 Software Design Specification

Software Design

Software should be designed in accordance with recognized design standards where appropriate. Design Specifications covering software design are required for custom applications. This is not normally required for configurable products, where software design is normally reviewed or evaluated as part of the supplier assessment and is often proprietary.

Software Description

The modules that will form the system should be described, briefly stating the purpose of each. A list of all interfaces between modules, and any interfaces to external systems should be given. A system diagram is recommended. For SaaS the delivery model (e.g., single-instance-multitenant) should be described.

System Data

System data and the major data objects should be defined. Data should be characterized in a hierarchical manner with complex objects built up of simpler objects:

  • Instances and tenants
  • Databases and collections of files
  • Files / Records

Data objects should include: data types, data format, data precision, and data accuracy. Each file and data structure should be uniquely identified. The use of formal data description methods such as Entity Relationship Models should be considered. In multitenant environments, the method of data separation and security should be described.

Module Description

For each software module:

  • Module operation: may take the form of pseudo code or a flow chart
  • Interfaces to other modules
  • Error handling and data checking
  • Data mapping to each module
  • Data load and module performance aspects

For each sub-program:

  • Sub-program operation (pseudo code or flow chart)
  • The steps and inputs/outputs from each step
  • Parameters: input/output/input-output, pass by value/reference
  • Algorithms, side effects, language including version
  • Reference to programming standards
  • Description of all display screens and all implemented reports

Commentary: Software Design Specification and Traceability

Traceability: from requirements to design to testing

The DS is not just a technical document; it is the skeleton of the validation plan. The structure of a complete traceability matrix:

RS requirement ID → CS/DS design section → validation test case ID → test result

For example:

  • RS-SEC-003 (minimum password length of 8 characters) → CS section 4.2 (password policy configuration) → OQ-TC-015 (password length verification test) → PASS
  • RS-AUDIT-007 (audit trail for batch data modification records) → DS module 3.4 (audit trail engine design) → OQ-TC-042 (audit trail integrity test) → PASS

Without a traceability matrix, validation testing is just "what was tested," rather than "proof of which requirements the system meets."

Design review: more than a document review

Appendix M5 covers design review in more detail. But the core message in D3 is that design review is the key step for ensuring the CS/DS is correct.

Who needs to take part in the design review?

  • System Owner
  • Business Process Owner
  • IT representative (confirms technical feasibility)
  • QA representative (confirms GxP compliance)
  • Validation engineer (confirms verifiability)

What should the design review check?

  • Does every RS requirement have a corresponding CS/DS section? (completeness)
  • Does the configuration/design actually meet the requirement? (adequacy)
  • Does the configuration/design introduce functions not mentioned in the RS? (scope control)
  • Does every GxP-critical requirement have a corresponding validation test case?

CDMO pitfall: skipping the design review

Many CDMOs skip the design review and go straight to OQ testing. The result is that a mismatch between the design (or configuration) and the requirements is discovered only during OQ, requiring the configuration to be redone and retested, wasting a great deal of time.

The cost of a design review is always lower than rework after validation. As a general rule: before IQ/OQ starts, the CS/DS must have completed design review and been approved.

Multi-tenant SaaS: the importance of data segregation

When a CDMO uses a multi-tenant SaaS system (for example Veeva Vault), the DS must clearly explain how each tenant's data is segregated. This is especially important for a CDMO, because you may manage data for several clients (sponsors) at the same time and must ensure that Client A's data cannot be accessed by Client B. This requirement usually needs to be confirmed in the supplier's security architecture review and documented in the quality agreement.

The Traceability Chain: Requirements → Design → Testing

Complete Lifecycle Traceability

A fundamental principle throughout Appendices D1 and D3 is that all specifications should be structured to support traceability through the entire life cycle from individual requirements through configuration/design and into testing. This is referenced in both appendices as fundamental to GxP validation.

Traceability Requirements for RS

  • Requirements should be uniquely identified and version controlled
  • Requirements should enable clear communication and management of critical requirements throughout the life cycle
  • Requirements should be linked to the associated business-process step(s) where appropriate
  • Requirements should support full traceability through configuration/design and testing — see Appendix M5

Traceability Requirements for CS/DS

  • All specifications should be structured in a way that supports traceability through the life cycle from individual requirements to associated testing
  • Each specification in a tier should be uniquely referenced and traceable back to its appropriate higher-level specification
  • This high-level design should also be traceable to the RS, where appropriate

Design Review and Traceability (Reference to Appendix M5)

Further information on design review and traceability is provided in Appendix M5. The design review process is the formal mechanism through which the traceability chain is verified — confirming that the CS/DS adequately addresses all requirements in the RS before proceeding to testing.

Commentary: Traceability Is the Backbone of the Validation Plan

Structure of the complete traceability chain

Business process requirement
    ↓
RS requirement ID (e.g. RS-GMP-007)
    ↓
Regulatory basis (e.g. EU GMP Annex 11 §9)
    ↓
CS/DS section (e.g. CS section 5.3: electronic signature configuration)
    ↓
Validation test case ID (e.g. OQ-TC-023)
    ↓
Test result + pass/fail + tester signature

All consolidated in the "Traceability Matrix"

Practical uses of the traceability matrix

The traceability matrix is not a document "made for the inspector"; it has real engineering uses in the project:

  • Planning the validation scope: all GMP-critical requirements need to be tested - the traceability matrix lets you ensure none are missed
  • Assessing change impact: when the system changes, quickly identify which requirements and which test cases need to be reassessed
  • Audit preparation: when an auditor asks, "Show me how the audit trail requirement was verified" - the traceability matrix finds the answer in a second
  • Deviation investigation: when the system behaves abnormally, quickly trace back to the relevant design decisions and validation evidence

Section Summary

  • RS is the "what," CS/DS is the "how." A Requirements Specification defines business needs independent of technical solutions. Configuration and Design Specifications explain how those needs will be implemented. Never mix the two.
  • SMART requirements enable validation. A requirement that cannot be tested cannot be validated. The most common failure is writing compliance statements instead of testable functional requirements.
  • The requirements hierarchy flows top-down. URS (business) → FRS (functional) → CS/DS (implementation). Each level derives from the one above and must remain traceable to it.
  • CS for COTS, DS for custom. Configured commercial software needs a Configuration Specification recording parameters and their business justification. Custom-developed software needs a full Design Specification including module architecture, data structures, and algorithms.
  • Traceability is the backbone of validation. Every requirement should trace forward to a design decision and a test case. The Traceability Matrix is the instrument that makes this explicit, supports impact assessment, and provides evidence for regulatory review.
  • Two critical CDMO pitfalls: over-specifying COTS and under-specifying URS. Over-specifying COTS creates requirements that cannot be met (you can't configure what the software doesn't allow). Under-specifying URS leads to scope creep and a system that doesn't meet business needs.
ISPE GAMP 5, 2nd Edition | Appendices D4–D5 | p207 – p234

Learning Objectives

  • Understand the GAMP 5 approach to software development lifecycle, version control, code review, and release management for GxP systems
  • Distinguish IQ, OQ, and PQ in the context of computerized systems (computer CSV) versus traditional equipment qualification
  • Apply a risk-based test strategy to determine the appropriate level and type of testing required for different software categories
  • Differentiate scripted vs. unscripted testing approaches and know when each is appropriate
  • Explain defect management during validation: how to classify critical, major, and minor defects, and apply a go/no-go decision framework
  • Describe regression testing and test environment management best practices, including why a dedicated validated test environment separate from production is essential
Appendix D4 — Management, Development, and Review of Software

D4.1 Introduction & Scope

This appendix provides guidance for the management, development, and review of software. It also includes general guidance on coding practices. Software tools to assist with these activities are available, and in general, automation of these processes will lead to better compliance, efficiency, and reproducibility when compared to manual approaches.

Software development should be performed within a defined life cycle. The criteria for releasing developed and tested software should be understood and defined. Software released into the GxP environment should be based on, and meet, defined requirements.

This appendix is not intended to constrain the choice of development methods and models. Suppliers should select and use the most appropriate methods and models for their use. Any examples used, or technologies assumed, are intended to illustrate general principles and are not intended to be restrictive.

Scope: While the main focus is on software development using high-level and low-level languages, the principles of this appendix also may be applied to various techniques used for process control system software development/configuration.

Changes from GAMP 5 First Edition

This appendix has been updated in line with changes made to Appendix D1 (Specifying Requirements), and also to align with information in new appendices D8 (Agile) and D9 (Tools).

Commentary: D4 Introduction and Scope

Who is D4 written for?

D4 is aimed mainly at software suppliers (such as LIMS, MES, and ERP vendors) and custom development teams (pharmaceutical companies that develop their own Category 5 software).

Key message: software development in a GxP environment is not "write it and make sure it runs." It requires a defined lifecycle, version control, and code review, and the software must meet defined acceptance criteria before release.

Analogy: software development is like batch manufacturing

In pharmaceutical manufacturing, every lot of raw material has a lot number, a formulation, manufacturing step records, and QC testing, and it must meet specification before release.

Software development works the same way: every build should have a version number, requirements traceability, code review records, and a test report, and it must meet defined acceptance criteria before deployment to a GxP environment.

Practical Points

D4 explicitly states that the guide does not prescribe a development method (both waterfall and Agile are acceptable), but it requires that the lifecycle be defined. This is the core philosophy of GAMP 5: it specifies "what to do," not "how to do it."

D4.3.1 Software Development

The software development method should be defined and regardless of the model used, should also define:

  • Coding standards
  • Code repository standards
  • Software development tools
  • Naming conventions
  • Configuration and use of build and deployment systems

Source code should be subject to review with the approach and extent of such reviews based upon the coding language, tools, and risk to patient safety, product quality, and data integrity.

D4.3.1.1 General Design and Documentation Principles

Software design artifacts should be sufficient to initially develop and subsequently support working software. The degree and nature of the design will be based on the life cycle model used, software complexity, code/configuration language, and interfaces. Design artifacts may be in documentation form or as records within software development tools.

It is good practice to include a Software Bill of Materials (SBOM) that provides the list of components/modules that make up the overall software and can also be used to identify/track inter-dependencies.

Software should ultimately:

  • Meet requirements
  • Be reliable and robust
  • Be maintainable
  • Be capable of handling error conditions
  • Be well designed and sufficiently commented

D4.3.1.2 Software Module Identification

Each software module should include the following attributes:

  • Module name
  • Reference/traceability to associated design artifacts
  • Constituent source file names
  • Module version number
  • Project name/reference (if applicable)
  • Brief description of the module
  • References/dependencies to other software modules
  • Any specific command files required to compile and build the module

Commentary: Software Development Lifecycle

SBOM (Software Bill of Materials) — A New Highlight

The second edition of GAMP 5 introduces the concept of the Software Bill of Materials (SBOM). It follows the manufacturing practice of the BOM (bill of materials):

  • Lists the components, modules, and third-party libraries that make up the system
  • Identifies dependencies between components
  • When a security vulnerability emerges, makes it possible to quickly determine which systems are affected

When validating a LIMS, requesting an SBOM from the supplier has become a new best practice.

Software module identification = drug product labeling

Just as every drug product must state its active ingredient, excipients, lot number, and expiry date, every software module must have a version number, its associated requirements documents, and the other modules it depends on. Without this information, maintenance and troubleshooting become extremely difficult.

Audit Focus

A common question during FDA/EMA inspections: "What is the difference between version 3.2.1 and version 3.1.0 of your LIMS? Is there a change record?"

If you cannot answer, it indicates inadequate version control and may lead to a Form 483 observation.

D4.3.1.3–1.7 Version Control, Maintainability & Code Quality

Software Module Change Traceability

All software module changes should be clearly identified according to the chosen software development life cycle and technology used. It should be possible to obtain a history of changes made, which should include the following information for each change:

  • Change request number(s) (if applicable)
  • Applicable version number(s)
  • Dates change(s) made
  • Identity of person(s) changing the code
  • Summary of the changes

D4.3.1.4 Maintainability

Software should be written and structured in a way that a competent person (other than the original author) would be able to understand and modify it safely. The software should be created using the coding standards relevant to the technology.

D4.3.1.5 Open-Source Modules

Where modules are imported from an open-source code repository, updates as and when the open-source code is updated need to be determined. In addition, occasionally open-source modules may be retired, in which case future vulnerabilities will not be addressed, which increases risk.

D4.3.1.6 Removal of Dead Code

Dead code is code that cannot be executed due to the logic of the program, and should be removed. It is usually a symptom of poor maintenance. Code that has been included for purposes of testing or for later diagnosis during support work, and which can be configured on or off, is not regarded as dead code.

D4.3.1.7 Reliability and Error Recovery

"Defensive" coding techniques should be employed. Input values should be checked, and subroutines should check that the values of the received parameters are within range. In the event of a failure, a safe, documented error recovery or a controlled shutdown should be performed, with an informative error message.

Software Configuration Management

Software should be subject to a defined software configuration management process that includes version control of software code, scripts, and any relevant artifacts required by the technologies in use. Such change and version control is especially important during software development, as mistakes in this regard can lead to problems such as reintroduction of previously removed defects.

Commentary: Version Control and Code Quality

The GxP Significance of Version Control

Version control is not just an IT tool (such as Git). In a GxP context, it must ensure:

  • Traceability: every code change can be traced to a corresponding Change Request
  • Tamper-resistance: a reviewed and tested version cannot be modified by unauthorized persons
  • Reproducibility: any historical version can be rebuilt so that issues can be investigated

Dead code = expired inventory

Imagine a warehouse holding a lot of expired raw material that nobody knows whether it can still be used. Dead code is the same: code that exists should have a clear purpose. Code that can never be executed signals poor maintenance and may carry security or data integrity risks.

Special Considerations for Open Source Software (OSS)

The second edition of GAMP 5 significantly expands the guidance on open source software. Many LIMS and MES products use open source components (such as PostgreSQL and Python libraries). Regulatory focus:

  • Is the open source community still actively maintaining it?
  • Are security vulnerability updates being tracked?
  • Is the version locked and documented?

Defensive Coding

This is especially important for QC systems. For example, when an instrument sends an abnormal value (such as a pH of -999), the system should reject it and log an error rather than accept it and calculate an absurd result. This bears directly on data integrity.

D4.3.2 Source Code Review

Source code reviews have three objectives:

  • To ensure that programming standards, such as those described above, are consistently and correctly applied
  • To ensure that the code is written in accordance with the design
  • To identify and enable removal of defects

The review aims to ensure that the code is fit to release, and that the code can be effectively and efficiently maintained during the period of use of the application.

The review should be performed by at least one independent person with sufficient knowledge and expertise, in conjunction with the author of the code.

Automated source code review comparison tools are particularly useful when making small changes to existing code.

D4.3.2.1 Static Analysis of Software

"Static analysis involves a set of methods, supported by tools, used to analyze software source code or object code to determine how the software functions and establish criteria to check its correctness."

These include:

  • Control Analysis – analysis of the controls used in calling structure, control flow, and state transitions
  • Data Analysis – ensures proper operation is applied to data objects and data structures
  • Fault/Failure Analysis – identifies incorrectly specified or built components and incorrect behavior of components
  • Interface Analysis – checks the accuracy of interface structure, and the ability to prevent errors during user interaction

Formal Verification methods may also be considered for high-risk areas of code using formal mathematical methods to provide "proof" of algorithms under multiple input conditions.

A decision based upon known risks should determine the extent of software review required for a given project. The criteria for making this assessment, and the outcome, should be documented.

Commentary: Source Code Review

Three GxP Objectives of Code Review

In a GxP environment, code review (Code Review) is not just a quality tool but part of the validation activities:

  • Compliance confirmation: are the coding standards being followed?
  • Design consistency: does the code implement the logic defined in the design specification (DS)?
  • Defect identification: finding problems before testing is the lowest-cost option

Static analysis = incoming raw material inspection before the process

Static analysis (Static Analysis) examines code structure without executing the code. It is like reviewing the CoA and performing identification testing on incoming raw materials: you do not need to put the material into the process to discover the problem.

Tool examples: SonarQube (code quality), Checkmarx (security vulnerabilities)

The Importance of Independent Review

GAMP 5 requires that code review be performed by someone other than the original author. This is consistent with the GMP principle that "the operator cannot be the reviewer."

In the validation of Category 5 custom-developed systems, code review records are examined by regulators.

D4.3.3 Third-Party Software (including Open-Source)

D4.3.3.1 Purchased Software

In most cases evidence of software control can be obtained through standard mechanisms for supplier assessment, such as supplier audit (see Appendix M2). Conclusions of the assessment should be documented. If inadequacies are noted, the regulated company may require remedial actions by the supplier, possibly enforced via a Service Level Agreement (SLA), or may decide that further testing is required.

D4.3.3.2 Free and Open-Source Software (OSS)

OSS often plays an important role in the infrastructure of regulated companies. Examples include the Linux operating system, R environment for statistical computing, and many web design, programming, and support tools.

Note that commercial software may contain open-source components incorporated into it. It is becoming more common to request a SBOM when evaluating new commercial software or validating in-house developed systems.

If a decision is taken to use OSS, then such software should be implemented in accordance with the appropriate life cycle activities as for commercial software, with consideration of the following:

  • Knowing the size and sustainability of the open-source community
  • Checking for development standards and good documentation
  • Knowing what version is in use — verify download from a reputable source and ensure code was not altered (via checksum)
  • Understanding the governance model for updates and patches
  • Keeping up-to-date with patches and updates; vulnerabilities are often exposed in software using outdated versions of open-source libraries
  • Participating in the open-source community

To determine whether there is an adequate level of software maturity and control, a risk assessment should be performed to determine the business and GxP risks of using OSS products, and to identify appropriate controls.

Commentary: Third-Party and Open Source Software

Real-world scenario: using the R language for QC statistical analysis

Many QC departments use R for statistical analysis (such as process capability analysis, Cpk). Under GAMP 5 D4.3.3.2:

  • The R version in use must be locked (no automatic updates)
  • The versions of the packages used must be documented (the SBOM concept)
  • The impact on GxP functions must be assessed before any update
  • The statistical calculation logic must be verified
Appendix D5 — Testing of Computerized Systems

D5.1 Introduction

This appendix covers the testing of GxP computerized systems. It is not intended to offer a prescriptive approach — it does not require that testing must be done a certain way in a particular sequence. Instead, this appendix identifies the influences on testing and offers information on the types of testing and testing approaches that can be used selectively and in combination for optimal assurance that the system is fit for its intended use.

The application of critical thinking is vital to achieve effective testing that adds value and fulfills objectives such as:

  • Identifying defects so they can be corrected or removed before operational use
  • Reducing the risk of failures that might affect patient safety, product quality, or data integrity
  • Demonstrating that the system as installed and configured meets its requirements
  • Providing evidence that the system is fit for its intended use
  • Offering a basis for user acceptance
  • Meeting a key regulatory requirement
  • Ensuring that risk management controls are effective

Testing should be carried out in accordance with an appropriate test strategy based on the risk to patient safety, product quality, and data integrity. Testing may occur both in the vendor's development life cycle and in the regulated company's implementation life cycle.

Key Changes from GAMP 5 First Edition

  • Critical thinking should be applied when planning testing efforts such that the level of effort is commensurate to the risk acceptable within the organization
  • Testing by any means and in any part of the life cycle all contributes to finding defects and confirming fitness for intended use
  • Testing should not be limited to detailed and prescriptive step-by-step scripted protocols. Exploratory testing and other unscripted techniques are encouraged
  • Modern approaches may rely on records and artifacts in automated tools in place of formal specification and test documentation — either approach is acceptable

Commentary: Introduction to the D5 Testing Appendix

The Shift in Testing Philosophy in the Second Edition of GAMP 5

Compared with the first edition, the biggest changes in the second edition are:

  • Removal of prescriptiveness: a linear flow of URS → FS → DS → IQ → OQ → PQ is no longer required
  • Embracing unscripted testing: exploratory testing (Exploratory Testing) is officially recognized
  • Acknowledging the validity of tool-based records: records from tools such as Jira tickets and CI/CD logs can serve as test evidence
  • Critical thinking is central: it matters more than procedural compliance

The Seven Purposes of Testing — Memory Aid

These seven purposes cover three dimensions of testing:

  • Technical dimension: finding defects and confirming that requirements are met
  • Regulatory dimension: meeting regulatory requirements and providing evidence of fitness for intended use
  • Risk dimension: reducing risks to patient safety, product quality, and data integrity, and confirming that risk controls are effective

D5.3 Roles and Responsibilities

The regulated company should define roles and responsibilities covering testing. Such definitions typically are included in an appropriate planning document, test strategy, or company procedure. Traditional roles and responsibilities may include:

The quality of the test cases and the functionality they cover directly impact the effectiveness of the testing. No matter who is creating the test case, they should be an SME in the associated field.

Commentary: Roles and Responsibilities in Testing

Independence Principle

GAMP 5 emphasizes two key independence requirements:

  • Developer ≠ tester: the person who writes the code must not test their own code (they will only confirm expected behavior and will miss design blind spots)
  • Executor ≠ reviewer: the person who executes a test must not review their own test results

This is the same as the GMP "second-person verification" principle and ensures objectivity.

The Role of SMEs in Test Design

GAMP 5 specifically emphasizes that the designer of test cases must be an SME in the relevant field.

Take the sample receipt function of a LIMS as an example:

  • A test written by an IT tester: "Fill in the fields, save, and confirm there is no error"
  • Tests written by a QC SME: "Test whether the system alerts when a sample ID is duplicated," "Test the priority flag for urgent samples," "Test the notification logic for cross-department receipt"

SME tests cover business scenarios and IT tests cover system functions; both are indispensable.

Supplier Responsibility vs. Regulated Company Responsibility

No matter how much is outsourced, ultimate responsibility for regulatory compliance always rests with the regulated company. Supplier testing can be leveraged, but it cannot replace the regulated company's validation decisions.

D5.4 Influences on Test Activities & Table 25.1

Test coverage and approaches are influenced by the:

  • Criticality of the business process supported by the system, including the GxP impact
  • Requirements for the system
  • Supplier's development life cycle and quality system
  • Regulated company's internal computerized systems validation policy
  • Risk culture within the company
  • Use and existence of additional controls elsewhere in the business process
  • Level of test automation available

Table 25.1: Key Testing Activities and Their Influences

Commentary: Factors Influencing Testing Activities

Risk-Based Test Strategy — Core Concept

One of the most important messages of GAMP 5: not all functions need the same degree of testing.

Testing effort should be commensurate with risk (commensurate with risk):

  • High-risk functions (such as electronic signatures and batch release calculations) → detailed scripted testing (Scripted Testing: Robust)
  • Medium-risk functions (such as report generation) → limited scripted testing (Scripted Testing: Limited)
  • Low-risk functions (such as UI preference settings) → unscripted exploratory testing is sufficient

Test Planning Example for LIMS Validation

Taking the validation of a LIMS as an example, following the logic of Table 25.1:

  • Requirements definition: first complete the URS/FRS and confirm the scope of GxP impact
  • Supplier assessment: does the supplier have complete test records for GAMP Category 4 software? This can reduce the testing burden on the regulated company
  • Functional risk assessment: the sample tracking function (high risk) vs. report format settings (low risk)
  • Test planning: high-risk functions require 100% scripted testing; low-risk functions can use exploratory testing

Key Content of the Validation Report

A validation report is more than a summary stating that "all tests passed." It also needs to:

  • Record where the test records are stored (in a tool? paper protocols?)
  • List unresolved deviations and their impact assessments
  • Confirm whether the validation strategy was executed as planned

D5.9.1.1 Installation Qualification (IQ) Context for Computerized Systems

Although the term is not used in this Guide (which refers to "installation verification activities" in general), many companies still use the traditional process validation term Installation Qualification (IQ).

The purpose is to verify and document that system components are combined and installed in accordance with specifications, supplier documentation, and local and global requirements. Installation testing provides a verified configuration baseline for subsequent verification and validation activities, and verifies any installation methods, tools, or scripts used.

For cloud-native or virtualized software, the use of automated installers combined with configuration management tools and service management tools will be more effective than conventional installation approaches. If they provide dashboard functionality, then a review-by-exception approach can replace many of the static verification activities.

Whatever the approach, the availability of appropriate technical information should be verified. Examples include:

  • User and technical guides; SOPs; Training schedules
  • SLAs; Security procedures; Log books
  • Hardware inventory; Instrument lists; Specification sheets
  • Certificates and calibration procedures
  • Software inventory (including installation procedure, system software list, application software list, data list, initial data settings)
  • Preventive maintenance program; List of critical spare parts

Commentary: What IQ/OQ/PQ Mean for Computerized Systems

Important: IQ/OQ/PQ for computerized systems is not the same as equipment qualification!

Many engineers and QA staff apply an equipment qualification mindset to software validation, which is a common mistake. Let us compare them using a LIMS as the example:

LIMS IQ: Installation Qualification

Purpose: confirm that the system has been installed correctly according to specification

Items checked:

  • Hardware specification check (do CPU, RAM, and storage meet the minimum requirements?)
  • Operating system version and patch level check
  • LIMS software version check (consistent with the purchase specification)
  • Configuration file records (database connection settings, network configuration)
  • Security settings check (firewall, user access architecture)
  • Backup and restore functions have been set up

Note: IQ confirms "what has been installed correctly," not whether the functions work properly.

LIMS OQ: Operational Qualification

Purpose: confirm that all configured functions of the system operate correctly under expected conditions

Test content (corresponding to every functional requirement in the FRS):

  • Sample receipt workflow testing (normal cases + boundary cases)
  • Test method configuration verification (calculation logic, specification limits)
  • Electronic signature function (signing, meaning, non-repudiation)
  • Audit trail (record creation, modification, deletion)
  • User access segregation (QC staff cannot modify method specifications)
  • Instrument interface data transfer testing

OQ is the most time-consuming phase, typically accounting for 60-70% of the validation effort.

LIMS PQ: Performance Qualification

Purpose: use real-world scenarios (end-to-end) to confirm that the system meets its intended use under actual operating conditions

Test approach:

  • "Day in the Life" testing performed by actual users (QC analysts, sample receipt staff)
  • Use real sample types and typical batch quantities
  • Complete workflow: sample login → assignment → analysis → review → reporting → batch release
  • Cross-department integration testing (LIMS ↔ ERP batch record system)

The question PQ answers is: "Can this system really support our business?"

Risk-based: not every system needs a PQ

GAMP 5 does not require every system to go through all three stages of IQ/OQ/PQ. A low-risk Category 3 system (such as an office productivity tool) may need only installation verification plus limited functional testing. The depth of testing is determined by risk, not by traditional convention.

D5.5 Test Planning/Coverage & Test Cases

D5.5.1 Test Management Processes

Early and efficient testing, during development as well as during the verification phase, improves defect detection and reduces the occurrence of defects surviving into the operational phase.

A test management process may include:

  • Any special prerequisites to testing
  • Identifying what combination of scripted or unscripted, manual or automated testing will be used
  • The personnel required for each test or group of tests
  • The expectations regarding test evidence (including the recording and reporting of test pass/fail results)
  • How the environment in which the testing is to occur will be identified and recorded
  • How the progress and outcome of testing will be monitored
  • How test cases and completed tests will be reviewed and approved

D5.5.2 Scripted vs. Unscripted Testing — Table 25.2 Summary

Table 25.2: Acceptable Assurance Approaches and Records

Table 25.3: Comparison of Unscripted vs. Scripted Testing

Commentary: Testing Documentation Requirements

Core question: which documents need prior approval, and which are recorded afterward?

This is the question QA reviewers ask most often. Table 25.2 gives a clear answer:

  • Scripted testing (Scripted: Limited/Robust): the test script (test script) must be independently reviewed and approved before execution
  • Unscripted testing: only high-level test objectives are needed before execution; the steps do not need to be predefined
  • All testing: results records (including who performed the test, when, and what was found) can be recorded after execution

"Unscripted ≠ undocumented"

GAMP 5 states explicitly: "Unscripted testing does not mean undocumented testing."

Minimum documentation requirements for unscripted testing:

  • What was tested (what was tested)
  • By whom (by whom)
  • When (when)
  • Any issues found (any issues found)

Exploratory testing = DOE in process development

In process development, design of experiments (DOE) lets you systematically explore the parameter space and discover unexpected interactions. Exploratory testing is similar: testers use their own knowledge to explore the software systematically and find boundary cases that scripted testing may miss.

PDCA cycle: plan the exploration objectives → execute → record findings → adjust direction and continue exploring

Too Many Screenshots Is Not a Good Thing

GAMP 5 explicitly criticizes the practice of "prolific screenshots." Principles:

  • Pass (Pass): simply record "Pass"; no screenshot is needed
  • Fail (Fail): record "Fail" plus a description of the difference between the actual and expected results
  • Screenshots are valuable only for complex data scenarios that require a "before and after" comparison

D5.5.3 Executing Tests

The quality of testing is significantly impacted by the knowledge and skill of the tester, therefore:

  • Day in the life testing needs to be executed by a skilled business process user
  • All other manual testing needs to be executed by a skilled software tester; where unscripted testing is used, knowledge of the system under test is also essential
  • All staff responsible for test execution, including end users, should be trained in test procedures and should be able to demonstrate sufficient confidence in operating the system under test
  • Training should be documented

The identity of the tester should be recorded for all executed test cases. It is disproportionate and unnecessary to use test witnessing or to require the tester to initial every test step.

Automated test tools require a documented assessment of their adequacy prior to use.

Test Evidence — Critical Thinking

It is recommended to apply critical thinking when defining the need for test records and evidence:

  • Ensure that even where testing is not scripted in detail, the objective of the test remains clear, and the results record adequately what testing was carried out
  • Ensure that test evidence is only collected for proving steps that are not inherently covered by evidence from another test
  • Adopt an exception-reporting approach: if the system response matches expected results, a simple "Pass" can be recorded. If the system responds unexpectedly, record both a "Fail" and a description of how the response differed
  • Recognize that routine requirements for screenshots as objective evidence bring little value to verification activities

D5.5.4 Reviewing and Reporting Completed Tests

Completed testing should be reviewed to verify that the computerized system is fit for its intended use. While test evidence should be reviewed to confirm completeness and accuracy, there is little value in:

  • Looking for minor errors in documentation that have no impact on patient safety, product quality, or data integrity
  • Scrutinizing test evidence in extreme detail to assess whether the tester has exactly followed the test instructions for non-critical functionality

Unusual patterns of test failures associated with particular authors and/or individual testers should be investigated to determine whether there are any wider testing implications.

Test reports should be produced that summarize activities and findings and state the final conclusions.

Commentary: Test Execution and Defect Management

Defect Management — Decision Framework During Validation

Defects found during testing must be classified by severity, and then a decision made on whether validation can continue:

Exception-Reporting Approach

GAMP 5 principle for efficient test recording:

  • Normal outcome: recording "Pass" is sufficient
  • Abnormal outcome: record "Fail" + expected result + actual result + description of the difference

This eliminates a great deal of unnecessary paperwork (such as step-by-step screenshots) and lets testers focus on recording the defects that truly add value.

Test review focuses on function, not document format

GAMP 5 states explicitly that the focus of test review is: "Does the function work correctly?", not "Did the tester follow every step exactly?"

Common examples of over-auditing (explicitly discouraged by GAMP 5):

  • Requiring testers to sign next to every step
  • Rejecting an entire test record because the date was written as 01/04 instead of 2026-04-01

D5.6 Types of Testing

D5.6.1 Test Types Unique to Unscripted Testing

  • Ad-hoc testing — unscripted testing performed without planning or predefined documentation. Aimed at finding defects as early as possible.
  • Error guessing — a testing technique designed to expose anticipated and potential defects based on the specialist tester's knowledge and experience of failure modes.
  • Exploratory testing — experience-based testing where the tester spontaneously designs and executes tests based on existing specialist knowledge, prior exploration, and typical common software behaviors and types of failure and defects.
  • Day in the life testing — allows users of the system to carry out their normal day-to-day activities to uncover issues not identified by scripted testing. These testers need real-life business process experience and knowledge.

D5.6.2 Other Test Types (Scripted or Unscripted)

  • White box testing (structural testing) — test cases identified based on the internal structure (source code) of the component or system.
  • Black box testing (functional testing) — based on an analysis of the specification, without reference to internal structure.
  • Normal Case testing — challenges the system's ability to do what it should do, including triggering alerts and error messages per specifications.
  • Negative Case testing — challenges the system's ability to correctly prevent actions that specifications state should be prohibited or blocked.
  • Repeatability testing — challenges the system's ability to repeatedly do what it should.
  • Performance testing — challenges the system's ability to perform as fast and effectively as required by specifications.
  • Volume/Load testing — challenges the system's ability to manage high loads. Typically automated. Required when system resources are critical.
  • Structural/Path testing — challenges a program's internal structure by exercising detailed program code.
  • Smoke testing — superficial testing to check critical functionality as a measure of system build stability.
  • Regression testing — challenges the system's ability to still do what it should after being modified; verifies that portions not involved in the change were not adversely affected.

D5.6.3 Contractual/Acceptance Testing: FAT and SAT

Acceptance tests are a predefined set of functional tests that demonstrate fitness for intended use. Acceptance may be carried out in two stages:

  • Factory Acceptance Tests (FAT) — performed at the supplier site before delivery to show that the system is working well enough to be installed and tested on-site.
  • Site Acceptance Tests (SAT) — show that the system is working in its operational environment and that it interfaces correctly with other systems and peripherals.

These acceptance tests may be leveraged in support of, but do not replace the need for, process qualification including Process Performance Qualification (PPQ) where relevant.

Commentary: Test Types Explained

Regression Testing — The Most Critical Testing After a Software Update

When the LIMS supplier releases an update from v4.1 → v4.2, you need to determine which tests must be re-run:

  • Affected functional modules: based on the supplier's Release Notes and change list, identify which functions were modified
  • Dependent functions: modifying the sample receipt module may affect sample status tracking and the reporting module
  • Core GxP functions: electronic signatures, audit trail, batch release — include these in regression testing regardless of whether the update touches them
  • Tests that need not be re-run: functions that are completely unaffected, supported by a documented risk assessment

FAT/SAT vs IQ/OQ/PQ — Clarifying the Relationship

In the validation of automated equipment (such as the control system of an aseptic filling line):

  • FAT: confirms the basic functions of the system at the supplier's site (close to IQ + part of OQ)
  • SAT: confirms that the system works in the actual environment on site (close to OQ + part of PQ)
  • FAT/SAT can be leveraged (leveraged) as evidence for IQ/OQ, reducing duplicate testing
  • But FAT/SAT cannot replace PQ (process performance qualification must be carried out under actual production conditions)

Negative Case Testing Is Often Overlooked

Many validation protocols test only "what the system will do" and forget to test "whether the system correctly rejects what it should not do."

Examples of LIMS negative tests:

  • Does the system refuse batch release while testing is incomplete?
  • Does the system correctly trigger an OOS alert when an out-of-specification value is entered?
  • Does the system block an unauthorized user who attempts to modify a signed record?

D5.7 Test Environments

Testing may take place in different environments during a project, which may include:

  • Development environment — where prototyping or programming takes place
  • Formal testing environment — dedicated environment for structured validation testing
  • Operational environment — the target production environment

The use of multiple environments provides the opportunity to:

  • Assess changes before they are rolled out to production
  • Provide a target for restore testing of backup data
  • Reduce risk to the live operational environment and data

The DevOps environment used in Agile approaches may be replicated into multiple test environments on demand. The test environments, whether on-premise or hosted by the SaaS provider, need to be functionally equivalent for the testing to be representative and effective. Differences should be assessed and mitigated.

It is essential that a record is kept of the environment in which a test case was run, and the current build and configuration of that environment. This record may reside in an automated tool (e.g., continuous integration orchestration).

There is no benefit to repeating testing already performed in another, but still adequately controlled environment just because it was not the formal test environment.

It is the scope, rigor, and coverage of the testing that ensures the quality of the system as implemented. Any significant differences between the environments used for testing should be assessed to determine the equivalency of test results.

Commentary: Test Environment Management

Why is an independent test environment needed?

Testing directly in the production environment (Production) is a major GxP risk:

  • Test data may contaminate real GxP records
  • Test steps may inadvertently modify validated configurations
  • Test failures may cause production interruptions
  • It is difficult to maintain the integrity of the "validated state"

Environment equivalence (Functional Equivalency) = analytical method transfer

If an analytical method has been validated in Laboratory A and is to be used in Laboratory B, a method transfer must be performed to demonstrate that the results of the two laboratories are comparable.

The test environment is the same: for testing completed in the TEST environment, if the environment configuration differs from PROD, you need to assess whether the differences affect the validity of the test results. If they do not (for example, audit trail test results are identical in both environments), there is no need to repeat the test in PROD.

Special Considerations for SaaS Environments

For cloud SaaS systems (such as some cloud-based LIMS), the test environment is managed by the supplier. Key requirements:

  • Confirm that the sandbox/staging environment provided by the supplier is functionally equivalent to the production environment
  • Record the environment version at each test (the supplier may update at any time)
  • Define responsibility for maintaining the test environment explicitly in the contract (SLA)

D5.8 Leveraging Previous Testing Including Supplier Testing

D5.8.1 Supplier Testing

The more extensive and effective the testing done by the supplier during their life cycle, the less testing the regulated company may need to do. If the supplier testing is confirmed as sufficient to demonstrate that the system functions correctly against supplier specifications, then the regulated company verification activity may be limited to a subset of requirements testing.

Common test types executed during a supplier development life cycle are:

  • Acceptance Testing for Purchased Hardware and Software — subject to acceptance testing before being used for the system's development
  • Unit/Module Tests — stand-alone tests for software components, ensuring readiness for further integration
  • Integration and Functional Tests — testing of integrated software components, sub-systems, and the complete system

In order to leverage supplier testing, the regulated company should first consider:

  • The extent, coverage and quality of the supplier testing
  • How supplier test records can be made available for appropriate assessment and review
  • How traceability between the regulated company's requirements and the supplier's test cases will be established

D5.8.2 Ongoing Life Cycle Maintenance

New releases of the software by the supplier will result in the need for the regulated company to actively maintain the validated state after upgrading. Critical thinking and risk management should be used to determine the extent of the effort required.

As a minimum, the regulated company will need to verify the updated software remains fit for their intended use. The supplier should provide a clear understanding of the changes being implemented. Regression testing of the configuration and workflows — whether manual or automated — may be implemented to confirm the latest software version still provides a system fit for the regulated company's intended use.

D5.8.3 Testing in Multiple Environments

Testing in environments other than the validation or production environment contributes to finding defects and confirming the system is fit for intended use. Where there is no impact to the particular test types arising from the difference in the environments, the testing should be leveraged no matter what environment it occurred in — there is no benefit to repeating that testing in the formal test or production environments.

Commentary: Leveraging Supplier Testing

The "Leverage" Strategy for Supplier Testing

The core efficiency principle of GAMP 5: do not repeat good testing that has already been done.

Prerequisites for leveraging supplier testing:

  1. Supplier assessment is complete (Appendix M2): confirms that the supplier's quality system and development processes meet requirements
  2. Supplier test records can be reviewed: the test plan, test cases, and test results are all available
  3. Traceability is established: the supplier's test cases can be mapped to the regulated company's URS/FRS requirements

Analogy: trusting a qualified raw material supplier

Materials from a certified raw material supplier do not need full testing on every lot; you can rely on the CoA and perform only identification testing.

Similarly, for a software supplier that has been well audited and has complete test records, the regulated company can partially rely on the supplier's test results, reducing duplicate testing.

Regression Testing Decisions After a Software Update

When the supplier releases a patch or new version, the regulated company's decision process is:

  1. Obtain the Release Notes: understand what has changed
  2. Impact assessment: do these changes affect the GxP functions we have configured?
  3. Decide the scope of testing：
    • If it is only a security patch with no functional changes → minimal testing (such as a smoke test)
    • If there are functional changes → full regression testing of the affected functions
    • If there are major architectural changes → re-execute all testing
  4. Document the rationale for the decision: risk assessment record

D5.9 Testing Applied to Different Software Categories

D5.9.1.2 Points to Consider for All Systems

This is an aide memoire only and does not replace the need to apply critical thinking and a risk-based approach. Test coverage may include:

  • Power failure testing — prevention against loss of critical data; ease of controlled restart
  • System access and security features
  • Data audit trails for creation, modification, and deletion of GxP records
  • Manual data entry features, input validation
  • Electronic signature features
  • Alarms and error messages
  • Critical calculations
  • Critical transactions
  • Transfer of critical data into other packages or systems
  • Interfaces and data transfers
  • Backup and restore
  • Data archival and retrieval
  • Ability to deal with high-volume loads

D5.9.2 Category 3: Standard Product Testing

Software products used off-the-shelf (not configurable, or limited parameterization). Testing should focus on:

  • Installation testing as described above
  • Requirements testing that demonstrates fitness for intended use (may include testing against requirements depending on risk)
  • For very simple measurement systems, regular calibration may substitute for testing
  • Any further or more rigorous tests as a result of risk and supplier assessments

D5.9.3 Category 4: Configured Functionality Testing

Commercially available software configured for a specific business process. Testing should focus on:

  • Installation testing
  • Configuration testing — verifying that the system has been configured in accordance with the agreed specification
  • Functional testing — challenging functionality supporting the specific business process based on risk and supplier assessments
  • Requirements testing — challenging the configuration and user-defined workflows to ensure business process needs have been met

D5.9.4 Category 5: Custom Application Testing

Custom-developed software. Testing should include Category 4 approaches, plus:

  • Code review for new code required as a result of risk assessments (see Appendix D4)
  • Software module testing — testing software modules defined in the design specification
  • Software integration testing — testing that the modules work when operating together

Commentary: Test Strategies by Software Category

Important "System-Wide" Test Checklist

Regardless of Category, several items on the D5.9.1.2 list are examined almost every time:

  • Audit Trail: a core requirement of 21 CFR Part 11 / Annex 11
  • Electronic signatures: the meaning statement and non-repudiation must be tested
  • Backup & Restore: restore testing is often overlooked, but the consequences are serious
  • Critical calculations: all numerical calculations must be verified using known data

LIMS is the Representative Category 4 System
