Data must be partitioned into training, validation, and test sets during the concept phase — and this partitioning must be maintained throughout development to prevent data contamination that would compromise unbiased model evaluation.

31.3.2.3 Data Preprocessing and Classification

Data preparation includes: profiling and formatting, cleansing and transformation, anonymization (for GDPR compliance), and augmentation for diversity. The quality and integrity of data is critical — the quality of output is determined by the quality of input. Preparation must be documented and performed by data scientists with domain expertise.

"The quality and integrity of the data is critical (the quality of output is determined by the quality of input) and must be suitably classified and labeled. This is one of the most time-consuming efforts in the process."
— ISPE GAMP 5 Second Edition, Appendix D11, §31.3.2.3

Commentary: The AI/ML Lifecycle — Concept Phase

GIGO: Garbage In, Garbage Out

This is the most important maxim in AI/ML: Garbage In, Garbage Out (GIGO).

You can have the most advanced deep learning algorithm in the world, but if the training data itself is biased or wrong, the conclusions the model produces will be wrong too. Worse, the model presents those wrong conclusions with a high degree of "confidence."

In a pharma GxP environment, the consequences of GIGO are especially serious: if the training data systematically omits a rare defect, the visual inspection AI will never recognize that defect, and the production team will have no idea the blind spot exists.

What the Five Design Principles Mean for GxP

  • Interpretability: FDA 21 CFR Part 211 and EU GMP both require that the basis for quality decisions can be understood and explained. If an AI rejects a batch but cannot explain why, that is unacceptable in a GxP environment.
  • Reproducibility: for the same input, the model must always produce the same (or statistically consistent) output. This maps directly onto the GMP principle of consistency.
  • Liability: if the AI makes a wrong decision, who is responsible? In a GxP environment the answer must be a named person: AI is a support tool, and final responsibility rests with authorized personnel.

The Key to Dataset Partitioning

Why split the data into three parts (training, validation, test)?

  • Training set: the data the model learns patterns from. It can be used many times.
  • Validation set: used during training to tune hyperparameters and to assess whether the model is overfitting the training data.
  • Test set: kept completely isolated and used only once, at the end, for final performance confirmation (equivalent to UAT). It must never be used in any training or tuning activity.

If test set data "leaks" into the training process, the model will show falsely high accuracy and then perform poorly in real production. This is one of the most common pitfalls in AI validation.

The CDMO Data Challenge: Data Silos

Most pharma CDMOs have a data silo problem: batch records in MES, test data in LIMS, process data in equipment PLCs and deviation records in the QA system all sit in different systems, in different formats, and are hard to integrate.

Before starting an AI/ML project, the feasibility and cost of data integration must be assessed first; otherwise the assumptions made in the concept phase will face serious challenges in the project phase.

31.3.3 Life Cycle: Project Phase — Model Development

The project phase follows an iterative and incremental approach — not the linear waterfall model familiar from traditional computerized system validation. Each iteration cycles through: model design/selection → engineering → training → evaluation → hyperparameter tuning.

31.3.3.1 Model Requirements and Specifications

Initial requirements define the functionality and performance expectations of the ML sub-system. Performance metrics are a key component — they serve as the technical specifications for model acceptability and drive the iterative training-evaluation cycle. Nonfunctional requirements (deployment constraints, integration, security) must also be defined early to inform algorithm selection.

31.3.3.2 Model Design and Selection

A ML model is selected based on the question it is expected to answer. For a new system, it is unlikely that the algorithm choice is clear-cut from the start. Candidate algorithms should be evaluated against operational, performance, and regulatory requirements through a structured prototyping process.

31.3.3.3–31.3.3.4 Model Engineering and Training

Model engineering involves constructing architectures and surrounding infrastructure for training and hyperparameter tuning. During training:

  • The training dataset is supplied to the model; output is compared to ground truth to measure performance and update model parameters
  • The validation dataset evaluates training quality and assists in fine-tuning — it does NOT act as the final acceptance test
  • Hyperparameter optimization can be manual or automated (e.g., Bayesian optimization), but data scientists should not take a completely hands-off approach
  • Bias due to non-representative training data is a significant risk and must be actively managed

31.3.3.5 Model Evaluation and Testing

The best-performing models from training iterations are evaluated using performance scorecards. Visual qualitative evaluation of validation results — not just quantitative metrics — often yields better insights into failure modes. When target performance is achieved, models are selected as candidates for deployment.

31.3.3.6 Integration and Deployment

The ML sub-system is migrated from development code (optimized for fast prototyping) to deployment code (optimized for production maintenance). Key architectural principle: modularization — the inference module (forward pass from input to output) is isolated from training, evaluation, and parameter-update code.

31.3.3.7 Verification, Acceptance, and Release

The test dataset — excluded from all training and validation activities to date — is used as an independent dataset to confirm acceptable performance within the overarching application's UAT prior to deployment. At minimum, the verification process must be specified and documented, resulting in formal release of the first AI/ML sub-system version.

Commentary: Key Concepts in Model Development

What Are Hyperparameters?

Machine learning has two kinds of "numbers":

  • Model parameters: internal values the model learns automatically during training (such as the weights of a neural network). You do not set them directly; the model adjusts them itself.
  • Hyperparameters: the "control knobs" you set before training, such as the learning rate, tree depth or number of hidden layers. They determine how the model learns, not what it has learned.

Hyperparameter optimization is one of the most time-consuming tasks in AI/ML development, requiring repeated trials of different combinations to find the setting that gives the best model performance on the validation set.

The Relationship Between "Modular" Design and GxP

GAMP 5 requires that the inference module be separated from the training code. Why does this matter for GxP?

Analogy: in pharmaceutical manufacturing, we manage the "production recipe" separately from the "process development records." The production version is fixed and controlled; the development version can be modified iteratively.

In AI, the inference module is the "fixed, controlled production version," while the training code is the "development tool that can keep being optimized." Validation only needs to cover the deterministic behavior of the inference module, not the whole training infrastructure.

The Test Set in UAT: Like a "Process Confirmation Batch" in Pharma

The role of the test set in AI/ML validation is similar to that of Process Performance Qualification (PPQ) batches in pharmaceutical process validation:

  • It is completely sealed off throughout development and never takes part in training or hyperparameter tuning
  • It is used only once, at final confirmation, as an "independent performance verification on entirely new data"
  • If test set performance falls short, you cannot simply "adjust the model and try again"; you need to go back to the design and perform a root cause analysis

Visual Assessment vs Numerical Metrics Alone

GAMP 5 specifically stresses: do not rely only on numerical metrics such as accuracy and recall, because qualitative visual assessment often gives better insight.

A pharma AI example: a visual inspection AI reaches 99% overall accuracy, but its miss rate for one specific defect type (for example micro-bubbles) is 40%. The overall metric gives no hint of this problem. Only a case-by-case visual review of the errors can reveal this systematic blind spot.

31.3.4 Life Cycle: Operation Phase — Monitoring & Continuous Evaluation

The operation phase is where AI/ML systems diverge most significantly from traditional computerized systems. The performance of the AI/ML sub-system must be continuously monitored and maintained.

31.3.4.1 Monitoring for Static Systems

For static systems, changes must follow the organization's formal change-management process with risk-based evaluation of impact on current and future production data, and robust testing before deployment.

31.3.4.1 Monitoring for Dynamic Systems

For dynamic systems, appropriate KPIs and acceptable tolerance limits must be established to ensure the algorithm is still operating as intended. A systematic approach to integrating additional training data must be maintained:

  1. Acquire additional data of the needed sub-class
  2. Assign a portion to training set; exclude the remainder for testing
  3. Retrain model on augmented training dataset (expect possible performance drop due to increased challenge)
  4. Evaluate revised model against original model on augmented test dataset
  5. Confirm revised model achieves acceptable performance level before deployment

31.3.4.2 Performance Trending

In the operational phase, it is possible to integrate and prepare new data to further refine existing models, develop new predictive models, and establish performance monitoring to track ongoing effectiveness. Quality control standards and measures must exist to ensure acceptable performance throughout the model's usable life.

"In all cases, periodic review and monitoring of the model performance is needed to identify potential bias, overfitting, etc. The timing of reviews may be determined based on a defined interval and/or by activity such as the incorporation of new case data."
— ISPE GAMP 5 Second Edition, Appendix D11, §31.3.5.1

Commentary: The Operations Phase — Model Drift and Governance

Model Drift: AI's Most Hidden Risk

Model drift is the gradual decline of an AI model's predictive performance over time, which usually occurs in the following situations:

  • Data drift: the characteristics of the input data in the production environment change (for example, a new filling machine is installed and its vibration signature differs from the training data)
  • Concept drift: the definition of the "correct answer" changes (for example, the visual acceptance criteria are updated but the model still uses the old criteria)
  • Overfitting degradation: the model is too closely fitted to specific historical data and its performance on new data declines

The danger is that drift is slow and hard to notice. Without continuous performance monitoring, you may only discover months later that the model has failed, and every decision made in the meantime was based on an unreliable model.

Analogy: Continued Process Verification (CPV) in Process Validation

Pharmaceutical process validation has three stages: process design (PPD), process qualification (PPQ) and continued process verification (CPV). The core of CPV is continuous monitoring of process data to confirm that the process remains within its validated state.

"Continuous performance monitoring" of an AI/ML model follows exactly the same logic: validation is not a one-time activity but runs through the whole product lifecycle. Every model update triggered by monitoring must follow the change control procedure, just like a process parameter change.

The Challenge of Defining Acceptable Performance Parameters (Tolerance Limits)

For static software, the validation criterion is clear (the result must equal the expected value). For an AI/ML model, you need to define:

  • Which KPIs are used for monitoring? (accuracy, precision, recall, F1 score, and so on)
  • What is the lower limit of acceptable performance?
  • What is the response procedure when limits are exceeded? (alert, automatic switch to human review, mandatory retraining)
  • How often is periodic review performed? (at time intervals, or based on accumulated data volume?)

These criteria must be defined in advance in the validation plan, not after the fact; this is a basic requirement of GxP compliance.

31.3.5 Supporting Processes: Risk Management, Data Governance & Change Management

31.3.5.1 Risk Management

Risk management drives the process and controls needed for AI/ML systems. The risk framework must address both the traditional risks of the original process AND the additional risks introduced by ML intervention. Key risk considerations include:

  • Use of external vendors and data suppliers
  • Retaining sufficient technical and scientific knowledge within the organization
  • Model maturity and extent of previous real-world use
  • Changes to production data and/or errant (corrupted) input data
  • System performance, downtime, and business continuity

For static ML sub-systems where all data acquisition and annotation are supervised and validated prior to inclusion, standard data validation and change-management processes are generally sufficient.

For dynamic ML sub-systems — including online learning with continuous model parameter updates — additional controls are required: continual and robust performance evaluation, defined acceptance criteria for ongoing model changes, and heightened monitoring.

In all cases, periodic review is needed to identify bias, overfitting, and performance degradation. Review frequency may be determined by time interval or by activity triggers such as incorporation of new case data.

31.3.5.2 Data Governance

Data governance is critical to the success of ML. Not all data is created equal. The governance strategy must address:

  • Data size, quality, and prevalence — what volume is sufficient for training?
  • Whether a separate data-acquisition project is needed before model development begins
  • Format specification, annotation tools, and cleanup processes
  • How ground-truth data is generated, validated, and maintained

For image-based tasks (e.g., visual inspection), ground truth is produced by expert labeling — a time-intensive process that requires people with both technical and domain expertise.

31.3.5.3 Change Management

Change management integrates data controls into system responses and processes to maintain ongoing control. Drivers of change in AI/ML systems include:

  • Performance Requirements: Changes to improve performance — may trigger model retraining on existing datasets
  • Additional Inputs: Changes to case data or algorithms to expand compatibility and increase generalization
  • Intended Use: Changes to the significance of information or the clinical/manufacturing context

User stories and system controls, along with human intervention as a check, are effective starting points for designing the change-management workflow. Visual process maps with scenarios and mitigations help identify weak points.

Commentary: Supporting Processes — Data Governance, Risk Management, Change Control

AI/ML Risk Assessment: What Dimensions Are Added Over Conventional Systems?

A traditional GAMP 5 risk assessment asks: "If the system fails, what is the impact on patient safety, product quality and data integrity?"

AI/ML must additionally ask:

  • Does the training data represent all important use scenarios? (coverage risk)
  • Does the model have systematic bias toward particular subgroups (specific product types, specific equipment)? (bias risk)
  • Is the model's decision process transparent? Can an auditor understand the basis for a decision? (black-box risk)
  • If the model suddenly fails in production, what is the fallback procedure? (business continuity risk)

Data Governance: GMP Material Management for AI

Pharmaceutical production has strict material management: CoA confirmation, supplier qualification, incoming inspection and storage conditions. The quality of every batch of material directly affects the final product.

AI training data is the model's "raw material." Data governance is the "material management" of AI:

  • Data source verification (equivalent to supplier qualification)
  • Data quality confirmation (equivalent to incoming inspection)
  • Data version control (equivalent to batch records)
  • Labeling quality management (equivalent to in-process control)

A pharmaceutical plant without GMP material management is non-compliant; AI/ML development without data governance likewise cannot pass GxP validation.

GxP Requirements for "Human in the Loop"

This is one of the most important principles in deploying GxP AI/ML. For GxP-critical decisions (batch release, OOS determination, deviation classification), AI can provide supporting analysis and recommendations, but the final decision must be made and signed by a qualified person.

This is not only required by the current regulatory position; it is also a deeper quality principle:

  • AI models may have blind spots we have not yet discovered
  • Abnormal situations may fall outside the model's training boundaries
  • Regulatory accountability cannot be transferred to an algorithm

CDMO practice recommendation: clearly define the boundary between "AI-assisted" and "AI-decided" in SOPs, and ensure that every GxP decision is backed by the electronic signature of a qualified person.

The Complexity of AI Change Management: What Counts as a "Major Change"?

Change management for conventional software is relatively clear: a code modification triggers the change process. But what counts as a "change" in AI/ML is more complex:

  • The model updates itself automatically after new training data is added: is that a change?
  • After fine-tuning hyperparameters, model performance improves by 0.5%: is revalidation needed?
  • The distribution of the input data has drifted: the model itself has not changed, but its validity has

GAMP 5 recommends defining in advance, in the validation documents, the trigger conditions for a "major change" (for example, a performance change of more than X%) and the approval path for each type of change, to avoid disputes after the fact.

31.4 Regulatory Perspectives on AI/ML in Pharma Manufacturing

The regulatory framework for AI/ML in pharmaceutical manufacturing is actively evolving. GAMP 5 Appendix D11 references the Joint US FDA/Health Canada/UK MHRA Good Machine Learning Practice (GMLP) for Medical Device Development: Guiding Principles — currently the most comprehensive regulatory framework directly applicable to AI/ML in regulated environments.

Key FDA / MHRA GMLP Guiding Principles Referenced

The "Continuous Learning" Validation Challenge

The FDA's action plan for AI/ML-based Software as a Medical Device (SaMD) acknowledges that traditional pre-market testing cannot fully address the unique nature of continuously learning AI systems. The regulatory challenge is that a model validated at time of approval may legitimately change over time through continued learning — raising questions about re-validation triggers.

Current industry consensus positions:

  • Any significant model change (performance shift beyond defined tolerance, change in intended use, change in training data scope) should trigger a formal change assessment
  • Pre-defined performance monitoring plans with explicit alert thresholds should be part of the validation package
  • Transparency reporting to regulators for significant post-deployment model changes is emerging as expected practice

Human Oversight Requirement

Current regulatory guidance universally maintains that for GxP-critical decisions, AI should assist human judgment, not replace it. Automated AI-driven decisions in GxP-critical processes (batch release, OOS investigation conclusions) remain a high regulatory risk without explicit guidance permitting them. Decisions must be attributable to qualified human reviewers who can evaluate and accept or override AI-generated recommendations.

Commentary: Regulatory Outlook and CDMO Strategic Positioning

FDA GMLP Guiding Principles: Applicability to Pharmaceutical Manufacturing

The joint FDA/Health Canada/MHRA GMLP guiding principles cited in GAMP 5 were originally developed for medical device software (SaMD), but their principles apply directly to AI in pharmaceutical manufacturing:

  • Multidisciplinary teams (data scientists + process engineers + QA)
  • Good software engineering practices (version control, code review, test documentation)
  • Transparent performance evaluation methods
  • Post-deployment continuous monitoring and issue reporting mechanisms

What this means for CDMO QA: the validation plan (VP) and validation summary report (VSR) for an AI system should explicitly reference the relevant GMLP principles as a guiding framework.

The Regulatory Dilemma of "Continuous Learning": A Thought Experiment

Suppose you completed full validation of a visual inspection AI in January 2026. By June 2026 the model has learned from 50,000 new production data points, and its accuracy has risen from 97% to 99%.

The question: do you need to revalidate? Performance has improved, but the model's "DNA" is now completely different.

The current regulatory position leans toward yes: a change assessment and revalidation should be triggered, even though performance has improved. The reason is that no one can be fully certain the improvement has not come at the expense of performance in some edge cases.

The White Raven Case: Competitive Intelligence on CDMO AI

The European competitor White Raven CDMO has begun deploying AI solutions on its SA25 filling machines. This shows the industry frontier is moving fast.

The key question: how are they solving the GxP validation problem? Possible paths:

  • Using static AI models (periodically retrained and updated, each update handled as a controlled change), which carries the lowest regulatory risk
  • Using AI as an "advisory recommendation system" rather than a decision system, with personnel still making the final decision
  • Building validation experience first on non-GxP-critical applications (predictive maintenance)

This should also be the starting strategy for our CDMO AI roadmap: begin with predictive maintenance and non-GxP-critical monitoring, build validation capability step by step, then expand to GxP-critical applications.

Recommended CDMO AI/ML Implementation Roadmap

Phase 1 (can start now): non-GxP-critical applications

  • Predictive maintenance for filling machines
  • Decision-support tool for freeze-drying process parameter optimization
  • Anomaly detection in EM trend analysis (alerts, not decisions)

Phase 2 (after 12-18 months): supporting GxP applications

  • AI-assisted batch record review (improving the efficiency of human review)
  • Support for preliminary deviation classification

Phase 3 (long term): GxP-critical applications

  • Automated visual inspection (requires full GMLP conformance validation)
  • Real-time process analytical technology (PAT) AI models

Section Summary

Appendix D10 — Blockchain Key Takeaways

  • Blockchain provides cryptographic immutability for transaction records — but cannot guarantee the accuracy of data initially posted; input controls remain critical.
  • The blockchain is typically the source of truth, not the source of origin; all source systems (MES, LIMS) must maintain ALCOA+ compliance.
  • Four control categories are required: Input / Output / Processing / Access — with particular attention to API integrity and private key management.
  • Smart contracts must undergo code review and change management as GxP software components.
  • Regulatory guidance on whether blockchain records satisfy 21 CFR Part 11 / EU Annex 11 is still evolving — conservative validation approaches and industry engagement (ISPE GAMP CoP) are recommended.
  • Technology maturity evaluation (network age, developer community, change governance) is essential before selecting a blockchain platform for GxP use.

Appendix D11 — AI/ML Key Takeaways

  • AI/ML introduces a fundamentally different validation challenge: dynamic models change as they learn — validation must be ongoing, not a one-time activity.
  • Data is the primary asset in AI/ML: GIGO applies — data governance (classification, labeling, partitioning, provenance) is as important as algorithm selection.
  • Dataset partitioning into training / validation / test sets must be established and maintained from the concept phase — data contamination undermines all subsequent validation.
  • Model drift (gradual performance degradation) requires continuous KPI monitoring with pre-defined tolerance limits and periodic review triggers.
  • For GxP-critical decisions, AI must assist, not replace, qualified human judgment — "human in the loop" remains the regulatory baseline.
  • Start AI/ML programs with non-GxP-critical applications (predictive maintenance, EM trend analysis) to build validation competency before expanding to GxP-critical use cases.

Shared Theme: Risk-Based Validation in the Age of Emerging Technologies

Both blockchain and AI/ML represent cases where traditional SDLC and validation frameworks must be applied with adaptation. GAMP 5 2nd Edition's core message: the principles remain the same (risk-based, life-cycle approach, patient-centered), but their application must evolve with the technology.

For both technologies, the concept phase is disproportionately important — decisions made about governance, data architecture, and system boundaries at the start determine whether GxP compliance is achievable at all.

A Common Theme for Emerging Technologies: The Decisive Role of the Concept Phase

The shared lesson of blockchain and AI/ML: the quality of decisions made in the concept phase determines the validatability of the whole system. Rushing into development often means fundamental architectural problems are discovered only at the validation stage, at very high cost.

The core mindset: do not ask "what can this AI/blockchain technology do?" but "can this technology be validated and maintained in a GxP-compliant way?" Technical feasibility and regulatory feasibility must be assessed together.

7

Section 7: App O1-O13: Operation Appendices (Handover, CAPA, Security, Archiving)

p281-p346

ISPE GAMP 5 Second Edition | Appendices O, O1–O5 | Doc p281–p302

Learning Objectives

  1. Understand the purpose and scope of GAMP 5 Operational Appendices and how they extend the lifecycle beyond go-live.
  2. Define what constitutes a complete and compliant handover (App O1) from the project team to the operations/support organization.
  3. Describe the structure and GxP-specific considerations for Service Level Agreements (SLAs) and support tiers.
  4. Identify what system monitoring must cover in a GxP context beyond standard IT availability checks, including audit trail integrity and access control.
  5. Explain incident classification, the three-tier support model, and when a GxP deviation must be raised.
  6. Apply the CAPA process to computerized system failures, linking IT incident records to the quality management system.
App OAppendix O — Introduction to Operation Appendices (p281–p282)

Appendix O — Introduction to Operation Appendices

Purpose and Scope

The Operational Appendices provide guidance on how to maintain GxP computerized systems in a compliant state after go-live. These appendices ensure that GxP computerized systems continue to maintain patient safety, product quality, and the integrity of GxP data and records.

The Operational Appendices may be used as a foundation for implementing policies, procedures, and/or work instructions within the QMS. The application of good IT practices such as described by ITIL [54], supported by IT service management tools and automation, is encouraged.

Scalability Principle

Scalability should be considered: controls can be implemented at a level of formality and complexity appropriate to the individual organization and across a wide range of systems. Organizations may have straightforward controls for simple systems and more sophisticated tools and procedures for systems with increased impact, size, and complexity.

Non-Prescriptive Approach

In view of the diversity of computerized systems and organizations, it is not considered appropriate to be prescriptive regarding how operational controls are implemented. To ensure compliance with regulatory expectations, regulated companies should be able to demonstrate that they have:

  • Considered and reviewed the maintenance and support needs for each system
  • Decided what procedures, processes, and records should be established and maintained

Operational Process Relationships

Figure 32.1🔍 (source document) illustrates the relationships between operational processes. The key operational appendices — Handover (O1), Support Services (O2), System Monitoring (O3), Incident & Problem Management (O4), CAPA (O5), and Change & Configuration Management (O6) — form an interconnected lifecycle management framework.

Commentary — Introduction to the Operational Appendices

Why do we need the "Operational Appendices"?

Most of GAMP 5 focuses on the validation project phase, but passing validation is only the midpoint of the lifecycle, not the end. The Appendix O series deals specifically with maintaining compliance after go-live.

Core concept: from "validated" to "maintaining the validated state"

Regulators (FDA, EMA) are concerned not only that a system was compliant at go-live, but also that the company can demonstrate the system continuously remains in a controlled, compliant state throughout its service life (years or even decades). This is the core reason the Operational Appendices exist.

Why GAMP 5 references the ITIL framework

GAMP 5 explicitly references ITIL (IT Infrastructure Library) as the basis for good IT practice. For a CDMO, this means the IT department should not invent its own processes, but should adopt an established industry framework and layer GxP requirements on top of it.

Practical meaning of scalability

The key point for a CDMO: GAMP 5 allows the stringency of controls to be scaled according to the importance of the system.

  • Category 3 formulation software (high GxP risk) → requires a full SLA, incident classification and a CAPA linkage
  • Office productivity systems (low GxP risk) → standard IT maintenance is sufficient

Analogy: maintaining plant equipment vs maintaining computer systems

Just as sterile filling equipment needs ongoing calibration, cleaning validation and a preventive maintenance program, computerized systems need a corresponding "IT version" of maintenance. This is exactly the framework that O1-O6 establish.

App O1Appendix O1 — Handover (p283–p286)

Appendix O1 — Handover

33.1 Introduction

Handover is the process of moving the computerized system from the project phase to the operational phase. This includes the transfer of responsibilities for:

  • Maintaining the computerized system in a validated state
  • Maintaining data integrity

These responsibilities transfer to the business process owner, data owner, and system owner.

33.2 Key Requirements

Regulated companies should be able to demonstrate the formal acceptance of computerized systems following the completion of validation activities and verification of the controlled transfer of the system into operational use.

33.4 Guidance

33.4.1 General Approach

The project management approach should include a process for handover from the project phase into the operational phase. Handover activities should ensure that the business and support organization are ready to receive the new or upgraded solution following confirmation of successful validation (e.g., approval of the validation report).

Key handover activities cover:

Confirmation of the Validated State: The validated state of the computerized system should be confirmed — including closure of change controls, approval of validation reports. System specifications should be in an "as built" state reflecting the configuration baseline of the validated system. SOPs should be updated to reflect changed roles; training provided in new or modified business processes.

Managed Handover of Open Items: Open items remaining at handover (defects, incomplete training, documents awaiting approval) must be transferred in a controlled manner in accordance with risk. Incident logs, change records, CAPAs, or deviations may be raised to ensure open items are appropriately assigned. Particular attention should be given to items that could negatively impact GxP or compromise compliance status.

Completion of Data Migration: Data migration/load has been successfully completed in accordance with a documented data migration plan and data migration report.

Update of Configuration Items: Updates to the CMDB/configuration items list for the system are complete. New system modules and infrastructure components have been registered.

Establishing Support Agreements and Controls: Establishment of appropriate SLAs, quality agreements, OLAs, or contracts defining support requirements. Creation/update of operational support procedures in accordance with the O Appendices. Service management tools used in the provision of support services have been established/updated.

Knowledge Transfer: Knowledge transfer to support organizations should be conducted. This includes transfer of system lifecycle documentation, business process and data flows, and risk assessments. Knowledge transfer is not only documentation — it also includes transfer of implicit knowledge and lessons learned by the project team.

"Knowledge transfer is not only the transfer of documentation and records; it also includes the transfer of knowledge and experiences of the project team, including implicit knowledge that cannot be easily articulated in documentation and records."

Hypercare and Monitoring of Business Readiness: Depending on complexity, a period of hypercare may be required — a phased transition from project team to support team following handover. Hypercare continues until defined criteria are met (e.g., post go-live incidents diminishing in quantity and severity; support organization has demonstrated adequate knowledge). A rollback strategy should be defined in the event of a significant problem.

33.4.2 Responsibilities

  • Project Manager: Prepares the system for handover
  • Process Owner, Data Owner, System Owner: Accepts the system into operational use

Commentary — System Handover

The essence of handover: a formal transfer of responsibility

Handover is not just "handing the system over"; it is a formal transfer of legal and quality responsibility. After handover, if the system develops a data integrity problem, accountability shifts from the project manager to the System Owner.

CDMO practice warning: handover is the most commonly neglected step

Many CDMOs rush to go live as soon as validation is passed, without completing a formal handover document. This appears repeatedly in FDA 483 observations: the inspector asks, "Who is maintaining the validated state of this system?" If no formal handover record can be found, it is equivalent to having no clear owner for the system.

CDMO handover checklist (practical version)

The following items must be confirmed before handover in a CDMO environment, corresponding to GAMP 5 requirements:

Hypercare: not optional, but essential

The second edition of GAMP 5 introduced the concept of Hypercare. For complex systems (such as MES and LIMS), a hypercare period of at least 4–8 weeks is recommended, during which:

  • The project team remains on standby at all times
  • The number and severity of incidents are recorded daily
  • Hypercare is formally closed only after the incident trend is stable and declining

The end of the hypercare period should be formally documented; this is the final confirmation that handover is complete.

The challenge of transferring "tacit knowledge"

GAMP 5 stresses that knowledge transfer is not just handing over documents; it must also include the "experiential knowledge" that cannot be written into documents. For a CDMO, this means:

  • Run workshops in which IT support staff "learn by using" the system
  • Record "known issues and their temporary workarounds"
  • The project team should actively coach during the hypercare period, not merely provide documents
App O2Appendix O2 — Establishing and Managing Support Services (p287–p290)

Appendix O2 — Establishing and Managing Support Services

34.1 Introduction

The process for establishing and managing support services ensures that support services (whether internal or external) are appropriately specified and managed. This is often governed by Service Level Agreements (SLAs).

34.2 Key Requirements

When external service providers are used, there should be a formal agreement that clearly states the services to be provided and the responsibilities of the service provider and the regulated company. This applies to both external third parties and internal support functions.

34.4 Guidance

Types of Agreements

Service Level Agreement (SLA): Describes the services to be provided and service level targets; specifies responsibilities of both parties. May be established separately for individual systems or cover groups of similar systems.

Operating Level Agreement (OLA): An agreement between a service provider and another part of the same organization. Example: agreement between the primary support organization and a storage management group regarding file restoration times.

Underpinning Contract (UC): A contract between a service provider and a third party that provides goods or services integral to overall support service delivery. Example: a contract with a telecommunications provider for WAN maintenance.

Additionally, a Quality Agreement may be required, defining the quality expectations of the regulated company to be achieved by the service provider.

34.4.4 SLA Content Requirements

SLAs should address:

  • Scope of computerized systems/support services to be provided
  • Service descriptions including:
    • Software releases, patch, maintenance, enhancement releases
    • IT infrastructure and platform services
    • IT security
    • Backup and restoration
    • Business continuity and disaster recovery
    • Information security and data privacy controls
    • Data archiving and retention
    • Record and asset disposal
    • System maintenance, administration, repair, housekeeping
    • Maintenance of separate development, testing, and operations environments
    • Routine testing and calibration
    • Training
  • System support tools (e.g., service desk, security monitoring, backup)
  • Roles and responsibilities
  • Reporting, prioritization, and processing times for support requests and faults
  • Key contact details
  • Escalation processes

34.4.5 Measurement, Reporting, and Review

Service performance monitoring should be defined in terms of:

  • Key performance metrics (system and data availability, downtime, unplanned outages, response times, incidents)
  • Reporting frequency
  • Periodic review meetings

34.4.6 Managing Contract Exit

Contract exit should be planned in advance. Agreements should address:

  • Knowledge transfer
  • Documentation/records transfer
  • Software code transfer
  • Data transfer

Commentary — Support Services Management

SLAs for GxP systems: far more than "99.9% availability"

A general IT SLA is about system availability (uptime). A GxP system SLA, however, must cover much more, because regulatory requirements are stricter than commercial SLAs.

The difference between the three agreement types (SLA / OLA / UC)

It is important for a CDMO to understand the difference between these three types of agreement, because during a regulatory inspection the inspector may ask to see:

  • SLA (external): the service agreement between the CDMO and a system supplier such as SAP or Oracle
  • OLA (internal): the internal service agreement between the IT department and the QA department (for example, how quickly IT commits to restore the system)
  • UC (supply chain): the contract between the IT supplier and its sub-suppliers (for example, the contract between a cloud service provider and a data center)

The importance of a Quality Agreement

For external cloud system suppliers (such as AWS or Veeva), a Quality Agreement is needed in addition to the SLA, and it should clearly define:

  • The supplier's responsibility for retaining audit trails
  • The obligation to support audits (allowing regulators to visit and inspect)
  • Data ownership
  • The procedure for returning/deleting data when the service is terminated

The absence of a Quality Agreement is one of the common causes of FDA Warning Letters.

Contract exit plan

The second edition of GAMP 5 added contract exit provisions. For a CDMO, this means planning at the time the SLA is signed: "If we need to change suppliers, how do we ensure a complete transfer of data and system knowledge?" This is an often-overlooked risk point, especially when the contract for a cloud SaaS system (such as Veeva Vault) expires.

App O3Appendix O3 — System Monitoring (p291–p293)

Appendix O3 — System Monitoring

35.1 Introduction

System monitoring can be used to monitor and report system failures, availability, performance, configuration baseline, and information security issues. Feedback from monitoring processes and tools can be used to anticipate and respond to potential computerized system incidents and to improve the overall controls environment.

35.2 Key Requirements

The need for, and extent of, monitoring activities should be based on business risk and risk to patient safety, product quality, and data integrity. The extent of monitoring shall also be relative to the type of system and its external exposure.

35.4 Guidance

35.4.1 General Approach

System monitoring may cover a specific system, group of systems, or an IT infrastructure environment. The extent of monitoring is dependent on system complexity, business impact, patient safety, product quality, data integrity impact, and external threats such as cybersecurity threats.

35.4.2 Responsibilities

System Owner:

  • Ensures system monitoring is in place based on risk
  • Works with system administrators and IT to ensure system monitoring is implemented
  • Participates in the incident and problem management process for detected incidents

Business Process Owner / Data Owner:

  • Agrees on scope of system monitoring
  • Participates in incident and problem management process

35.4.3 System Monitoring Considerations

System monitoring should consider the following:

  • System and data availability
  • System performance and resources (CPU utilization, storage capacities)
  • Network routing, load, and failures
  • Configuration status against configuration templates
  • Batch job failures including backups
  • System interface failures
  • Instrumentation communication failures
  • Software, operating system, and hardware failures
  • Printer queue failures
  • System and process alarms and events
  • Response times
  • Security vulnerabilities
  • Intrusion detection

35.4.4 System Monitoring Tools

System monitoring should leverage automated tools wherever possible. Such tools can detect and report potential system incidents and automatically alert support organizations. Some monitoring tools include self-rectification capabilities — resources such as processing and storage capacity can automatically adjust to address performance issues. Monitoring tools can also automatically reset configuration that does not meet the current configuration template.

35.4.5 Manual Monitoring

Manual monitoring activities can be defined in and scheduled from service management tools or defined in system administration plans or procedures. Monitoring tasks, roles, frequency, and reporting requirements should be clearly defined.

Commentary — System Monitoring

GxP system monitoring ≠ general IT system monitoring

IT departments usually monitor only whether "the system is running", but the monitoring scope required for GxP compliance goes far beyond that. For a CDMO, the following three dimensions are all indispensable:

The three dimensions of GxP monitoring

Dimension 1: IT availability monitoring (traditional IT)

  • Whether the system is online and response times are normal
  • CPU/storage resource utilization
  • Whether backups ran successfully

Dimension 2: GxP data integrity monitoring (GxP-specific)

  • Whether the audit trail is complete, with no abnormal modifications
  • Whether electronic signatures are valid
  • Whether the system clock is synchronized with standard time (a common route for timestamp falsification)
  • Periodic verification of backup integrity

Dimension 3: Information security and access control monitoring (compliance requirement)

  • Abnormal login attempts (logins outside working hours, signs of account sharing)
  • Whether access rights match the latest user role list
  • Configuration drift: whether system settings have deviated from the validated baseline

Configuration drift monitoring

GAMP 5 specifically mentions monitoring whether the "configuration status conforms to the configuration template". This is a key concept for GxP systems: validation establishes a "baseline configuration", and any unauthorized configuration change may take the system out of its validated state.

Practical case: a LIMS configuration drift event

At one CDMO, IT staff changed a calculation formula parameter in the LIMS without opening a change control. Three months later, an audit found that this configuration drift had caused calculation errors in some QC test results. With a configuration monitoring tool, an alert could have been raised immediately when the problem arose, rather than being discovered three months later.

Monitoring instrument communication failures

GAMP 5 specifically lists "instrumentation communication failures" as a monitoring item. For a sterile filling CDMO, this includes:

  • Communication loss from environmental monitoring system (EMS) sensors
  • Interface failures between weighing systems and the MES
  • Communication loss between SCADA and DCS systems

If such failures are not detected promptly, critical data may be missing from the batch record, triggering a data integrity deviation.

App O4Appendix O4 — Incident Management and Problem Management (p295–p297)

Appendix O4 — Incident Management and Problem Management

36.1 Introduction — Key Definitions

An incident relates to the effect of an unplanned interruption to a service, or reduction in service quality, typically linked to a breach of the SLA, user observation, or feedback from automated monitoring tools.

A problem relates to the root cause of one or more incidents. Problems can be raised in response to a single significant incident or multiple related incidents.

"Problems are the cause and incidents are the effect."

36.2 Key Requirements

Unplanned computerized system interruptions or reductions in service quality should be reported, investigated, and resolved by support teams in order to ensure incidents are effectively managed.

36.4.1 Incident Management — Three-Tier Support Model

Level 1 Support is usually provided by IT SMEs staffing the help desk. Level 1 gathers information describing the incident, conducts basic investigations and troubleshooting, and solves common problems such as access issues, solution understanding, setup issues, and service restoration.

Level 2 Support conducts a more in-depth assessment of the incident and identifies available solutions. Level 2 is generally provided by IT SMEs outside the help desk staff. If no available solutions exist, a problem is raised and handed to Level 3 support.

Level 3 Support is provided by SMEs such as developers, DevOps, architects, engineers, or external software/IT service suppliers. Level 3 investigates potential design and implementation issues. Technical solutions may be required.

Major Incidents: Relate to serious disruption to business operations and should be resolved with greater urgency. A major incident team comprising business, technical, and quality expertise may be assembled. Business continuity and disaster recovery plans may need to be invoked.

36.4.2 Problem Management

Problem management is the process by which the root cause of one or more incidents is determined and solutions are implemented to minimize the risk of similar incidents occurring in the future. Problem management processes are integrated with change management, configuration management, training, and disaster recovery.

Resolution of a problem may also involve GxP CAPA activities.

Communication is an essential part of the incident and problem management process to ensure business users and the quality unit are aware of any disruptions or restrictions of service.

36.4.3 Determining GxP Impact

Incidents are generally reported directly to technical functions who are not necessarily SMEs with respect to the impact on patient safety, product quality, and data integrity.

Configuration Management Databases (CMDB) or other tools should identify configuration items (hardware components, application modules, business processes, records) that impact GxP. Processes for GxP determination should be agreed by business process owners, data owners, and the quality unit.

The quality unit should be informed of incidents and problems potentially impacting product quality and patient safety. They may raise a quality deviation and initiate a CAPA process. IT, system owners, process owners, and the quality unit should cooperate in ensuring effective resolution.

36.4.4 Incident and Problem Records

Incident and problem records are typically managed in IT tools and capture: incident/problem information, impact assessments, root cause analyses, and actions taken to resolve the problem including any workarounds. If changes are required to system lifecycle documentation or configuration records, they should be updated and tested in line with any changes implemented.

Commentary — Incident Management and Problem Management

Incident vs Problem: a key conceptual difference

This is a core concept of the ITIL framework and is especially important in a GxP environment:

  • Incident: the symptom experienced by users (the system slows down, a function is unavailable)
  • Problem: the root cause behind the incident (a corrupted database index, a memory leak)

One "problem" may cause multiple "incidents". The goal of problem management is to permanently eliminate the root cause, not merely to resolve individual incidents one by one.

Applying the three-tier support model at a CDMO

Analogy: triage in emergency medicine

Just as an emergency system triages by severity (minor injury → clinic, serious injury → emergency room, critical → intensive care), the three-tier IT support model is a triage mechanism that optimizes resources.

  • L1 (Helpdesk): the "clinic visit" - ask clarifying questions first and resolve simple issues such as password resets
  • L2 (system administrators/application support): the "emergency room" - diagnose in depth and provide known solutions
  • L3 (supplier/developer): the "operating room" - fundamental fixes that require changes to code or design

CMDB: the basis for GxP impact assessment

GAMP 5 emphasizes that the CMDB (Configuration Management Database) should flag which configuration items are within GxP scope. In practice, this means:

  • When building the CMDB, IT needs input from QA to flag GxP relevance
  • When an incident is logged in the helpdesk system, the system should automatically query the CMDB and display a warning that "this configuration item is GxP-relevant"
  • In this way, even IT staff who do not understand GxP know that "QA must be notified about this issue"

Common pitfall: IT does not notify QA

The most common finding in audits: IT handled an incident affecting a GxP system but did not notify QA, did not raise a deviation report, and did not open a CAPA. This usually stems from the lack of a clear "GxP impact determination process" between IT and QA. GAMP 5 requires this process to be documented and approved by QA.

When must a GxP deviation report be raised?

This is the question CDMO QA staff are asked most often. The criterion:

Guiding principle: any event that may affect data integrity, patient safety or product quality requires a deviation report and a CAPA assessment.

App O5Appendix O5 — Corrective and Preventive Action (CAPA) (p299–p302)

Appendix O5 — Corrective and Preventive Action (CAPA)

37.1 Introduction

CAPA is a process for investigating, understanding, and correcting deviations and nonconformities to address the immediate impact of the issue and to minimize the risk of recurrence. The scope and depth of CAPA plans should be developed relative to the risk of the associated deviation or nonconformity to patient safety, product quality, and data integrity.

37.2 Key Requirements

The CAPA process covers:

  • Assessment of the impact of the identified deviation or problem
  • Corrective action planning to address the impact
  • Root cause analysis to determine the cause
  • Preventive action to minimize the risk of recurrence
  • Monitoring of action completion
  • Effectiveness checks and trending analysis

Computerized system failures and issues are typically reported through the IT incident and problem management process. Incidents related to GxP configuration items should be reported as a deviation and investigated for any impact to patient safety, product quality, or data integrity. CAPAs are created in response to the investigation.

37.4 Guidance

37.4.1 General Approach

The CAPA process is part of the quality system and is used to manage corrective and preventive actions associated with GxP deviations and nonconformities. CAPAs are typically managed within an electronic QMS but may be managed as paper records.

Actions arising from CAPAs should be assigned to appropriate SMEs, including technical functions such as IT and engineering. Planned actions should be proportionate to the identified risk.

37.4.2 Responsibilities

Quality Unit:

  • Establishes the CAPA process within the quality system
  • Provides oversight of the CAPA process

Business Process Owners and System Owners:

  • Review and approve CAPAs to ensure suitability
  • Monitor the status of CAPAs relating to computerized systems within their remit

Subject Matter Experts:

  • Support CAPA planning
  • Implement assigned actions in accordance with planned completion dates

37.4.3 CAPA Process — Key Steps

Investigation: Determines the impact from the deviation, nonconformance, or problem. Must determine the impact on patient safety, product quality, and data integrity.

Corrective Action Planning: Defines the actions to address the impact — including invoking disaster recovery or restoration processes, resolving data issues, reinstating services. Must also consider actions to isolate the issue and prevent further impact.

Root Cause Analysis: Determines why the problem occurred. Conducted by relevant SMEs.

Preventive Action: Defines actions to minimize the risk of recurrence. May involve solution enhancements, bug fixes, training, SOP updates, improved system security, or improved system monitoring.

Monitoring: Ensures CAPAs are addressed within defined targets. Automated or manual monitoring of CAPA target dates ensures reminders and escalations. CAPA target date extensions should be reviewed and approved by business process owner and system owner following impact assessment. Quality Unit oversight should ensure CAPA extensions are being appropriately managed.

Effectiveness Checks: Ensure that the implemented actions are effective in addressing the root cause of the problem being addressed.

Trending: Ensures that recurring CAPA themes are investigated. Trends may be identified at an organizational, process, or system level. Learning from deviation and CAPA trends may be used to inform updates to computerized system risk assessments.

CAPA action planning should be risk-based and proportionate to the impact on patient safety, product quality, and data integrity. CAPA plans should be reviewed and approved by business process owners, system owners, and action owners.

Commentary — CAPA (Corrective and Preventive Action)

Computerized system CAPA: similarities and differences with process CAPA

The CAPA most pharmaceutical QA staff are familiar with addresses manufacturing process problems. When CAPA is applied to computerized systems, there are several key differences to be aware of:

IT incident record → deviation report → CAPA: the complete linkage

GAMP 5 requires these three record systems to form a traceable linkage:

  1. An incident record #INC-12345 exists in the IT helpdesk system (such as ServiceNow)
  2. QA opens deviation report #DEV-2026-0088 in the eQMS, referencing #INC-12345
  3. After the deviation investigation, CAPA #CAPA-2026-0045 is opened in the eQMS, referencing #DEV-2026-0088
  4. After the CAPA is completed, the effectiveness check record references #CAPA-2026-0045

This complete chain is what the inspector wants to see. If any link is broken, it is a compliance gap.

How software bugs enter the CAPA process

The question most often encountered in practice: "IT found a software bug; does it need a CAPA?" The decision flow is as follows:

  • Step 1: Does this bug affect a GxP configuration item? (check the CMDB)
  • Step 2: If so, has any GxP data already been affected? (impact assessment)
  • Step 3: If already affected → raise a deviation → start a CAPA
  • Step 4: If not affected but there is a potential risk → start a change control for the fix and assess whether a preventive CAPA is needed

Analogy: CAPA effectiveness check

Just as a clinical trial needs an endpoint to confirm efficacy, a CAPA effectiveness check needs a predefined definition of "what counts as effective":

  • Problem: audit trail interruption events occur 3 times per month
  • CAPA action: replace the monitoring tool and set up real-time alerts
  • Effectiveness criterion: within 3 months after implementation, the number of audit trail interruption events is 0

Without predefined effectiveness criteria, the CAPA is effectively incomplete.

CAPA trend analysis: learning from system to organization

GAMP 5 requires trend analysis of CAPAs, an advanced requirement that many CDMOs have not yet implemented. Recommended practice:

  • Produce a quarterly computerized system CAPA trend report for management review
  • Classification tags: training issue, system design issue, process issue, supplier issue
  • If CAPAs for the same system exceed a set threshold (for example, ≥ 3 within 6 months), trigger a system-level risk reassessment
  • Feed the trend report back into updates of the computerized system risk assessment document

Practical application of the "principle of proportionality"

GAMP 5 repeatedly emphasizes that CAPA actions should be proportionate to risk. For a CDMO:

  • Password reset issue → resolved by L1 support, no CAPA needed
  • Report calculation error → CAPA needed, but revalidation is not necessarily required
  • Missing data in a batch record → CAPA + assessment of affected batches + possible partial revalidation

Overreaction (opening a CAPA for every minor issue) is also a problem, because it dilutes attention from genuinely high-risk issues.

Transition Pitfalls — Common Pitfalls from the Validation Project to the Operational Validated State

Common Pitfalls: Project to Operational Transition

Pitfall 1: Incomplete Handover Documentation

Handover is declared complete but the support organization lacks the information necessary to maintain the system in a validated state. Common causes: validation reports approved but not transferred to operations; system specifications not reflecting the "as built" state; training completed but not documented.

Pitfall 2: No Formal Hypercare Period

The project team disappears immediately after go-live, leaving the support organization without adequate knowledge to handle early operational issues. GAMP 5 recommends a defined hypercare period with explicit exit criteria before full handover to operations.

Pitfall 3: GxP Impact Determination Not Documented

No written procedure defines which IT incidents must be escalated to the quality unit. IT resolves GxP-impacting incidents through the helpdesk without QA involvement, creating undocumented deviations.

Pitfall 4: IT Records and QMS Records Not Linked

IT manages incidents in ServiceNow; QA manages deviations in TrackWise — but no cross-reference links the records. During an audit, the chain from incident to deviation to CAPA cannot be demonstrated.

Pitfall 5: SLA Does Not Address GxP Requirements

The SLA with the IT service provider covers standard uptime and response times but does not address audit trail retention, configuration change notification requirements, or the obligation to notify QA of GxP incidents.

Pitfall 6: No Defined Rollback Strategy

A significant post-go-live problem is discovered but there is no documented rollback procedure and no pre-defined criteria for invoking it. The organization struggles with decision-making under pressure.

Commentary — Systematic Risk Management in the Handover Transition Period

Why is the transition period the most dangerous?

The early period after go-live (the first 3–6 months) has the highest incident rate. At this time the project team has disbanded and the support team is not yet familiar with the system, so a vacuum easily arises in which "nobody knows how to handle it".

CDMO action list: reducing risk in the handover transition period

  • Complete the System Operation Manual before handover, covering common issues and known defects
  • Set a documented Hypercare period (4–8 weeks recommended for complex systems) and define exit criteria
  • Flag GxP systems in the IT helpdesk system and set up a workflow that automatically notifies QA
  • Explicitly include a "GxP incident notification obligation" clause in the SLA
  • Before handover, hold 1–2 "Tabletop Exercises" in which IT and QA rehearse the incident response process together
  • Define rollback procedures and complete their document approval before handover
  • Establish regular IT–QA joint meetings (monthly recommended) to review system monitoring reports and unresolved incidents together

Three maturity levels of QMS and IT system integration

Level 1 (manual): IT finds a GxP incident → notifies QA by phone → QA manually creates a deviation record in the eQMS → manually references the IT incident number

Level 2 (semi-automated): the IT system (ServiceNow) is configured with rules so that if an incident is flagged as GxP, an email with an incident summary is automatically sent to QA

Level 3 (fully integrated): the IT system is integrated with the eQMS (TrackWise/Veeva) via API, so a draft deviation record can be generated from an IT incident with one click, with two-way status tracking

Most CDMOs are currently at Level 1; reaching Level 2 is an achievable short-term goal.

Section Summary (Appendices O, O1–O5)

English Summary

The GAMP 5 Operational Appendices (O–O5) establish a comprehensive framework for maintaining computerized systems in a validated, compliant state throughout their operational life. The lifecycle does not end at go-live — it continues through structured handover, ongoing support management, proactive monitoring, and integrated quality processes.

  • App O: Establishes the purpose and scalability principle of all operational appendices, grounded in ITIL good practices with GxP overlay requirements.
  • App O1 (Handover): Requires formal, documented transfer of validated state, open items, knowledge, and support agreements from project team to operations. Hypercare is a key new element in the 2nd Edition.
  • App O2 (Support Services): Mandates SLAs that cover not just uptime but also GxP-specific requirements including audit trail retention, change notification, and quality agreement provisions. OLAs and UCs form the supporting agreement ecosystem.
  • App O3 (System Monitoring): Risk-based monitoring must cover availability, performance, configuration baseline, security, and — uniquely for GxP — audit trail integrity, instrumentation communication, and configuration drift.
  • App O4 (Incident & Problem Management): Three-tier support model (L1/L2/L3); critical GxP requirement to assess every incident for GxP impact using CMDB, and to link GxP incidents to the deviation and CAPA process via quality unit involvement.
  • App O5 (CAPA): Full CAPA lifecycle applied to IT failures: investigation, corrective action, root cause analysis, preventive action, monitoring, effectiveness checks, and trending — all proportionate to GxP risk and documented in the QMS.

Summary

GAMP 5 Operational Appendices O to O5 establish a complete framework for keeping computerized systems compliant and in a validated state throughout the operational lifecycle. Passing validation is only the starting point; compliance maintenance runs through the entire service life of the system.

  • Appendix O: Establishes the purpose and scalability principle of all the operational appendices, using ITIL as the underlying framework with a GxP requirements layer on top.
  • Appendix O1 (Handover): Requires the transfer of responsibility from the project team to the operations organization to be formally documented, including confirmation of validated status, handover of open items, and knowledge transfer. Hypercare is an important new concept in the second edition.
  • Appendix O2 (Support Services): SLAs must cover GxP-specific requirements (audit trail retention, change notification obligations, quality agreement clauses), not just general IT availability metrics.
  • Appendix O3 (System Monitoring): Risk-based monitoring looks beyond whether the system is online; it also monitors audit trail integrity, access control status, configuration drift and instrument communication anomalies.
  • Appendix O4 (Incident and Problem Management): The core of the three-tier support model is GxP impact assessment: use the CMDB to determine whether each incident affects GxP, and link relevant incidents to QA's deviation report and CAPA processes.
  • Appendix O5 (CAPA): The full CAPA lifecycle for computerized systems: investigation → corrective action → root cause analysis → preventive action → monitoring → effectiveness check → trend analysis, executed throughout in proportion to GxP risk and recorded in the quality management system.
ISPE GAMP 5 (2nd Edition) | Appendices O6–O13 | p303–p346

Learning Objectives

  1. Distinguish operational change management (post-go-live) from project change management, and understand how the same change process governance shifts the moment a system is released to production.
  2. Identify the six GAMP 5 change types (standard, like-for-like, system administration, emergency, temporary, global) and apply the correct control pathway for each.
  3. Design a risk-based periodic review programme — including frequency determination, review scope, and documented outcome — and understand why this activity is commonly under-resourced in CDMOs.
  4. Apply the RPO/RTO framework to backup strategy design, and explain why backup testing (restore verification) — not backup creation — is the audit-critical activity.
  5. Evaluate security management requirements under GAMP 5 and 21 CFR Part 11, covering access control, MFA, shared accounts, audit trail integrity, and security patch management.
  6. Explain electronic records archiving obligations — including readability over 30+ year retention periods, retrieval testing, and the distinction between archive and backup — and the regulatory expectations under 21 CFR Part 11 and EU GMP Annex 11.

Appendix O6 — Operational Change and Configuration Management

38.4 Guidance

38.4.1 Responsibilities

Process Owner: Ensures that change control and configuration management processes and procedures are in place.

System Owner: Coordinates changes and evaluates the technical impact of the change.

Quality Unit: Ensures that the process and procedures are followed.

Change Plan Task and Action Owners: Ensure completion in accordance with defined timelines.

38.4.2 General Approach

The point of transfer from project change management (see Appendix M8) to operational change management should be clearly defined and documented, e.g., in the computerized system validation report, system release documentation, or handover reports. Operational change management should start no later than handover for operational use.

38.4.2.1 Change Request / Change Proposal and Evaluation

The change request or change proposal is the initial definition of the change outlining the business and/or technical reason for the change and the general scope. The change may be accepted or rejected based on business value; the decision outcome should be communicated to key stakeholders.

38.4.2.2 Change Specification

The change specification defines the change in terms of impacted configuration items (e.g., business process, data, functionality, configuration, service, infrastructure). It may be defined in the change record and/or supported by system life cycle records.

38.4.2.3 Impact and Risk Assessment

Risk assessments should determine whether the change impacts patient safety, product quality, or data integrity. Existing risk assessment records should be updated to reflect the change. Impact assessments should consider:

  • Solution regression
  • Data
  • Business process and data flows (SOPs)
  • System life cycle documentation and records
  • Training
  • SLAs

38.4.2.4 Change Plan

The change plan defines actions and tasks required to implement the change, addressing the change specification and impact/risk assessment outputs. Responsibilities for all parties — including the regulated company and external service providers — should be clearly defined.

38.4.2.5 Backout Plan

Backout plans should be considered for complex and/or high-risk changes. They define how the solution/service is reverted to a known state if the change has an unexpected impact.

38.4.2.6 Authorize Implementation

The change should be authorized prior to implementation to confirm the adequacy of change specifications, impact and risk assessments, change plans, and backout plans. Authorization may take the form of review and approval of the change record.

38.4.2.7 Execute Change Plan

Actions and tasks defined in the change plan are executed, including but not limited to:

  • Update system documentation and/or records
  • Update system manuals/SOPs/work instructions/SLAs
  • Implement functional, configuration, and technical changes (where possible within a validation/test environment)
  • Creation/update and execution of tests/verification tasks to confirm correct outcome of the change
  • User and support organization training

38.4.2.8 Release to Operations

Once the change has been verified, the change and associated change records are reviewed to confirm all actions in the change plan have been fully executed. Installation into the operational environment should follow documented installation procedures. Knowledge transfer to support organizations should be completed before transition to operations.

38.4.3 Types of Change

  • Standard/Routine Changes: Considered low risk to patient safety, product quality, and data integrity. Work instructions, system administration plans, or service requests may define implementation tasks. A record of the change — including any verification tasks — should be maintained.
  • Like-for-Like Replacements/Repairs: Typically low-risk changes controlled by maintenance or service management procedures. Approved replacements include like-for-like (identical components) and like-for-kind (similar pre-approved components with the same functional characteristics).
  • System Administration Changes: Some system administration activities involve changes to system components. These should be defined in system administration procedures (see Appendix O12).
  • Emergency Changes: Implemented when delay may result in greater impact, e.g., data loss, data integrity impact, or impact on system availability due to a cyber security attack. Emergency changes may require retrospective application of the change process to document the change.
  • Temporary Changes: Planned for a limited period. Particular attention should be paid to ensuring temporary changes are "rolled back" and properly reviewed before being made permanent. They should be monitored to ensure they do not remain beyond the planned duration.
  • Global Changes: Changes to global systems and services may require additional governance to minimize the impact of locally identified changes on other functions and regions.

38.4.4 Business, IT, Engineering, and Service Provider Change Processes

Changes often involve interdependencies between business organizations, internal IT organizations, and external service providers. Responsibilities for different aspects of the change should be clearly defined and agreed, e.g., through a quality agreement, operational agreement, or SLA.

Where IaaS, PaaS, and/or SaaS is used, risk-based supplier assessments and monitoring should be considered to ensure that the service provider has appropriate change and configuration management processes in place. Where a SaaS service provider controls the release of new software versions, the regulated company should evaluate the impact of new releases on business processes and ensure actions are taken to accept the new release.

38.4.5 Configuration Management

Operational configuration management should start with the baseline configuration that is part of system handover. Configuration management consists of four key activities:

  • Configuration Identification — WHAT to keep under control
  • Configuration Control — HOW to perform the control
  • Configuration Status Accounting — HOW to document the control
  • Configuration Evaluation — HOW to verify that control

Configuration items with GxP impact should be identified. The list of configuration items and their status (e.g., version) is called the configuration baseline and serves as the reference for the validated state of a system. Configuration items should be subject to version control to provide traceability to any implemented changes.

38.4.6 Cloud Service Provider Changes

Changes may be scheduled by IaaS/PaaS/SaaS providers with limited opportunity to evaluate impact. The regulated company should be aware of the service provider's release schedule and evaluate the impact of releases on business processes and regulated data. Where appropriate, testing should be conducted to confirm business processes have not been adversely impacted.

38.4.7 Software Development Change Management

Software development processes commonly follow an iterative or Agile development approach. Such processes are followed to manage the implementation of software enhancements and bug fixes. Suppliers and service providers may not differentiate change management from normal software development processes.

Commentary: Core Thinking on Operational Change Management

Project Change vs. Operational Change: A Watershed on the Same Day

This is where pharma IT and validation departments often get confused. GAMP 5 is explicit: the moment a system is handed over (handover) for production use, change control must switch from "project change management" to "operational change management".

The underlying logic is the same (assess impact, authorize, implement, verify), but responsibilities, documentation formats and approval levels are all different.

Practical advice: the validation report or system handover document should clearly record the date of this switch (the go-live date) and cite the version number of the first operational change SOP.

Analogy: A Construction Site vs. a Hotel That Has Opened

The project phase is like a construction site: engineers can change the drawings at any time without notifying guests. Once the hotel opens (go-live), even replacing a single door requires notifying operations, assessing the impact on guests and producing work records. That is the spirit of operational change management.

Emergency Change: A Common Audit Finding

Emergency changes are where audit deficiencies appear most often. GAMP 5 allows work to be done first and documented afterwards (retrospective documentation), provided that:

  • The SOP clearly defines what counts as an "emergency"
  • The retrospective documentation is completed within a defined time (typically 24-72 hours)
  • A full impact assessment is still performed afterwards

Inspectors often ask: "Within how many hours does your emergency change SOP require retrospective documentation to be completed? Do your actual records comply?" If you cannot answer, you have effectively declared the process to be a dead letter.

Temporary Change: The Most Easily Forgotten Time Bomb

Because they are "only temporary", temporary changes tend to escape control. GAMP 5 stresses two requirements:

  • There must be an expiry and rollback mechanism — who is responsible for confirming that the temporary change was reverted within the planned time?
  • It cannot be extended indefinitely — if it needs to be kept permanently, it must be re-assessed through the formal change process

In CDMO practice, it is quite common for a temporary workaround to end up as a permanent configuration, which is a breeding ground for data integrity risk.

Configuration Baseline: The Anchor of the Validated State

A configuration baseline is a "snapshot" of the system at a point in time (version numbers of all components, setting values and interface definitions). It is the reference for judging whether the system is still in a validated state.

The configuration baseline must be updated after every change. If the actual system state does not match the recorded baseline, the system has effectively left the validated state, which is a serious GxP problem.

SaaS Vendor Automatic Upgrades: A Passive Validation Challenge

Many companies use cloud SaaS (such as SAP or Veeva Vault), where the vendor pushes new versions on a regular basis and the company cannot stop them. GAMP 5 requires the company to proactively assess the impact of each release on its business processes. Even if you have no right to choose whether to update, you still have the responsibility to assess and document.

Change Type Quick Reference

Appendix O7 — Retired

39 Appendix O7 (Retired)

Repair activities are typically managed as standard changes, service requests, and/or system administration tasks. As such, the requirements for repair activities are defined in Appendix O6. Appendix O7 has been removed from this Guide.

Commentary

O7 Retired
