Agreement must be reached between all affected organizations. Representatives typically involved include:

  • Business process owners
  • Compliance / Quality / Regulatory
  • Legal
  • IT and Engineering
  • Purchasing groups and in some cases Finance

Key documentation update tasks for stakeholders:

  • Business process documentation
  • Policies, procedures, work instructions, test methods
  • Training materials and user manuals
  • Batch records (if applicable)
  • Archived records/data
  • Contracts, system interfaces, validation/qualification records
  • Record retention schedules

Training requirements must be evaluated against all changes made to QMS, ways of working, or documentation — at both organizational and system levels.

Commentary: Organizational Change Management for CSV Programs

Why Organizational Change Is a High-Risk Event for CSV

A validated computerized system exists within a specific organizational environment: a specific system owner, SOP owners, IT support staff and supplier relationship managers. When a major organizational change occurs, all of these roles may change at the same time, while the system itself (hardware, software, configuration) may remain exactly the same. But all the people and procedures on which maintenance of its validated state depends have been broken.

The Real Challenges of IT Restructuring / Outsourcing

Imagine a pharmaceutical company that decides to outsource its IT infrastructure to a third-party IT service provider:

  • The original in-house IT team (who knew every historical decision about the MES) has left
  • The new outsourcing provider does not understand GxP requirements and operates only according to a general IT SLA
  • Patch management, backup verification and access control administration for the system are now performed by people unfamiliar with GxP

The result is validated state drift: the system is still running, but no one ensures that it is still operating in a validated state.

Special Risks for a CDMO in M&A Scenarios

When a CDMO is acquired by a large pharmaceutical company, or acquires other CDMOs itself, common GxP risks include:

  • Pressure to integrate systems: IT is eager to consolidate systems (for example a single ERP), but the integration is itself a major change to validated systems and requires revalidation
  • SOP conflicts: the two organizations each have different CSV SOPs; a decision is needed on which set to follow, and all personnel must be trained
  • Restarting supplier audits: after an acquisition, the new parent company may have different supplier audit requirements, requiring all critical system suppliers to be re-audited
  • Transfer of record retention responsibility: how is continuity ensured for the retention of client batch records and validation documents after the contract is transferred?

Emergency Response to Supplier Failure

This is the hardest scenario to prepare for in advance, and the one with the most serious consequences. The key action list:

  1. Activate the business continuity plan immediately (if one exists)
  2. Invoke the software source code escrow agreement: if the contract contains a source code escrow clause, apply for release immediately
  3. Download all supporting documentation: technical manuals, validation documents and system configuration records, because the supplier's servers may be shut down at any time
  4. Evaluate existing maintenance contracts: the annual maintenance fee has been paid but the supplier has gone bankrupt; this is a legal issue as well as a compliance issue
  5. Decide on a replace-or-maintain strategy: if the system is to remain in use, find a third-party vendor capable of maintaining it

CSV Impact Assessment Framework for Organizational Change

We recommend carrying out the following CSV impact assessment whenever a major organizational change occurs:

  1. Inventory: list all affected GxP computerized systems and mark their GxP impact level
  2. Re-mapping of roles and responsibilities: who will be responsible for the system owner, SOP owner and IT contact roles under the new organizational structure?
  3. Procedure review: do SOPs that involve organizational structure or personnel responsibilities need to be updated?
  4. Training needs assessment: have the new responsible personnel received system-specific training?
  5. Contract review: are system support contracts and license agreements still valid after the organizational change?
  6. Enhanced monitoring during the transition: during the organizational transition, increase the frequency of event monitoring and set up a rapid escalation mechanism

Section Summary: Key Points of Appendices S1–S6

  • S1 — ASTM E2500: GAMP 5 and ASTM E2500 share the same science- and risk-based philosophy. ASTM E2500 focuses on manufacturing equipment; GAMP 5 covers all computerized systems. "Verification" replaces separate IQ/OQ as an integrated activity; the key requirement is evidence that critical-to-quality requirements have been met. The core of both is the same: a patient-risk-centered approach, leveraging supplier testing and reducing duplicated activities. The terminology may differ, but the documented evidence must be sufficient.
  • S2 — Electronic Production Records: EPR ≠ Batch Report — EPR is distributed data; the batch report is the human-readable output. Data audit trails are mandatory; they must be reviewed as part of the batch review process. The end user is always ultimately responsible for validated state, even for cloud-hosted systems. The audit trail must be reviewed as part of batch record review; RBE is compliant, but its filtering logic itself must be validated.
  • S3 — Spreadsheets: Unvalidated GxP spreadsheets are a top FDA 483 observation. Minimum requirements: formula verification, version control, access control, change control. Macros = Category 5 software. Spreadsheets with audit trail requirements should be replaced with proper database systems. Excel is not a safe option for GxP unless it has full validation and controls; a spreadsheet containing macros is equivalent to Category 5 software.
  • S4 — Patch Management: Enterprise security risk generally takes precedence over GxP risk. Quality unit approves the patch management process but not individual patch decisions. A documented risk-based decision for each patch — including the choice of strategy and testing scope — is required. Configuration records must show patch levels at any point in time. Only a QA-approved procedure plus risk records allows patches to be applied quickly while remaining compliant.
  • S5 — Retired: Content from GAMP 5 1st Edition S5 (Managing Quality within an Outsourced IS/IT Environment) has been moved to Appendix M11 in the 2nd Edition. The related content has been moved to Appendix M11.
  • S6 — Organizational Change: M&A, outsourcing, and supplier loss all threaten validated state. A CSV-specific organizational change impact assessment is essential. Training requirements must be re-evaluated. Stakeholders from Business, QA, Legal, IT, and Finance must all be engaged. Organizational change is not just an HR matter: it directly threatens the compliance status of validated systems and requires proactive QA involvement.
ISPE GAMP 5, 2nd Edition | p383–p404 | Section 08b

Learning Objectives

  1. Identify the key regulatory references cited in GAMP 5 (FDA 21 CFR Part 11, EU Annex 11, ICH Q9/Q10) and understand why each is foundational to computerized system validation.
  2. Distinguish between "validation," "qualification," and "verification" as defined in the GAMP 5 glossary, and apply the correct term in validation documentation.
  3. Explain the roles of "regulated company," "supplier," "system owner," and "process owner," and describe how responsibilities differ.
  4. Use the GAMP 5 glossary and reference list to correctly cite standards and define key terms during regulatory inspections or validation document preparation.

Commentary — Guide to the References

Core Regulatory Documents — Why Cite These Standards?

Appendix G1 of GAMP 5 Second Edition lists more than 100 referenced documents. For CDMO practitioners, the three most important regulatory cornerstones are the following:

21 CFR Part 11 (US FDA)
Scope: electronic records and electronic signatures. Core requirement: electronic records must be trustworthy, reliable, and equivalent to paper records, and electronic signatures must carry legal weight.
Common misunderstanding: Part 11 does not require "every system to have electronic signatures"; rather, when you choose to use electronic records, you must meet these requirements.

EU GMP Annex 11 (European Union)
Scope: computerized systems within the GMP scope. Key difference from 21 CFR Part 11: Annex 11 covers a broader system life cycle concept, including supplier assessment, acceptance testing, periodic review, and data integrity requirements.
Key point: in some respects Annex 11 is stricter than 21 CFR Part 11, for example in explicitly requiring supplier audits.

ICH Q9 (Quality Risk Management)
The scientific basis of the GAMP 5 risk-based approach. ICH Q9 provides the official definitions of terms such as "harm," "risk," "severity," and "detectability," and the GAMP 5 glossary cites these definitions directly.
Why it matters: when you perform risk assessments in validation documents, your methodology must map to the ICH Q9 framework.

ICH Q10 — The Overall Framework for the Pharmaceutical Quality System

ICH Q10 (Pharmaceutical Quality System) complements ICH Q9. Q10 describes the requirements for maintaining a quality system throughout the product life cycle, and CSV work under GAMP 5 must operate within the Q10 framework.

ASTM E2500-20 is equally important: it provides the standard basis for "Verification" as an alternative route to CSV, and is an important foundation of the ISPE Vol. 5 C&Q Guide.

Principles for Using the GAMP 5 References

  • When a document cites "ICH Q9 [14]," the number in square brackets is the reference number in Appendix G1.
  • When citing GAMP 5 in your own validation documents, the correct format is: "ISPE GAMP 5 Second Edition, 2022, www.ispe.org".
  • Auditors may ask you to demonstrate that you understand the specific requirements of the documents you cite, not merely that they are listed in the references.
  • PIC/S PI 011-3 (Guidance on computerized systems) applies to EU GMP inspections and is an important supplement to GAMP 5.

CDMO Practical Application — Citing Regulations Correctly in Validation Documents

Regulatory basis section of the Validation Master Plan (VMP):

  • List all applicable regulations: 21 CFR Part 11, EU GMP Annex 11, ICH Q9, ICH Q10
  • State the market each regulation applies to (US, EU, Japan, etc.)
  • Cite GAMP 5 as the methodological guidance

In risk assessment documents:

  • State explicitly that the risk assessment method is based on ICH Q9 principles
  • Define the terms used (Harm, Hazard, Risk, Severity, Detectability)

Common audit question:
"Which guideline is your CSV approach based on?" — Answer: GAMP 5 2nd Edition (2022), supported by EU GMP Annex 11 and 21 CFR Part 11.

21 CFR Part 11 vs. EU Annex 11 — Similar but Not the Same

A CDMO selling to both the FDA and EU markets must comply with both sets of requirements, applying the stricter one where they differ.

Appendix G2, Section 53.1 — Acronyms & Abbreviations

G2 — Acronyms and Abbreviations

Commentary — The 20 Most Important GAMP 5 Acronyms

Acronym Questions Auditors Often Ask

During an FDA or EU inspection, auditors may ask at random what an acronym means, to confirm your CSV knowledge base:

  • "What is COTS software under GAMP 5?" — Commercial off-the-shelf software, whose suitability has been proven by a broad base of commercial users, which can simplify the validation strategy.
  • "What is the difference between URS and FS?" — The URS describes the users' business requirements; the FS describes how the system fulfils those requirements.
  • "What does GxP cover?" — A collective term for all applicable regulatory standards, including GMP, GLP, GCP, GDP, and GVP.
  • "What is ALCOA+?" — The nine principles of data integrity: attributable, legible, contemporaneous, original, accurate, complete, consistent, enduring, and available.

CSA vs. CSV — Key Differences Between the New and Old Approaches

In 2022 the FDA issued the draft Computer Software Assurance (CSA) guidance as a complementary direction to traditional CSV:

  • CSV: emphasizes documentation completeness, the three IQ/OQ/PQ stages, and detailed test scripts
  • CSA: emphasizes critical thinking, risk-based determination of testing scope, and less unnecessary documentation
  • GAMP 5 2nd Edition supports both approaches, allowing regulated companies to choose the one that suits them according to risk.

Appendix G2, Section 53.2 — Definitions

G2 — Definitions (A–M)

Acceptance Criteria
Source: ASTM E2500 [8]
The criteria that a system, component must satisfy in order to be accepted by a user, customer or other authorized entity.
Acceptance Test
Source: IEEE [96]
Testing conducted to determine whether or not a system satisfies its acceptance criteria and to enable the customer to determine whether or not to accept the system.
Application Software
Source: ISO/IEC 13066 [97]
Software or a program that is specific to the solution of an application problem.
Audit
Source: ISO 9000 [1]
Systematic, independent, and documented process for obtaining audit evidence and evaluating it objectively to determine the extent to which agreed criteria are fulfilled.
Batch Record
Source: GAMP 5
The set of records of all relevant process information in any physical or electronic format.
Blockchain
Source: Merriam-Webster [98]
A digital database containing information (such as records of financial transactions) that can be simultaneously used and shared within a large decentralized, publicly accessible network.
Business Continuity Planning
Source: ISO/IEC 27031:2011 [99]
Documented procedures that guide organizations to respond, recover, resume, and restore to a pre-defined level of operation following disruption.
Calibration
Source: ICH Q7 [100]
The demonstration that a particular instrument or device produces results within specified limits by comparison with those produced by a reference or traceable standard over an appropriate range of measurements.
Change Management
Source: ICH Q10 [21]
A systematic approach to proposing, evaluating, approving, implementing and reviewing changes.
Commercial Off-the-Shelf Software (COTS)
Source: IEEE [96]
Software defined by a market-driven need, commercially available, and whose fitness for use has been demonstrated by a broad spectrum of commercial users. Also known as COTS.
Computer System
Source: IEEE [96]
A system containing one or more computers and associated software.
Computerized System
Source: GAMP 5
A broad range of systems including, but not limited to, automated manufacturing equipment, automated laboratory equipment, process control and process analytical, manufacturing execution, laboratory information management, manufacturing resource planning, clinical trials data management, vigilance and document management systems. The computerized system consists of the hardware, software, and network components, together with the controlled functions and associated documentation.
Computerized System Validation
Source: GAMP 5
Achieving and maintaining compliance with applicable GxP regulations and fitness for intended use by:
  • The adoption of principles, approaches, and life cycle activities within the framework of validation plans and reports
  • The application of appropriate operational controls throughout the life of the system
Critical Process Parameter (CPP)
Source: ICH Q8 [84]
A process parameter whose variability has an impact on a critical quality attribute and therefore should be monitored or controlled to ensure the process produces the desired quality.
Critical Quality Attribute (CQA)
Source: ICH Q8 [84]
A physical, chemical, biological or microbiological property or characteristic that should be within an appropriate limit, range, or distribution to ensure the desired product quality.
Detectability
Source: ICH Q9 [14]
The ability to discover or determine the existence, presence, or fact of a hazard.
Factory Acceptance Test (FAT)
Source: IEEE [96]
An Acceptance Test in the Supplier's factory, usually involving the Customer. See also Acceptance Test. Contrast to Site Acceptance Test.
GxP Compliance
Source: GAMP 5
Meeting all applicable pharmaceutical and associated life-science regulatory requirements.
GxP Regulated Computerized System
Source: GAMP 5
Computerized systems that are subject to GxP regulations. The regulated company must ensure that such systems comply with the appropriate regulations.
GxP Regulation
Source: GAMP 5
The underlying international pharmaceutical requirements, such as those set forth in the US FD&C Act, US PHS Act, FDA regulations, EU Directives, Japanese regulations, or other applicable national legislation or regulations under which a company operates. These include but are not limited to:
  • Good Manufacturing Practice (GMP) — pharmaceutical, including API, veterinary, and blood
  • Good Clinical Practice (GCP)
  • Good Laboratory Practice (GLP)
  • Good Distribution Practice (GDP)
  • Good Quality Practice (GQP)
  • Good Pharmacovigilance Practice (GVP)
  • Medical Device Regulations
  • Prescription Drug Marketing Act (PDMA)
Harm
Source: ICH Q9 [14]
Damage to health, including the damage that can occur from loss of product quality or availability.
Hazard
Source: ICH Q9 [14]
The potential source of harm (ISO/IEC Guide 51).
Incident
Source: GAMP 5
Operational event which is not part of standard operation.
Middleware
Source: GAMP 5
Software that provides common services such as communication and data management to software applications beyond those available from the operating system.

Commentary — Key Definitions Explained (Part 1)

Distinguishing the Three Most Easily Confused Terms

Validation vs. Qualification vs. Verification

In everyday usage these three terms are often used interchangeably, but GAMP 5 gives them a precise hierarchical relationship:

  • Verification: the umbrella concept used by ASTM E2500, "a systematic approach to verify that manufacturing systems are fit for intended use"; it covers all means of confirmation.
  • Validation: GAMP 5's definition for Computerized System Validation, emphasizing "achieving and maintaining GxP compliance and fitness for intended use" across the entire life cycle.
  • Qualification: specifically the stage-by-stage confirmation activities such as IQ/OQ/PQ for equipment or systems; it is one component of Validation.

In short: Verification ⊇ Validation ⊇ Qualification (from broadest to narrowest scope)

The Formal Definition of Computerized System — Broader Than You Think

GAMP 5 explicitly defines a computerized system as comprising hardware + software + network components + controlled functions + associated documentation.

This means even a simple PLC controller (including its program logic) is a computerized system and needs an appropriate validation approach. Computerized systems commonly found at a CDMO include MES, LIMS, EDMS, ERP, DCS/PLC, weighing systems, and others.

The Regulatory Scope of GxP — Understood at a Glance

A CDMO is mainly subject to GMP, but if it manufactures investigational medicinal products for clinical trials, it must also consider GCP requirements for data integrity.

Terms Sourced from ICH Q9 — Auditors' Favorites

The GAMP 5 glossary cites many terms directly from ICH Q9. Auditors often ask:

  • Harm: damage to health, including the damage that can occur from loss of product quality or availability.
  • Hazard: the potential source of harm (not the same as Harm).
  • Risk = Probability × Severity: the combination of the probability of occurrence of harm and the severity of that harm.
  • Detectability: the ability to discover or determine the existence, presence, or fact of a hazard (helps lower the overall risk score).

COTS Software — A Supermarket Food Analogy

COTS (commercial off-the-shelf software) is like ready-made food sold in a supermarket: the manufacturer (supplier) has already produced it at scale and it has been proven in the market. You (the regulated company) do not need to build it from scratch, but you do need to confirm that it fits your specific needs (URS) and configure it correctly in your environment.

By contrast, custom-developed software (Category 5) is like cooking from scratch: it requires full development life cycle control.

G2 — Definitions (N–Z)

Network
Source: FDA [101]
A system [transmission channels and supporting hardware and software] that connects several remotely located computers via telecommunications.
Periodic Review
Source: GAMP 5
A documented assessment of the documentation, procedures, records, and performance of a computer system to determine whether it is still in a validated state and what actions, if any, are necessary to restore its validated state. The frequency of review is dependent upon the systems complexity, criticality, and rate of change.
Pharmacovigilance
Source: EMA [103]
Science and activities relating to the detection, assessment, understanding and prevention of adverse effects or any other medicine-related problem.
Process
Source: ISO 9000 [1]
Set of interrelated or interacting activities that use inputs to deliver an intended result.
Process Analytical Technology (PAT)
Source: ICH Q8 [84]
A system for designing, analyzing, and controlling manufacturing through timely measurements (i.e., during processing) of critical quality and performance attributes of raw and in-process materials and processes with the goal of ensuring final product quality.
Process Owner
Source: GAMP 5
This is the owner of the business process or processes being managed. The process owner is ultimately responsible for ensuring that the computerized system and its operation is in compliance and fit for intended use in accordance with applicable company Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs). The process owner may also be the system owner. The process owner may be the de facto owner of the data residing on the system (data owner) and therefore, ultimately responsible for the integrity of the data. Process owners are typically the head of the functional unit using the system. (cf. System Owner)
Process Validation
Source: FDA [104]
The collection and evaluation of data, from the process design stage through commercial production, which establishes scientific evidence that a process is capable of consistently delivering quality products.
Product Lifecycle
Source: ICH Q9 [14]
All phases in the life of the product from the initial development through marketing until the product's discontinuation.
Quality (ICH Q9)
Source: ICH Q9 [14]
The degree to which a set of inherent properties of a product, system or process fulfills requirements.
Quality by Design (QbD)
Source: ICH Q8 [84]
A systematic approach to development that begins with predefined objectives and emphasizes product and process understanding and process control, based on sound science and quality risk management.
Quality Management System (QMS)
Source: ISO 9000 [1]
Part of a management system with regard to quality. (A management system is a set of interrelated or interacting elements of an organization to establish policies and objectives, and processes to achieve those objectives.)
Quality Risk Management (QRM)
Source: ICH Q9 [14]
A systematic process for the assessment, control, communication and review of risks to the quality of the drug (medicinal) product across the product lifecycle.
Risk
Source: ICH Q9 [14]
The combination of the probability of occurrence of harm and the severity of that harm (ISO/IEC Guide 51).
Risk Assessment
Source: ICH Q9 [14]
A systematic process of organizing information to support a risk decision to be made within a risk management process. It consists of the identification of hazards and the analysis and evaluation of risks associated with exposure to those hazards.
Risk Management
Source: ICH Q9 [14]
The systematic application of quality management policies, procedures, and practices to the tasks of assessing, controlling, communicating and reviewing risk.
Severity
Source: ICH Q9 [14]
A measure of the possible consequences of a hazard.
Site Acceptance Test (SAT)
Source: IEEE [96]
An Acceptance Test at the Customer's site, usually involving the Customer. See also Acceptance Test, contrast to Factory Acceptance Test.
Software Life Cycle
Source: NIST [81]
Period of time beginning when a software product is conceived and ending when the product is no longer available for use. The software life cycle is typically broken into phases denoting activities such as requirements, design, programming, testing, installation, and operation and maintenance.
Subject Matter Expert (SME)
Source: ASTM E2500 [8]
Those individuals with specific expertise in a particular area or field. Subject Matter Experts should take the lead role in the verification of computerized systems. Subject Matter Expert responsibilities include planning and defining verification strategies, defining acceptance criteria, selection of appropriate test methods, execution of verification tests, and reviewing results.
Supplier
Source: GAMP 5
An organization or individual internal or external to the user associated with the supply and/or support of products or services at any phase throughout a systems life cycle.
System Owner
Source: GAMP 5
The system owner is responsible for the availability, and support and maintenance, of a system, and for the security of the data residing on that system. The system owner is responsible for ensuring that the computerized system is supported and maintained in accordance with applicable company SOPs. The system owner also may be the process owner (e.g., for IT infrastructure systems or systems not directly supporting GxP). For systems supporting regulated processes and maintaining regulated data and records, the ownership of the data resides with the GxP-process owner, not the system owner. (cf. Process Owner)
Testing, Functional
Source: IEEE [96]
Testing that ignores the internal mechanism or structure of a system or component and focuses on the outputs generated in response to selected inputs and execution conditions. Also called black-box testing or input/output driven testing. Contrast with testing, structural.
Testing, Structural
Source: IEEE [96]
Testing that takes into account the internal mechanism [structure] of a system or component. Types include branch testing, path testing, statement testing. Also called white-box testing, glass-box testing, logic driven testing. Contrast with functional testing.
User
Source: GAMP 5
The pharmaceutical customer or user organization contracting a supplier to provide a product. In the context of this document it is not intended to apply only to individuals who use the system, and is synonymous with customer.
Verification
Source: ISO 9000 [1]; ASTM E2500 [8]
(1) Confirmation, through the provision of objective evidence that specified requirements have been fulfilled. (2) A systematic approach to verify that manufacturing systems, acting singly or in combination, are fit for intended use, have been properly installed, and are operating correctly. This is an umbrella term that encompasses all types of approaches to assuring systems are fit for use such as qualification, commissioning and qualification, verification, system validation, or other.

Commentary — Key Definitions Explained (Part 2)

Periodic Review — A Frequently Overlooked Requirement

EU GMP Annex 11 explicitly requires periodic review of computerized systems, and the GAMP 5 glossary also provides a formal definition:

  • Evaluate documentation, procedures, records, and system performance
  • Confirm the system remains in a validated state
  • Review frequency depends on the system's complexity, criticality, and frequency of change

Practical advice: establish a Periodic Review SOP, perform reviews every 1-3 years, and record the results. If you cannot produce these records during an inspection, EU inspectors will issue a deficiency.

How to Cite the Glossary During Inspections — Practical Tips

When an auditor asks you to define a term, the standard response strategy is:

  • Layer 1: Cite the official definition from GAMP 5 or its source reference (such as ICH Q9)
  • Layer 2: Explain how that definition applies to your system or process
  • Layer 3: Show where your SOPs or validation documents reflect that definition

For example, when asked "What is GxP Compliance?":
"According to the GAMP 5 definition, GxP Compliance means meeting all applicable pharmaceutical and life science regulatory requirements. For our company, the main applicable regulations include FDA 21 CFR Parts 211 and 11 and EU GMP Annex 11, as detailed in our VMP."

CDMO Application Tip — Practical Arrangement of Role Responsibilities

Typical CDMO computerized system responsibility matrix:

  • MES / ERP：System Owner = IT Director；Process Owner = VP Operations
  • LIMS：System Owner = IT；Process Owner = QC Manager
  • EDMS (Document Management System): System Owner = IT; Process Owner = QA Manager
  • Environmental Monitoring System (EMS): System Owner = Engineering; Process Owner = QA Manager
  • DCS / PLC：System Owner = Engineering；Process Owner = Manufacturing Manager

Establishing this matrix and recording it in the VMP or system inventory is a basic expectation in EU Annex 11 inspections. The Process Owner must genuinely understand the system's functions, not merely sign in name only.

System Owner vs. Process Owner — Responsibility Boundary Checklist

CDMO practice: for a LIMS, the IT department is the System Owner and the QC head is the Process Owner. Responsibility for data integrity lies with the QC head, not IT.

Section Summary

Appendix G1 — References

  • Over 106 cited documents spanning ISO standards, ICH guidelines, FDA regulations, EU directives, ISPE/GAMP guides, PIC/S, WHO, and ASTM standards.
  • The three pillars of GAMP 5's regulatory framework: 21 CFR Part 11 (US electronic records), EU GMP Annex 11 (EU computerized systems), and ICH Q9 (quality risk management).
  • 21 CFR Part 11 and EU Annex 11 have overlapping but not identical requirements; companies supplying both markets must satisfy the stricter of the two.
  • ICH Q9 provides the scientific foundation for GAMP 5's risk-based approach and defines core risk terminology.
  • When citing GAMP 5 in validation documents, use the full citation: ISPE GAMP 5, Second Edition, 2022, www.ispe.org.

Appendix G2 — Glossary

  • 96 acronyms defined, from AI to XaaS, covering all major GxP and IT terms used in GAMP 5.
  • Key distinction: Verification (ASTM umbrella term) ≥ Validation (life cycle compliance) ≥ Qualification (IQ/OQ/PQ phases).
  • Process Owner owns data integrity; System Owner owns system availability — these are distinct roles with different accountability.
  • Periodic Review is a formal GAMP 5-defined requirement, not optional — it confirms the system remains in a validated state.
  • COTS software has fitness demonstrated by broad commercial use; custom code requires full development life cycle controls.
  • GxP Regulation extends beyond GMP to include GLP, GCP, GDP, GVP, and medical device regulations depending on the business context.

The 106 referenced documents in Appendix G1 form the regulatory foundation of the GAMP 5 body of knowledge, while the glossary in Appendix G2 ensures that all stakeholders (regulated companies, suppliers, auditors) share a consistent understanding of the same terms. For a CDMO, mastering these definitions is essential to passing FDA and EU inspections and a prerequisite for using technical terminology correctly in validation documents.
